# RFC 9000 (QUIC transport) - normative facts for client conformance

_Generated from the raw RFC text fetched from rfc-editor.org via FABLE extraction + double independent Opus verification (default-refute). Every quote is verbatim from the fetched RFC 9000 text and confirmed against the source by two verifiers. Rows flagged VERIFY had a verifier disagreement -- correction inline, needs a manual source check._

### 9000 section 1-2 - Overview, Terms and Definitions, Notational Conventions, and Streams (types/identifiers, sending/receiving, prioritization, operations)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `1-2-1` | informative | QUIC version 1 conforms to the version-independent protocol invariants defined in QUIC-INVARIANTS (RFC 8999). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This document defines version 1 of QUIC, which conforms to the version-independent properties of QUIC defined in [QUIC-INVARIANTS]. | quic/packet.go: long-header invariant layout (fixed first-byte bits, version field, DCID/SCID length prefixes) must match RFC 8999 invariants. |
| `1-2-2` | informative | QUIC packets are carried inside UDP datagrams; one or more QUIC packets can be encapsulated in a single UDP datagram. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | QUIC packets are carried in UDP datagrams [UDP] to better facilitate deployment in existing systems and networks. | quic/packet_write.go + conn_recv.go: coalescing writer and receive loop must handle multiple QUIC packets per UDP datagram. |
| `1-2-3` | informative | QUIC authenticates the entirety of each packet and encrypts as much of each packet as practical. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | QUIC authenticates the entirety of each packet and encrypts as much of each packet as is practical. | quic/conn_seal.go + crypto*.go: AEAD covers full packet with header as AAD; header protection applied per RFC 9001. |
| `1-2-4` | informative | Only clients are able to migrate a connection to a new network path in QUIC version 1. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Only clients are able to migrate in this version of QUIC. | quic/conn.go: this client deliberately does NOT implement migration; confirm it never initiates path change and disables active_migration via transport_params.go. |
| `1-2-5` | informative | 0-RTT (client sending data immediately) requires some form of prior communication or configuration to enable. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | This includes an option for clients to send data immediately (0-RTT), which requires some form of prior communication or configuration to enable. | quic/handshake.go: 0-RTT is a declared non-goal; confirm no 0-RTT keys/packets are ever produced. |
| `1-2-6` | informative | An ack-eliciting packet is one containing any frames other than ACK, PADDING, and CONNECTION_CLOSE; such packets cause the recipient to send an acknowledgment. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A QUIC packet that contains frames other than ACK, PADDING, and CONNECTION_CLOSE. These cause a recipient to send an acknowledgment; see Section 13.2.1. | quic/ack_tracker.go + loss.go: verify ack-eliciting classification excludes exactly ACK, PADDING, CONNECTION_CLOSE on both send (in-flight tracking) and receive (ack scheduling) paths. |
| `1-2-7` | informative | A connection ID is selected by each endpoint for its peer to include in packets sent toward that endpoint, and its value is opaque to the peer. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Each endpoint selects one or more connection IDs for its peer to include in packets sent towards the endpoint. This value is opaque to the peer. | quic/connid.go: client must treat server-issued CIDs as opaque bytes (no structure parsing) and use them verbatim as DCID on outgoing packets. |
| `1-2-8` | informative | The client is the endpoint that initiates a QUIC connection; the server is the endpoint that accepts it; these are the only two endpoint types. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Client: The endpoint that initiates a QUIC connection. | quic/conn.go: role is fixed to client (isClient=true everywhere role matters: stream ID parity, Initial keys, header protection direction). |
| `1-2-9` | informative | All QUIC wire values use network byte order (big endian), with fields placed starting from the high-order bits of each byte. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This document uses network byte order (that is, big endian) values. Fields are placed starting from the high-order bits of each byte. | internal/bytesx + quic/packet.go + frame.go: all multi-byte encode/decode must be big-endian, bit fields packed MSB-first. |
| `1-2-10` | informative | Fields notated 'x (i)' hold integers using the variable-length encoding of Section 16; fields notated 'x (A..B)' always end on a byte boundary. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | x (i): Indicates that x holds an integer value using the variable-length encoding described in Section 16 | internal/bytesx varint codec: all (i)-notated fields (stream IDs, offsets, lengths, error codes) must round-trip through the QUIC varint codec. |
| `1-2-11` | informative | A single STREAM frame can open a stream, carry data for it, and close it; streams are created by sending data. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a single STREAM frame (Section 19.8) can open, carry data for, and close a stream. | quic/stream.go: receiving a STREAM frame with FIN on an unseen server-initiated stream must open+deliver+end it in one step. |
| `1-2-12` | informative | QUIC provides no ordering guarantee between bytes on different streams. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | QUIC does not provide any means of ensuring ordering between bytes on different streams. | quic/stream.go + http3/: consumers must not assume cross-stream delivery order (e.g. control-stream SETTINGS vs. request stream data). |
| `1-2-13` | informative | Concurrent stream count and data volume are arbitrary but subject to flow control constraints and stream limits. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | subject to flow control constraints and stream limits; see Section 4. | quic/recvflow.go + stream.go: both MAX_DATA/MAX_STREAM_DATA credit and MAX_STREAMS limits must gate stream creation and data send. |
| `1-2-14` | informative | A stream ID is a 62-bit integer (0 to 2^62-1) unique for all streams on a connection, encoded as a variable-length integer. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A stream ID is a 62-bit integer (0 to 2^62-1) that is unique for all streams on a connection. | quic/stream.go + internal/bytesx: stream IDs stored as uint64 capped at 2^62-1, encoded/decoded via varint. |
| `1-2-15` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must never reuse a stream ID within a connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A QUIC endpoint MUST NOT reuse a stream ID within a connection. | quic/stream.go / conn.go: client-side stream allocator must be strictly monotonically increasing per type; never recycle an ID even after stream close. |
| `1-2-16` | informative | The least significant bit (0x01) of the stream ID identifies the initiator: client-initiated streams are even (bit 0), server-initiated streams are odd (bit 1). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Client-initiated streams have even-numbered stream IDs (with the bit set to 0), and server-initiated streams have odd-numbered stream IDs (with the bit set to 1). | quic/stream.go: client must allocate only even IDs; incoming odd IDs classified as server-initiated. Check bit test uses 0x01. |
| `1-2-17` | informative | The second least significant bit (0x02) of the stream ID distinguishes bidirectional (bit 0) from unidirectional (bit 1) streams. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The second least significant bit (0x02) of the stream ID distinguishes between bidirectional streams (with the bit set to 0) and unidirectional streams (with the bit set to 1). | quic/stream.go: directionality derived from ID&0x02; HTTP/3 request streams must be 0x00 type, client uni control/QPACK streams 0x02, server uni 0x03. |
| `1-2-18` | informative | The four stream types are: 0x00 client-initiated bidirectional, 0x01 server-initiated bidirectional, 0x02 client-initiated unidirectional, 0x03 server-initiated unidirectional. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x00 \| Client-Initiated, Bidirectional \| | quic/stream.go: verify the two-bit type table (0x00-0x03) matches; http3/ maps request streams to type 0x00 and its uni streams to 0x02/0x03. |
| `1-2-19` | informative | The stream space for each type begins at the minimum value 0x00 through 0x03 respectively, and successive streams of each type get numerically increasing stream IDs. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The stream space for each type begins at the minimum value (0x00 through 0x03, respectively); successive streams of each type are created with numerically increasing stream IDs. | quic/conn.go stream allocator: first client bidi stream must be ID 0, first client uni stream ID 2, incrementing by 4 within each type. |
| `1-2-20` | informative | Using a stream ID out of order implicitly opens all lower-numbered streams of that same type. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A stream ID that is used out of order results in all streams of that type with lower-numbered stream IDs also being opened. | quic/stream.go / conn_recv.go: receiving a frame for server stream N must implicitly open all lower server streams of that type (they count against stream limits and can later receive frames without being 'new'). |
| `1-2-21` | informative | An endpoint uses the Stream ID and Offset fields in STREAM frames to place received data in order. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint uses the Stream ID and Offset fields in STREAM frames to place data in order. | quic/stream.go receive path: reassembly keyed on (streamID, offset), not arrival order. |
| `1-2-22` | MUST | Endpoints must be able to deliver stream data to the application as an ordered byte stream, which requires buffering out-of-order data up to the advertised flow control limit. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints MUST be able to deliver stream data to an application as an ordered byte stream. | quic/stream.go + recvflow.go: out-of-order reassembly buffer must accept gaps up to the stream's advertised MAX_STREAM_DATA; buffer bound = advertised limit (peer-input policy: bound by construction). |
| `1-2-23` | MAY | Implementations may optionally offer out-of-order delivery of stream data to the receiving application. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | implementations MAY choose to offer the ability to deliver data out of order to a receiving application. | quic/stream.go: not offered — in-order Read only; fine, this is optional. |
| `1-2-24` | informative | Data received again at the same stream offset can be discarded by the receiver. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Data that has already been received can be discarded. | quic/stream.go receive reassembly: duplicate/overlapping ranges already delivered or buffered must be dropped without re-charging flow control. |
| `1-2-25` | MUST NOT | A sender must not change the data at a given offset within a stream when it is transmitted multiple times. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The data at a given offset MUST NOT change if it is sent multiple times | quic/stream.go + loss.go retransmission: retransmitted STREAM data must come from the original retained send buffer at the same offsets — never re-slice mutated application buffers. |
| `1-2-26` | MAY | An endpoint may treat receipt of different data at the same offset within a stream as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint MAY treat receipt of different data at the same offset within a stream as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/stream.go + errors.go: optional check — if overlap comparison is implemented, mismatch should raise connection close with PROTOCOL_VIOLATION (0x0a); if not implemented, that is conformant too. |
| `1-2-27` | informative | STREAM frame boundaries are not preserved across transmission, retransmission, or delivery; streams carry no structure beyond an ordered byte stream. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | STREAM frame boundaries are not expected to be preserved when data is transmitted, retransmitted after packet loss, or delivered to the application at a receiver. | quic/stream.go + loss.go: retransmission may re-chunk stream data at different frame boundaries; http3/ must not rely on QUIC frame boundaries for HTTP/3 frame parsing. |
| `1-2-28` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not send data on any stream beyond the flow control limits set by its peer. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT send data on any stream without ensuring that it is within the flow control limits set by its peer. | quic/stream.go send path: writes must block/queue until both per-stream (MAX_STREAM_DATA) and connection (MAX_DATA) send credit cover the bytes; check the credit gate covers retransmissions too (they don't consume new credit but new data does). |
| `1-2-29` | SHOULD | A QUIC implementation should provide a way for the application to indicate the relative priority of streams, and use that to allocate resources. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A QUIC implementation SHOULD provide ways in which an application can indicate the relative priority of streams. | quic/conn.go send scheduler: check whether any stream priority hook exists; for a load-gen client, round-robin scheduling with no API is a documented SHOULD-level gap, not a MUST failure. |
| `1-2-30` | informative | The stream-write operation lets the application know when stream flow control credit has been reserved for the written data. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | write data, understanding when stream flow control credit (Section 4.1) has successfully been reserved to send the written data | quic/stream.go: Write should return/complete only once credit is reserved (blocking write semantics satisfy this); verify http3 request-body path surfaces flow-control backpressure. |
| `1-2-31` | informative | Ending a stream cleanly results in a STREAM frame with the FIN bit set. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | end the stream (clean termination), resulting in a STREAM frame (Section 19.8) with the FIN bit set | quic/stream.go + frame_write.go: Close/CloseWrite on the send side must emit STREAM with FIN (possibly zero-length) rather than RESET_STREAM. |
| `1-2-32` | informative | Resetting a stream (abrupt termination) results in a RESET_STREAM frame if the stream is not already in a terminal state. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | reset the stream (abrupt termination), resulting in a RESET_STREAM frame (Section 19.4) if the stream was not already in a terminal state | quic/stream.go: reset operation must be a no-op (no RESET_STREAM emitted) when send side already terminal (Data Recvd or Reset Sent per Section 3 states). |
| `1-2-33` | informative | Aborting reading of a stream and requesting closure may result in a STOP_SENDING frame. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | abort reading of the stream and request closure, possibly resulting in a STOP_SENDING frame (Section 19.5). | quic/stream.go: CancelRead-style operation should emit STOP_SENDING when the receive side is not already terminal; http3 uses this when abandoning a response body. |
| `1-2-34` | informative | The implementation should let the application be informed of stream state changes: peer opened/reset a stream, peer aborted reading, new data available, and writability changes due to flow control. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An application protocol can also request to be informed of state changes on streams, including when the peer has opened or reset a stream, when a peer aborts reading on a stream, when new data is available, and when data can or cannot be written to the stream due to flow control. | quic/stream.go API surface: verify RESET_STREAM and STOP_SENDING from the peer are surfaced to http3 (error on Read/Write), and readable/writable events exist in some form. |
| `1-2-35` | informative | An implementation designed for a specific application protocol may provide only the stream operations that protocol uses. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An implementation designed for use with a specific application protocol might provide only those operations that are used by that protocol. | quic/stream.go: license to keep API minimal for HTTP/3 — omitting unused operations (e.g. out-of-order read) is conformant. |

### 9000 section 3 - Stream States (Sending Stream States, Receiving Stream States, Permitted Frame Types, Bidirectional Stream States, Solicited State Transitions)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `3-1` | informative | The stream state machines are largely informative; an implementation may use a different internal state machine provided its observable behavior matches one that implements the described states. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An implementation can define a different state machine as long as its behavior is consistent with an implementation that implements these states. | quic/stream.go: internal stream state representation need not mirror RFC states, but observable frame emission/acceptance must match; audit against facts below. |
| `3-2` | informative | Unidirectional streams use only the sending or only the receiving state machine (per stream type and role); bidirectional streams use both state machines at both endpoints. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Unidirectional streams use either the sending or receiving state machine, depending on the stream type and endpoint role. | quic/stream.go: verify unidirectional streams (client send-only types 0x2, peer 0x3) only instantiate the relevant half; bidi streams track both halves independently. |
| `3-3` | informative | For a bidirectional stream, opening either the send or the receive side opens the stream in both directions. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the opening of either the send or receive side causes the stream to open in both directions. | quic/stream.go: creating/receiving either half of a bidi stream must open both halves (send half Ready, recv half Recv) atomically. |
| `3-4` | informative | Stream ID type encoding: the sending part of self-initiated streams is types 0 and 2 for clients (1 and 3 for servers) and is opened by the application, starting in Ready. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The sending part of a stream that the endpoint initiates (types 0 and 2 for clients, 1 and 3 for servers) is opened by the application. | quic/stream.go: client-initiated stream IDs must have low bits 0b00 (bidi) / 0b10 (uni); verify ID allocation sets type bits correctly. |
| `3-5` | informative | Sending the first STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame transitions the sending part from Ready to Send. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Sending the first STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame causes a sending part of a stream to enter the "Send" state. | quic/stream.go: send-side state transition on first STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED emission. |
| `3-6` | informative | An implementation may defer allocating a stream ID until it sends the first STREAM frame, allowing better prioritization. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | An implementation might choose to defer allocating a stream ID to a stream until it sends the first STREAM frame | quic/stream.go: optional; if IDs are allocated eagerly that is also conformant — check current allocation point is consistent with §2.1 ordered creation. |
| `3-7` | informative | The sending part of a peer-initiated bidirectional stream (type 1 for a client) starts in Ready when the receiving part is created. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The sending part of a bidirectional stream initiated by a peer (type 0 for a server, type 1 for a client) starts in the "Ready" state | quic/stream.go + conn_recv.go: when a server-initiated bidi stream (type 0x1) is created by inbound frame, its send half must start in Ready so the client can respond. |
| `3-8` | informative | In the Send state an endpoint transmits stream data in STREAM frames and retransmits lost stream data as necessary. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint transmits -- and retransmits as necessary -- stream data in STREAM frames | quic/loss.go + stream.go: lost STREAM frame data must be retransmitted while the send half is in Send/Data Sent. |
| `3-9` | informative | In the Send state the endpoint respects peer stream flow control limits and continues to accept and process MAX_STREAM_DATA frames. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint respects the flow control limits set by its peer and continues to accept and process MAX_STREAM_DATA frames. | quic/stream.go + recvflow.go: send path must gate on peer's MAX_STREAM_DATA and apply increases received mid-stream. |
| `3-10` | informative | An endpoint in the Send state generates STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frames when blocked from sending by stream flow control limits. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | generates STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frames if it is blocked from sending by stream flow control limits | quic/stream.go + frame_write.go: verify STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED is emitted when the send half hits the peer stream limit. |
| `3-11` | informative | After all data is submitted and a STREAM frame with FIN is sent, the sending part enters Data Sent, where it only retransmits data as necessary. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | a STREAM frame containing the FIN bit is sent, the sending part of the stream enters the "Data Sent" state. | quic/stream.go: send half transitions on FIN emission; only retransmissions allowed afterwards (no new offsets beyond final size). |
| `3-12` | informative | In the Data Sent state the endpoint need not check flow control limits or send STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frames for that stream. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint does not need to check flow control limits or send STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frames for a stream in this state. | quic/stream.go: retransmissions in Data Sent may bypass stream flow control checks (data was already within limits). |
| `3-13` | informative | In the Data Sent state an endpoint can safely ignore MAX_STREAM_DATA frames received for that stream. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint can safely ignore any MAX_STREAM_DATA frames it receives from its peer for a stream in this state. | quic/conn_recv.go: MAX_STREAM_DATA for a stream in Data Sent must not be treated as an error — ignore, do not raise STREAM_STATE_ERROR. |
| `3-14` | informative | Once all stream data is acknowledged, the sending part enters Data Recvd, a terminal state. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Once all stream data has been successfully acknowledged, the sending part of the stream enters the "Data Recvd" state, which is a terminal state. | quic/stream.go + loss.go: terminal transition requires full ACK of all stream data including FIN; stream send-side resources releasable then. |
| `3-15` | informative | From Ready, Send, or Data Sent, application abandonment or receipt of a STOP_SENDING frame causes the endpoint to send RESET_STREAM, entering Reset Sent. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | the endpoint sends a RESET_STREAM frame, which causes the stream to enter the "Reset Sent" state. | quic/stream.go: abort-write API and STOP_SENDING handling must both emit RESET_STREAM and move send half to Reset Sent. |
| `3-16` | MAY | An endpoint MAY send RESET_STREAM as the very first frame that mentions a stream, opening the sending part and immediately transitioning it to Reset Sent. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY send a RESET_STREAM as the first frame that mentions a stream | quic/stream.go: allow reset of a stream that never sent STREAM data; also the recv path must accept RESET_STREAM as the opening frame of a peer stream. |
| `3-17` | informative | Once a packet containing the RESET_STREAM is acknowledged, the sending part enters Reset Recvd, a terminal state. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Once a packet containing a RESET_STREAM has been acknowledged, the sending part of the stream enters the "Reset Recvd" state, which is a terminal state. | quic/loss.go + stream.go: RESET_STREAM must be retransmitted until acknowledged; ACK of it terminates the send half. |
| `3-18` | informative | The receiving part of a peer-initiated stream (types 1 and 3 for a client) is created when the first STREAM, STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED, or RESET_STREAM frame for it arrives. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | (types 1 and 3 for a client, or 0 and 2 for a server) is created when the first STREAM, STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED, or RESET_STREAM frame is received | quic/conn_recv.go: any of STREAM / STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED / RESET_STREAM must implicitly create the recv half of server-initiated streams (0x1, 0x3). |
| `3-19` | informative | For peer-initiated bidirectional streams, receipt of a MAX_STREAM_DATA or STOP_SENDING frame (targeting the sending part) also creates the receiving part. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | receipt of a MAX_STREAM_DATA or STOP_SENDING frame for the sending part of the stream also creates the receiving part | quic/conn_recv.go: MAX_STREAM_DATA or STOP_SENDING for an unopened server-initiated bidi stream must open it, not error. |
| `3-20` | informative | The initial state for the receiving part of a stream is Recv. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The initial state for the receiving part of a stream is "Recv". | quic/stream.go: newly created recv half starts in Recv. |
| `3-21` | informative | For a self-initiated bidirectional stream (type 0 for a client), the receiving part enters Recv when the sending part enters Ready. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the receiving part enters the "Recv" state when the sending part initiated by the endpoint (type 0 for a client, type 1 for a server) enters the "Ready" state | quic/stream.go: opening a client bidi stream (0x0) must create its recv half immediately, ready to accept response data. |
| `3-22` | informative | MAX_STREAM_DATA or STOP_SENDING may arrive before any STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame for a stream due to packet loss or reordering, and must be handled as stream-opening. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Either frame might arrive before a STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame if packets are lost or reordered. | **VERIFY** quic/conn_recv.go: reordered MAX_STREAM_DATA/STOP_SENDING for not-yet-seen peer streams must not be rejected as unknown-stream errors. -- The quote is verbatim (lines 830-831) and the level is right, but the requirement text drops the scope the source paragraph carries. Lines 824-831 are entirely about BIDIRECTIONAL streams: 'An endpoint opens a bidirectional stream when a MAX_STREAM_DATA or STOP_SENDING frame is received from the peer for that stream. ... Either frame might arrive before a STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame if packets are lost or reordered.' As written ('MAX_STREAM_DATA or STOP_SENDING ... must be handled as stream-opening', reconcile: 'reordered MAX_STREAM_DATA/STOP_SENDING for not-yet-seen peer streams must not be rejected'), the fact generalises to unidirectional streams, where the opposite is required: per §19.10 MAX_STREAM_DATA on a receive-only stream, and per §19.5 STOP_SENDING for a receive-only stream, MUST be treated as STREAM_STATE_ERROR. Fix: restate as 'For a peer-initiated BIDIRECTIONAL stream, a MAX_STREAM_DATA or STOP_SENDING frame may arrive before any STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame due to loss or reordering and opens the stream (cf. 3-19, which is correctly scoped)', and amend the reconcile note to exclude unidirectional stream IDs (client: 0x3), which must still raise STREAM_STATE_ERROR. |
| `3-23` | MUST | Before a stream is created, all streams of the same type with lower-numbered stream IDs MUST be created, so creation order is consistent at both endpoints. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Before a stream is created, all streams of the same type with lower-numbered stream IDs MUST be created. | quic/stream.go + conn_recv.go: a frame for server-initiated stream N must implicitly create all lower-numbered same-type streams (matters for stream-limit accounting); client must open its own streams in ID order. |
| `3-24` | informative | In the Recv state, incoming STREAM data is buffered and can be reassembled into correct order for delivery to the application. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Incoming data is buffered and can be reassembled into the correct order for delivery to the application. | quic/stream.go: recv buffer must reassemble out-of-order STREAM frame offsets before delivery. |
| `3-25` | informative | As the application consumes data and buffer space frees, the endpoint sends MAX_STREAM_DATA frames to grant the peer more send credit. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | As data is consumed by the application and buffer space becomes available, the endpoint sends MAX_STREAM_DATA frames to allow the peer to send more data. | quic/recvflow.go: MAX_STREAM_DATA issuance tied to application consumption of the recv buffer. |
| `3-26` | informative | Receiving a STREAM frame with the FIN bit makes the final size of the stream known and moves the receiving part to Size Known. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | When a STREAM frame with a FIN bit is received, the final size of the stream is known | quic/stream.go: FIN receipt records final size (see §4.5 FINAL_SIZE_ERROR checks) and transitions recv half to Size Known. |
| `3-27` | informative | In the Size Known state the endpoint no longer needs to send MAX_STREAM_DATA frames; it only receives retransmissions of stream data. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | In this state, the endpoint no longer needs to send MAX_STREAM_DATA frames; it only receives any retransmissions of stream data. | quic/recvflow.go: suppress further MAX_STREAM_DATA once final size is known (connection-level MAX_DATA still applies separately). |
| `3-28` | informative | Once all data for the stream has been received, the receiving part enters Data Recvd — possibly from the same STREAM frame that carried FIN. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Once all data for the stream has been received, the receiving part enters the "Data Recvd" state. | quic/stream.go: recv half transitions to Data Recvd when the contiguous byte range [0, final size) is complete. |
| `3-29` | informative | After all stream data has been received, any further STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frames for that stream can be discarded. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | After all data has been received, any STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frames for the stream can be discarded. | quic/conn_recv.go: duplicate/late STREAM frames post-completion must be silently dropped (after §4.5 final-size validation), not treated as state errors. |
| `3-30` | informative | The Data Recvd state persists until stream data is delivered to the application; delivery moves the stream to Data Read, a terminal state. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Once stream data has been delivered, the stream enters the "Data Read" state, which is a terminal state. | quic/stream.go: recv half terminal only after the application has read all data; resources releasable then. |
| `3-31` | informative | Receiving RESET_STREAM while in Recv or Size Known moves the receiving part to Reset Recvd, possibly interrupting data delivery to the application. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receiving a RESET_STREAM frame in the "Recv" or "Size Known" state causes the stream to enter the "Reset Recvd" state. | quic/stream.go: RESET_STREAM handling in active recv states surfaces the reset to the reader. |
| `3-32` | informative | RESET_STREAM may arrive after all data was received (Data Recvd), and stream data may arrive after RESET_STREAM (Reset Recvd); an implementation may manage either ordering as it chooses. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An implementation is free to manage this situation as it chooses. | quic/stream.go + conn_recv.go: both orderings (reset-after-data, data-after-reset) must be tolerated without error. |
| `3-33` | MAY | On receiving RESET_STREAM, an implementation MAY interrupt delivery of stream data, discard unconsumed data, and signal the reset to the application. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An implementation MAY interrupt delivery of stream data, discard any data that was not consumed, and signal the receipt of the RESET_STREAM. | quic/stream.go: reset delivery semantics — check whether buffered-but-unread data is discarded and the reset error surfaces to http3 layer. |
| `3-34` | informative | A RESET_STREAM signal may be suppressed if all stream data was already completely received and buffered; in that case the receiving part remains in Data Recvd. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | If the RESET_STREAM is suppressed, the receiving part of the stream remains in "Data Recvd". | quic/stream.go: optional optimization — a reset arriving after full data receipt may be hidden from the reader; either behavior conformant. |
| `3-35` | informative | Once the application receives the reset signal, the receiving part transitions to Reset Read, a terminal state. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | the receiving part of the stream transitions to the "Reset Read" state, which is a terminal state. | quic/stream.go: terminal transition after application observes the reset. |
| `3-36` | informative | Only three frame types sent by the stream's sender affect stream state at either end: STREAM, STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED, and RESET_STREAM. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The sender of a stream sends just three frame types that affect the state of a stream at either the sender or the receiver | quic/frame_write.go + stream.go: no other frame from the send half should mutate stream state. |
| `3-37` | MUST NOT | A sender MUST NOT send STREAM, STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED, or RESET_STREAM frames for a stream from a terminal state (Data Recvd or Reset Recvd). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT send any of these frames from a terminal state ("Data Recvd" or "Reset Recvd"). | quic/stream.go + loss.go: block emission AND retransmission of these frame types once the send half is terminal. |
| `3-38` | MUST NOT | A sender MUST NOT send STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED for a stream in Reset Sent or any terminal state — i.e., after it has sent RESET_STREAM. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT send a STREAM or STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame for a stream in the "Reset Sent" state or any terminal state | quic/stream.go + loss.go: after sending RESET_STREAM, suppress pending/queued STREAM data and its retransmissions; only RESET_STREAM itself may be retransmitted. |
| `3-39` | informative | A receiver may legitimately receive STREAM, STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED, or RESET_STREAM in any state due to delayed packet delivery and must tolerate this. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver could receive any of these three frames in any state, due to the possibility of delayed delivery of packets carrying them. | quic/conn_recv.go: late STREAM/STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED/RESET_STREAM in any recv state must not raise a connection error (final-size checks aside). |
| `3-40` | informative | The receiver of a stream sends only MAX_STREAM_DATA and STOP_SENDING frames for that stream. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The receiver of a stream sends MAX_STREAM_DATA frames (Section 19.10) and STOP_SENDING frames (Section 19.5). | quic/recvflow.go + stream.go: recv half emits only these two frame types toward the peer's send half. |
| `3-41` | informative | The receiver only sends MAX_STREAM_DATA frames while in the Recv state. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The receiver only sends MAX_STREAM_DATA frames in the "Recv" state. | quic/recvflow.go: no MAX_STREAM_DATA after Size Known / Data Recvd / reset states. |
| `3-42` | MAY | A receiver MAY send STOP_SENDING in any state where it has not yet received RESET_STREAM (any state other than Reset Recvd or Reset Read), though it has little value in Data Recvd. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver MAY send a STOP_SENDING frame in any state where it has not received a RESET_STREAM frame | quic/stream.go: gate STOP_SENDING emission on not-yet-received RESET_STREAM. |
| `3-43` | informative | A sender may receive MAX_STREAM_DATA or STOP_SENDING in any state due to delayed delivery and must tolerate this without error. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender could receive either of these two types of frames in any state as a result of delayed delivery of packets. | quic/conn_recv.go: MAX_STREAM_DATA/STOP_SENDING arriving for terminal or reset send-halves must be ignored, not errored. |
| `3-44` | informative | A bidirectional stream can be modeled as open when either part is non-terminal and closed when both sending and receiving parts are terminal. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | presents the stream as "open" when either sending or receiving parts are in a non-terminal state and "closed" when both sending and receiving streams are in terminal states | quic/stream.go: stream teardown/GC must require BOTH halves terminal before releasing the stream (cf. conn-recycle-teardown-race memory). |
| `3-45` | informative | Table 2's optional HTTP/2-style composite mapping requires data be acknowledged before transitioning to closed or half-closed composite states; it is one possible mapping, not required. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | this mapping requires that data be acknowledged before the transition to a "closed" or "half-closed" state | quic/stream.go: informative only; no code obligation, but useful when mapping http3 request lifecycle onto QUIC stream halves. |
| `3-46` | informative | A stream is considered idle if it has not yet been created, or if its receiving part is in Recv without having received any frames. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A stream is considered "idle" if it has not yet been created or if the receiving part of the stream is in the "Recv" state | quic/stream.go: definition only — relevant to lazily materializing implicitly created (3-23) streams without allocating full state. |
| `3-47` | SHOULD | When the application aborts reading a stream in the Recv or Size Known state, the transport SHOULD send a STOP_SENDING frame to prompt closure of the opposite direction. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | the transport SHOULD signal this by sending a STOP_SENDING frame to prompt closure of the stream in the opposite direction | quic/stream.go: verify the abort-read/CancelRead API emits STOP_SENDING with the application error code when recv half is in Recv or Size Known (http3 request cancellation path). |
| `3-48` | informative | STREAM frames received after sending STOP_SENDING still count toward connection and stream flow control, even though the data can be discarded on receipt. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | STREAM frames received after sending a STOP_SENDING frame are still counted toward connection and stream flow control, even though these frames can be discarded upon receipt. | quic/recvflow.go: flow-control accounting must charge post-STOP_SENDING STREAM data at both stream and connection level even when payload is dropped. |
| `3-49` | MUST | An endpoint that receives STOP_SENDING MUST send a RESET_STREAM frame if the stream is in the Ready or Send state. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that receives a STOP_SENDING frame MUST send a RESET_STREAM frame if the stream is in the "Ready" or "Send" state. | quic/stream.go: inbound STOP_SENDING handler must emit RESET_STREAM when send half is Ready/Send (e.g., server cancels a request body upload). |
| `3-50` | MAY | If the stream is in Data Sent when STOP_SENDING arrives, the endpoint MAY defer sending RESET_STREAM until outstanding data is acknowledged or declared lost. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | If the stream is in the "Data Sent" state, the endpoint MAY defer sending the RESET_STREAM frame until the packets containing outstanding data are acknowledged or declared lost. | quic/stream.go: STOP_SENDING in Data Sent — immediate RESET_STREAM is also conformant; check which path is taken. |
| `3-51` | SHOULD | If any outstanding data is declared lost after STOP_SENDING was received, the endpoint SHOULD send RESET_STREAM instead of retransmitting the data. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | If any outstanding data is declared lost, the endpoint SHOULD send a RESET_STREAM frame instead of retransmitting the data. | quic/loss.go + stream.go: loss-triggered retransmission should check for received STOP_SENDING and convert to RESET_STREAM rather than resending stream data. |
| `3-52` | SHOULD | An endpoint SHOULD copy the error code from the received STOP_SENDING frame into the RESET_STREAM it sends, though any application error code is permitted. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD copy the error code from the STOP_SENDING frame to the RESET_STREAM frame it sends, but it can use any application error code. | quic/stream.go: RESET_STREAM emitted in response to STOP_SENDING should carry the STOP_SENDING's application error code. |
| `3-53` | MAY | An endpoint that sent STOP_SENDING MAY ignore the error code carried in any RESET_STREAM frames subsequently received for that stream. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that sends a STOP_SENDING frame MAY ignore the error code in any RESET_STREAM frames subsequently received for that stream. | quic/stream.go: after a local CancelRead, the peer's RESET_STREAM error code need not be surfaced to the application. |
| `3-54` | SHOULD | STOP_SENDING SHOULD only be sent for a stream that has not already been reset by the peer; it is most useful in Recv or Size Known. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | STOP_SENDING SHOULD only be sent for a stream that has not been reset by the peer. | quic/stream.go: suppress STOP_SENDING emission once RESET_STREAM has been received for the stream. |
| `3-55` | informative | An endpoint is expected to retransmit STOP_SENDING (send another) if the packet carrying a previous STOP_SENDING is lost. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint is expected to send another STOP_SENDING frame if a packet containing a previous STOP_SENDING is lost. | quic/loss.go: STOP_SENDING must be in the retransmittable-frame set and regenerated on loss. |
| `3-56` | informative | Once all stream data or a RESET_STREAM has been received — the stream is no longer in Recv or Size Known — sending (or retransmitting) STOP_SENDING is unnecessary. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the stream is in any state other than "Recv" or "Size Known" -- sending a STOP_SENDING frame is unnecessary | quic/loss.go + stream.go: on loss of STOP_SENDING, skip regeneration if the recv half already left Recv/Size Known. |
| `3-57` | informative | To terminate both directions of a bidirectional stream, an endpoint can send RESET_STREAM for its own direction and STOP_SENDING to encourage prompt termination of the opposite direction. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that wishes to terminate both directions of a bidirectional stream can terminate one direction by sending a RESET_STREAM frame | quic/stream.go: full request-abort path (http3 cancellation) should emit both RESET_STREAM and STOP_SENDING on the request stream. |

### 9000 section 4 - Flow Control

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `4-1` | SHOULD | Implementations should expose an interface letting the TLS/crypto layer communicate its buffering limits, since CRYPTO data is not flow controlled like stream data. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | QUIC implementations SHOULD provide an interface for the cryptographic protocol implementation to communicate its buffering limits. | quic/handshake.go + conn_crypto.go: check whether CRYPTO receive buffering is bounded and whether the TLS layer can signal a buffer limit. |
| `4-2` | informative | Data in CRYPTO frames is not subject to stream/connection flow control; the crypto layer bounds its own buffering. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Data sent in CRYPTO frames is not flow controlled in the same way as stream data. | quic/recvflow.go + conn_recv.go: verify CRYPTO bytes are excluded from MAX_DATA/MAX_STREAM_DATA accounting and bounded separately. |
| `4-3` | informative | Connection-level flow control limits only the total bytes of stream data carried in STREAM frames across all streams (two-level scheme: per-stream plus connection). <br>_(receiver,sender [CLIENT])_ | prevents senders from exceeding a receiver's buffer capacity for the connection by limiting the total bytes of stream data sent in STREAM frames on all streams | quic/recvflow.go + stream.go: connection-level accounting must count STREAM frame payload only, and both levels must be enforced. |
| `4-4` | MUST NOT | A sender must never send stream data beyond either the stream-level or the connection-level flow control limit. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Senders MUST NOT send data in excess of either limit. | quic/stream.go send path: verify writes block on BOTH per-stream send credit and connection send credit before emitting STREAM frames. |
| `4-5` | informative | Initial flow control limits for all streams and the connection come from transport parameters at handshake; larger limits arrive later via MAX_STREAM_DATA / MAX_DATA frames. <br>_(receiver,sender [CLIENT])_ | A receiver sets initial limits for all streams through transport parameters during the handshake | quic/transport_params.go: initial_max_data and initial_max_stream_data_* must seed both send-side credit (from peer TPs) and recv-side limits (from ours). |
| `4-6` | informative | MAX_STREAM_DATA carries an absolute maximum byte offset for the stream, not a relative window increment. <br>_(receiver,sender [CLIENT])_ | A MAX_STREAM_DATA frame indicates the maximum absolute byte offset of a stream. | quic/frame.go + stream.go: confirm the limit is treated as an absolute offset (unlike HTTP/2 WINDOW_UPDATE deltas). |
| `4-7` | informative | MAX_DATA carries the maximum for the sum of the absolute byte offsets consumed across all streams. <br>_(receiver,sender [CLIENT])_ | a MAX_DATA frame, which indicates the maximum of the sum of the absolute byte offsets of all streams | quic/conn.go + recvflow.go: connection send limit compared against cumulative sum of highest offsets sent per stream. |
| `4-8` | informative | A receiver keeps a cumulative sum of bytes received on all streams and uses it to detect violations of the connection and stream limits. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver maintains a cumulative sum of bytes received on all streams, which is used to check for violations of the advertised connection or stream data limits | quic/recvflow.go: verify a cumulative received-bytes counter exists and both limit checks run on every STREAM frame. |
| `4-9` | informative | Advertising a smaller flow control limit than previously advertised is not an error, but the smaller value has no effect. <br>_(receiver,sender [CLIENT])_ | it is not an error to advertise a smaller limit, but the smaller limit has no effect | quic/stream.go + conn.go: a non-increasing MAX_* must not be rejected as an error and must not shrink existing credit. |
| `4-10` | MUST | A receiver must close the connection with FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR when the peer sends data violating the advertised connection or stream data limits. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver MUST close the connection with an error of type FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR if the sender violates the advertised connection or stream data limits | quic/recvflow.go + errors.go: both stream-level and connection-level violations must raise a connection close with FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR. |
| `4-11` | MUST | A sender must ignore MAX_STREAM_DATA or MAX_DATA frames that do not increase the current flow control limit. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST ignore any MAX_STREAM_DATA or MAX_DATA frames that do not increase flow control limits. | quic/stream.go + conn.go: handling of MAX_STREAM_DATA/MAX_DATA must apply max(current, new), never regress send credit. |
| `4-12` | SHOULD | A sender blocked by flow control should send a STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED or DATA_BLOCKED frame to signal it has data pending. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD send a STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED or DATA_BLOCKED frame to indicate to the receiver that it has data to write but is blocked by flow control limits | quic/stream.go + frame_write.go: check STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED/DATA_BLOCKED are emitted when send credit is exhausted with data queued. |
| `4-13` | SHOULD | A flow-control-limited sender should periodically resend STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED or DATA_BLOCKED when no ack-eliciting packets are in flight, to keep the connection from idle-timing out. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | a sender that is flow control limited SHOULD periodically send a STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED or DATA_BLOCKED frame when it has no ack-eliciting packets in flight | quic/stream.go + pto.go/conn.go: verify a periodic blocked-frame keepalive exists while blocked, or accept idle-timeout risk knowingly. |
| `4-14` | MAY | A receiver may send MAX_STREAM_DATA or MAX_DATA multiple times per round trip, or early enough to cover loss and recovery, to avoid blocking the sender. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | a receiver MAY send a MAX_STREAM_DATA or MAX_DATA frame multiple times within a round trip or send it early enough to allow time for loss of the frame | quic/recvflow.go: credit-update cadence is implementation choice; confirm updates are sent proactively as data is consumed. |
| `4-15` | MUST NOT | A receiver must not wait for a STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED or DATA_BLOCKED frame before advertising more credit, since blocked senders are not required to send them. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | a receiver MUST NOT wait for a STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED or DATA_BLOCKED frame before sending a MAX_STREAM_DATA or MAX_DATA frame | quic/recvflow.go: MAX_* advertisement must be driven by consumption/thresholds, never gated on receipt of blocked frames. |
| `4-16` | informative | On receiving RESET_STREAM, an endpoint tears down state for that stream and ignores further data arriving on it. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint will tear down state for the matching stream and ignore further data arriving on that stream | quic/stream.go: RESET_STREAM handling must discard subsequently arriving STREAM frames for the reset receive direction without error. |
| `4-17` | MUST | RESET_STREAM terminates only one direction; both endpoints must keep flow control state for the unterminated direction of a bidirectional stream until it reaches a terminal state. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Both endpoints MUST maintain flow control state for the stream in the unterminated direction until that direction enters a terminal state. | quic/stream.go: verify a RESET_STREAM in one direction of a bidi stream does not free or corrupt the other direction's flow-control accounting. |
| `4-18` | informative | The final size of a stream is one greater than the largest byte offset sent, or zero if no bytes were sent; it equals the flow control credit consumed. <br>_(sender,receiver [CLIENT])_ | this is one higher than the offset of the byte with the largest offset sent on the stream, or zero if no bytes were sent | quic/stream.go: final-size computation for both FIN and locally-sent RESET_STREAM must match this definition. |
| `4-19` | informative | Final size is communicated reliably either as Offset+Length of the FIN-bearing STREAM frame (fields possibly implicit) or as the Final Size field of RESET_STREAM. <br>_(sender,receiver [CLIENT])_ | The final size is the sum of the Offset and Length fields of a STREAM frame with a FIN flag, noting that these fields might be implicit. | quic/frame.go + stream.go: derive final size from FIN STREAM frames (handling implicit offset/length=0) and from RESET_STREAM's Final Size field. |
| `4-20` | MUST | The receiver must use the stream's final size to account for all bytes sent on the stream in its connection-level flow controller (entering Size Known / Reset Recvd). <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The receiver MUST use the final size of the stream to account for all bytes sent on the stream in its connection-level flow controller | quic/recvflow.go + stream.go: on RESET_STREAM or FIN, connection-level FC must be charged up to final size even for bytes never actually received. |
| `4-21` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not send stream data at or beyond the final size once a final size exists for the stream. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT send data on a stream at or beyond the final size. | quic/stream.go: after sending FIN or RESET_STREAM, retransmissions must never extend past the final size; no new data at/after it. |
| `4-22` | SHOULD | A known final size cannot change; if a RESET_STREAM or STREAM frame indicates a different final size, the endpoint should respond with FINAL_SIZE_ERROR. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | If a RESET_STREAM or STREAM frame is received indicating a change in the final size for the stream, an endpoint SHOULD respond with an error of type FINAL_SIZE_ERROR | quic/stream.go + errors.go: validate RESET_STREAM Final Size and FIN offsets against any previously known final size; raise FINAL_SIZE_ERROR on mismatch. |
| `4-23` | SHOULD | A receiver should treat data received at or beyond the final size as FINAL_SIZE_ERROR, even after the stream is closed. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver SHOULD treat receipt of data at or beyond the final size as an error of type FINAL_SIZE_ERROR, even after a stream is closed | quic/stream.go: check STREAM frames extending at/past a known final size trigger FINAL_SIZE_ERROR, including on closed streams while state is retained. |
| `4-24` | informative | Generating FINAL_SIZE_ERROR is not mandatory, because it would require keeping final-size state for closed streams, a potentially significant state commitment. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Generating these errors is not mandatory, because requiring that an endpoint generate these errors also means that the endpoint needs to maintain the final size state for closed streams | quic/stream.go: it is conformant to skip final-size checks for streams whose state was already discarded; document the chosen policy. |
| `4-25` | informative | Stream concurrency is limited by cumulative count: only streams with ID less than (max_streams * 4 + first_stream_id_of_type) can be opened. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Only streams with a stream ID less than "(max_streams * 4 + first_stream_id_of_type)" can be opened | quic/conn.go + stream.go: both the client-side open gate and acceptance of server-initiated streams must use this cumulative-ID formula. |
| `4-26` | informative | Unidirectional and bidirectional streams have separate concurrency limits (initial via transport parameters, later via MAX_STREAMS frames). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Separate limits apply to unidirectional and bidirectional streams. | quic/conn.go + transport_params.go: distinct counters/limits for uni vs bidi streams in both directions; MAX_STREAMS frame carries a type bit. |
| `4-27` | MUST | A max_streams transport parameter or MAX_STREAMS frame with value greater than 2^60 must cause immediate connection close: TRANSPORT_PARAMETER_ERROR if from a transport parameter, FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR if from a frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the connection MUST be closed immediately with a connection error of type TRANSPORT_PARAMETER_ERROR if the offending value was received in a transport parameter or of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR | quic/transport_params.go: reject initial_max_streams_* > 2^60 with TRANSPORT_PARAMETER_ERROR; quic/frame.go: reject MAX_STREAMS > 2^60 with FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. |
| `4-28` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not open more streams than the cumulative limit its peer has advertised. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints MUST NOT exceed the limit set by their peer. | quic/conn.go: client stream-open path must gate on the peer's current max_streams (per type) before allocating a stream ID. |
| `4-29` | MUST | Receiving a frame with a stream ID exceeding the stream limit this endpoint advertised is a connection error of type STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that receives a frame with a stream ID exceeding the limit it has sent MUST treat this as a connection error of type STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR | quic/conn_recv.go + stream.go: server-initiated stream IDs must be checked against our advertised max_streams; errors.go must define STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR. |
| `4-30` | MUST | MAX_STREAMS frames that do not increase the current stream limit must be ignored (a smaller advertisement has no effect). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | MAX_STREAMS frames that do not increase the stream limit MUST be ignored. | quic/conn.go: MAX_STREAMS handling must apply max(current, new) per stream type and never regress the open budget. |
| `4-31` | SHOULD | An endpoint unable to open a new stream because of the peer's limit should send a STREAMS_BLOCKED frame (a debugging signal). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that is unable to open a new stream due to the peer's limits SHOULD send a STREAMS_BLOCKED frame | quic/conn.go + frame_write.go: check STREAMS_BLOCKED emission when a client stream-open is refused by the peer's max_streams. |
| `4-32` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not wait for a STREAMS_BLOCKED frame before advertising additional stream credit, since the peer may never send it. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT wait to receive this signal before advertising additional credit | quic/conn.go: MAX_STREAMS advertisement for peer-initiated streams (e.g., server uni streams for H3) must be driven by stream closure, not by STREAMS_BLOCKED receipt. |

### 9000 section 5-6 - Connections (connection IDs, packet matching, client/server handling, operations) and Version Negotiation

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `5-6-1` | informative | Connection IDs are independently selected by each endpoint; each endpoint selects the connection IDs that its peer will use to address it. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Connection IDs are independently selected by endpoints; each endpoint selects the connection IDs that its peer uses. | quic/connid.go — verify two distinct sets: CIDs the client issues (server uses as DCID) vs server-issued CIDs the client uses as DCID. |
| `5-6-2` | MUST NOT | Connection IDs must not contain information an external observer could use to correlate them with other connection IDs of the same connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Connection IDs MUST NOT contain any information that can be used by an external observer (that is, one that does not cooperate with the issuer) to correlate them | quic/connid.go — local CID generation must be crypto/rand, no embedded counter or linkable structure. |
| `5-6-3` | MUST NOT | The same connection ID value must never be issued more than once on the same connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the same connection ID MUST NOT be issued more than once on the same connection. | quic/connid.go — issuance path must dedupe against all previously issued CID values for the connection. |
| `5-6-4` | informative | Long-header packets carry both Source and Destination Connection ID fields, which set the connection IDs for new connections. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Packets with long headers include Source Connection ID and Destination Connection ID fields. These fields are used to set the connection IDs for new connections | quic/packet.go — long-header parse/emit of SCID+DCID; handshake.go adopts server SCID from first server packet (§7.2). |
| `5-6-5` | informative | Short-header packets carry only the Destination Connection ID with no explicit length; the receiving endpoint must know its own CID length out of band. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Packets with short headers (Section 17.3) only include the Destination Connection ID and omit the explicit length. | quic/packet.go — short-header parse must slice DCID using the client's known local CID length (possibly zero). |
| `5-6-6` | informative | A Version Negotiation packet echoes the connection IDs the client selected, both for routing and to prove it responds to a client packet. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | A Version Negotiation (Section 17.2.1) packet echoes the connection IDs selected by the client | quic/conn_recv.go — client should verify VN packet's echoed DCID/SCID match its chosen values before acting on it (§17.2.1). |
| `5-6-7` | informative | A zero-length connection ID may be used when a connection ID is not needed to route packets to the correct endpoint. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A zero-length connection ID can be used when a connection ID is not needed to route to the correct endpoint. | quic/connid.go — determine whether client uses zero-length source CID; several later rules hinge on this. |
| `5-6-8` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not run multiple concurrent zero-length-CID connections on the same local IP address and port (unless migration/rebinding/port-reuse are certainly absent). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT use the same IP address and port for multiple concurrent connections with zero-length connection IDs | quic/conn.go — if client SCID is zero-length, each Dial must get its own local UDP socket/port (a connected socket per conn satisfies this). |
| `5-6-9` | informative | The initial connection ID an endpoint issues is the Source Connection ID of its long headers during the handshake, and its sequence number is 0. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The sequence number of the initial connection ID is 0. | quic/connid.go — register server's handshake SCID as sequence 0 in the server-CID set (base for Retire Prior To math). |
| `5-6-10` | informative | If the preferred_address transport parameter is sent, the connection ID it supplies has sequence number 1. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | If the preferred_address transport parameter is sent, the sequence number of the supplied connection ID is 1. | quic/transport_params.go + connid.go — if preferred_address is parsed, its CID must register as seq 1 and count against active_connection_id_limit even though client never migrates. |
| `5-6-11` | MUST | The sequence number on each newly issued connection ID (via NEW_CONNECTION_ID) must increase by exactly 1. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | The sequence number on each newly issued connection ID MUST increase by 1. | quic/connid.go + frame_write.go — if client issues NEW_CONNECTION_ID, seq increments by 1; vacuous if client never issues. |
| `5-6-12` | informative | The client's first Destination Connection ID and any connection ID from a Retry packet are not assigned sequence numbers. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The connection ID that a client selects for the first Destination Connection ID field it sends and any connection ID provided by a Retry packet are not assigned sequence numbers | quic/retry.go + connid.go — original random DCID and Retry SCID must not occupy sequence slots in the active server-CID set. |
| `5-6-13` | MUST | After issuing a connection ID, an endpoint must accept packets carrying it for the connection's life or until the peer retires it via RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | it MUST accept packets that carry this connection ID for the duration of the connection or until its peer invalidates the connection ID via a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame | quic/conn_recv.go — every active locally issued CID (if any) must match as DCID until server retires it. |
| `5-6-14` | informative | Any active (issued, unretired) connection ID is valid for the connection at any time, in any packet type, including a preferred_address-supplied CID. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | any active connection ID is valid for use with the current connection at any time, in any packet type. | quic/conn_recv.go — DCID matching must not be restricted by packet type or encryption level. |
| `5-6-15` | SHOULD | An endpoint should keep its peer supplied with a sufficient number of available, unused connection IDs. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD ensure that its peer has a sufficient number of available and unused connection IDs. | quic/connid.go — client-side replenishment policy for CIDs it issues; vacuous if client uses zero-length/single CID. |
| `5-6-16` | informative | Endpoints advertise how many active connection IDs they will maintain via the active_connection_id_limit transport parameter. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints advertise the number of active connection IDs they are willing to maintain using the active_connection_id_limit transport parameter. | quic/transport_params.go — encode our active_connection_id_limit and parse the server's (default 2 per §18.2). |
| `5-6-17` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not provide more connection IDs than the peer's advertised active_connection_id_limit. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT provide more connection IDs than the peer's limit. | quic/connid.go — gate any NEW_CONNECTION_ID issuance on the server's advertised active_connection_id_limit. |
| `5-6-18` | MAY | An endpoint may temporarily exceed the peer's CID limit only if the NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame's Retire Prior To field forces retirement of the excess. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY send connection IDs that temporarily exceed a peer's limit if the NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame also requires the retirement of any excess | quic/connid.go — only relevant if client issues CIDs with Retire Prior To; likely N/A. |
| `5-6-19` | MUST | After processing a NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame (adds plus retirements), if active connection IDs exceed the endpoint's own advertised active_connection_id_limit, it must close with CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | if the number of active connection IDs exceeds the value advertised in its active_connection_id_limit transport parameter, an endpoint MUST close the connection with an error of type CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR | quic/connid.go NEW_CONNECTION_ID handler — count active server CIDs AFTER applying Retire Prior To; over-limit closes with CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR (errors.go 0x09). |
| `5-6-20` | SHOULD | An endpoint should supply a replacement connection ID when the peer retires one. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD supply a new connection ID when the peer retires a connection ID. | quic/connid.go — on RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID from server, issue a fresh NEW_CONNECTION_ID (if client issues CIDs at all). |
| `5-6-21` | MAY | If it provided fewer CIDs than the peer's limit, an endpoint may supply a new one when a packet arrives on a previously unused CID. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | it MAY supply a new connection ID when it receives a packet with a previously unused connection ID. | quic/connid.go — optional replenishment trigger; fine to omit. |
| `5-6-22` | MAY | An endpoint may cap the total number of connection IDs it issues per connection to avoid exhausting the CID space. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY limit the total number of connection IDs issued for each connection | quic/connid.go — optional issuance cap; fine to omit. |
| `5-6-23` | MAY | An endpoint may also limit CID issuance to bound per-path state, since the peer can use as many paths as there are issued CIDs. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY also limit the issuance of connection IDs to reduce the amount of per-path state it maintains | quic/connid.go — optional; client keeps one path, so trivially satisfied. |
| `5-6-24` | SHOULD | An endpoint that initiates migration and needs non-zero-length CIDs should ensure its peer's CID pool suffices for the peer to respond after migration. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | SHOULD ensure that the pool of connection IDs available to its peer allows the peer to use a new connection ID on migration | N/A — this client never initiates migration (project non-goal); vacuous. |
| `5-6-25` | informative | An endpoint that chose a zero-length connection ID at handshake cannot issue new connection IDs; a zero-length DCID is used toward it on every path. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that selects a zero-length connection ID during the handshake cannot issue a new connection ID. | quic/connid.go — if client SCID is zero-length, assert no NEW_CONNECTION_ID is ever emitted. |
| `5-6-26` | informative | An endpoint may switch which peer-supplied connection ID it uses to any other available one at any time during the connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint can change the connection ID it uses for a peer to another available one at any time during the connection. | quic/connid.go — client may pin one server CID; switching is optional without migration. |
| `5-6-27` | informative | Sending RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID declares the CID permanently unused by the sender and asks the peer to replace it via NEW_CONNECTION_ID. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | Sending a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame indicates that the connection ID will not be used again and requests that the peer replace it with a new connection ID | quic/frame_write.go — RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID codec; connid.go must mark a retired server CID unusable for sending immediately. |
| `5-6-28` | SHOULD | Endpoints should retire connection IDs whose associated local or destination address is no longer actively used. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints SHOULD retire connection IDs when they are no longer actively using either the local or destination address for which the connection ID was used. | quic/connid.go — single-path client never changes address, so vacuous in practice. |
| `5-6-29` | SHOULD | An endpoint forcing retirement via increased Retire Prior To should keep accepting the previously issued CIDs until the peer actually retires them. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint SHOULD continue to accept the previously issued connection IDs until they are retired by the peer. | quic/connid.go + conn_recv.go — if client ever raises Retire Prior To, old local CIDs must keep matching until retired; vacuous otherwise. |
| `5-6-30` | MAY | If an endpoint can no longer process connection IDs it asked the peer to retire, it may close the connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | If the endpoint can no longer process the indicated connection IDs, it MAY close the connection. | quic/close.go — optional close path; no action required. |
| `5-6-31` | MUST | On receiving an increased Retire Prior To, the peer must stop using and retire (with RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID) the indicated CIDs before adding the newly provided CID to its active set. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | the peer MUST stop using the corresponding connection IDs and retire them with RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames before adding the newly provided connection ID to the set of active connection IDs. | quic/connid.go NEW_CONNECTION_ID handler — process Retire Prior To FIRST (queue RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID per retired seq, stop sending on them), THEN add the new CID; ordering feeds the 5-6-19 limit check. |
| `5-6-32` | SHOULD | An endpoint should bound the number of locally retired CIDs whose RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames are still unacknowledged. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD limit the number of connection IDs it has retired locally for which RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames have not yet been acknowledged. | quic/connid.go + loss.go — track unacked RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames and bound the pending set. |
| `5-6-33` | SHOULD | An endpoint should be able to send and track at least 2x active_connection_id_limit pending RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD allow for sending and tracking a number of RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames of at least twice the value of the active_connection_id_limit transport parameter. | quic/connid.go — pending-retirement capacity >= 2 x our advertised active_connection_id_limit. |
| `5-6-34` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must never drop a peer-issued connection ID from its state without sending RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID for it. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT forget a connection ID without retiring it | quic/connid.go — any removal of a server CID from the active set must be paired with an emitted RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID. |
| `5-6-35` | MAY | An endpoint may treat having more CIDs pending retirement than its tracking limit as a connection error of type CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | it MAY choose to treat having connection IDs in need of retirement that exceed this limit as a connection error of type CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR. | quic/connid.go + errors.go — optional overflow close with CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR when pending retires exceed the 2x bound. |
| `5-6-36` | SHOULD NOT | Endpoints should not raise Retire Prior To again before all CIDs covered by the previous Retire Prior To value have been retired by the peer. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints SHOULD NOT issue updates of the Retire Prior To field before receiving RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames that retire all connection IDs indicated by the previous Retire Prior To value. | quic/connid.go — only if client ever sends Retire Prior To; vacuous otherwise. |
| `5-6-37` | informative | A packet with a non-zero-length Destination Connection ID matching an existing connection is processed as part of that connection; multiple CIDs can map to one connection. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | If the packet has a non-zero-length Destination Connection ID corresponding to an existing connection, QUIC processes that packet accordingly. | quic/conn_recv.go — DCID lookup against the set of all active local CIDs, not a single value. |
| `5-6-38` | informative | A zero-length-DCID packet is matched to a connection by addressing information (destination IP/port, or source plus destination addresses). <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | If the Destination Connection ID is zero length and the addressing information in the packet matches the addressing information the endpoint uses to identify a connection | quic/conn_recv.go — a connected UDP socket per connection provides the address match implicitly for zero-length local CIDs. |
| `5-6-39` | informative | Endpoints can send a Stateless Reset for packets that cannot be attributed to an existing connection. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | Endpoints can send a Stateless Reset (Section 10.3) for any packets that cannot be attributed to an existing connection. | N/A — client does not generate stateless resets (project non-goal); detection side is §10.3. |
| `5-6-40` | informative | Packets matched to an existing connection but inconsistent with its state are discarded (not fatal). <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Packets that are matched to an existing connection are discarded if the packets are inconsistent with the state of that connection. | quic/conn_recv.go — inconsistent packets must be silently dropped, not escalated to connection errors. |
| `5-6-41` | informative | Discard applies when a packet indicates a different protocol version than the connection's, or when packet-protection removal fails despite the expected keys being available. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | packets are discarded if they indicate a different protocol version than that of the connection or if the removal of packet protection is unsuccessful once the expected keys are available. | quic/conn_recv.go + conn_crypto.go — AEAD failure with available keys = drop packet, keep connection. |
| `5-6-42` | MAY | Invalid packets lacking strong integrity protection (Initial, Retry, Version Negotiation) may simply be discarded. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Invalid packets that lack strong integrity protection, such as Initial, Retry, or Version Negotiation, MAY be discarded. | quic/conn_recv.go + retry.go — malformed Initial/Retry/VN may be dropped silently. |
| `5-6-43` | MUST | If an endpoint processes any content of such an unauthenticated packet before discovering an error, it must either raise a connection error or fully revert all state changes made. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST generate a connection error if processing the contents of these packets prior to discovering an error, or fully revert any changes made during that processing. | quic/conn_recv.go — parse Initial/Retry/VN fully before committing any connection state, or ensure clean rollback on mid-parse failure. |
| `5-6-44` | informative | Every valid packet sent to a client carries a Destination Connection ID matching a value the client selected (or, for zero-length, matches local address/port). <br>_(client,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Valid packets sent to clients always include a Destination Connection ID that matches a value the client selects. | quic/conn_recv.go — verify the DCID of every received packet is checked against client-selected value(s) before processing. |
| `5-6-45` | informative | A client discards received packets that do not match an existing connection by DCID (or by local IP/port for zero-length). <br>_(client,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Packets that do not match an existing connection -- based on Destination Connection ID or, if this value is zero length, local IP address and port -- are discarded. | quic/conn_recv.go — non-matching datagrams dropped silently, no error, no response. |
| `5-6-46` | MAY | A client receiving packets encrypted with keys it has not yet computed may drop them or buffer them until the keys become computable. <br>_(client,receiver [CLIENT])_ | The client MAY drop these packets, or it MAY buffer them in anticipation of later packets that allow it to compute the key. | quic/conn_recv.go — check whether early-arriving Handshake/1-RTT packets are buffered or dropped before keys exist; either is conforming. |
| `5-6-47` | MUST | A client must discard any packet whose version differs from the version it initially selected. <br>_(client,receiver [CLIENT])_ | If a client receives a packet that uses a different version than it initially selected, it MUST discard that packet. | quic/conn_recv.go — version field check against QUIC v1 (0x00000001) on every long-header packet; non-matching dropped (VN packets, version 0, handled separately). |
| `5-6-48` | SHOULD | A server should send a Version Negotiation packet for an unsupported-version packet large enough to initiate a connection under any supported version. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | the server SHOULD send a Version Negotiation packet as described in Section 6.1. | N/A server-side — test peer only. |
| `5-6-49` | MAY | A server may limit how many packets it answers with Version Negotiation packets. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MAY limit the number of packets to which it responds with a Version Negotiation packet. | N/A server-side. |
| `5-6-50` | MUST | Servers must drop packets specifying unsupported versions that are smaller than the minimum initiating size. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers MUST drop smaller packets that specify unsupported versions. | N/A server-side; client consequence is 5-6-62 (pad first datagram). |
| `5-6-51` | SHOULD | Servers unable to interpret an unsupported-version packet should still respond with Version Negotiation provided the datagram is long enough. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers SHOULD respond with a Version Negotiation packet, provided that the datagram is sufficiently long. | N/A server-side. |
| `5-6-52` | informative | Accepting a fully conforming Initial packet and proceeding with the handshake commits the server to the client-selected version. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | This commits the server to the version that the client selected. | N/A server-side; client may rely on version stability after handshake proceeds (pairs with 5-6-47). |
| `5-6-53` | SHOULD | A server refusing a new connection should send an Initial packet carrying CONNECTION_CLOSE with error code CONNECTION_REFUSED. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | it SHOULD send an Initial packet containing a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame with error code CONNECTION_REFUSED. | quic/errors.go — CONNECTION_REFUSED (0x02) must be defined; conn_recv.go/close.go must surface a handshake-time CONNECTION_CLOSE to the Dial caller. |
