| `9-46` | informative | Migrating a connection to a new server address mid-connection is not supported in QUIC v1 (only the preferred_address mechanism exists). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Migrating a connection to a new server address mid-connection is not supported by the version of QUIC specified in this document. | quic/conn.go doc — justifies the fixed-server-address receive filter in conn_recv.go. |
| `9-47` | MAY | Servers may communicate a preferred address of each address family (IPv4 and IPv6) via the preferred_address transport parameter. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Servers MAY communicate a preferred address of each address family (IPv4 and IPv6) to allow clients to pick the one most suited to their network attachment. | quic/transport_params.go — client MUST be able to parse (or cleanly skip) a received preferred_address TP without error even though it acts on nothing; verify the decoder tolerates it. |
| `9-48` | SHOULD | Once the handshake is confirmed, a client should select one of the server-provided preferred addresses and initiate path validation to it. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Once the handshake is confirmed, the client SHOULD select one of the two addresses provided by the server and initiate path validation | quic/conn.go — deliberately NOT implemented (no migration); SHOULD-level, so ignoring preferred_address is conformant — document the deviation in doc.go. |
| `9-49` | informative | For preferred-address migration the client constructs packets using a previously unused active connection ID from the preferred_address TP or a NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame. <br>_(client [not-client])_ | A client constructs packets using any previously unused active connection ID, taken from either the preferred_address transport parameter or a NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame. | N/A — preferred-address migration not implemented; quic/connid.go need not track the preferred_address-embedded CID as usable (but must parse past it, see 9-47). |
| `9-50` | SHOULD | As soon as path validation of the preferred address succeeds, the client should send all future packets to the new server address with the new connection ID and stop using the old address. <br>_(client [not-client])_ | As soon as path validation succeeds, the client SHOULD begin sending all future packets to the new server address using the new connection ID | N/A — follows from not implementing 9-48; no action in quic/. |
| `9-51` | MUST | If path validation of the preferred address fails, the client must continue sending all future packets to the server's original IP address. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If path validation fails, the client MUST continue sending all future packets to the server's original IP address. | quic/packet_write.go — trivially satisfied: the client always sends to the original dialed address. |
| `9-52` | MUST | A client that migrates to a preferred address must validate the chosen address before migrating. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that migrates to a preferred address MUST validate the address it chooses before migrating; see Section 21.5.3. | quic/conn.go — trivially satisfied (never migrates); binds any future preferred_address implementation. |
| `9-53` | MUST | The server must keep sending non-probing packets from its original address until it receives a non-probing packet from the client at the preferred address and has validated the new path. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server MUST send non-probing packets from its original address until it receives a non-probing packet from the client at its preferred address | Server-role only; no quic/ action. |
| `9-54` | MUST | The server must probe the path toward the client from its preferred address, guarding against attacker-initiated spurious migration. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server MUST probe on the path toward the client from its preferred address. | Server-role only; no quic/ action. |
| `9-55` | SHOULD | After switching to its preferred address, the server should drop newer packets for the connection received on the old IP address. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD drop newer packets for this connection that are received on the old IP address. | Server-role only; no quic/ action. |
| `9-56` | MAY | The server may continue to process delayed packets received on the old IP address after migrating to its preferred address. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server MAY continue to process delayed packets that are received on the old IP address. | Server-role only; no quic/ action. |
| `9-57` | MUST NOT | Preferred addresses are valid only for the connection in which they were provided; a client must not use them for other connections, including resumed ones. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST NOT use these for other connections, including connections that are resumed from the current connection. | quic/transport_params.go + conn.go — verify preferred_address contents are never cached across connections (e.g., not stored alongside session tickets / resumption state in handshake.go). |
| `9-58` | SHOULD | A client needing to migrate before moving to the server's preferred address should path-validate both the original and preferred server addresses from its new address concurrently. <br>_(client [not-client])_ | the client SHOULD perform path validation to both the original and preferred server address from the client's new address concurrently | N/A — requires both client migration and preferred-address support; neither implemented in quic/. |
| `9-59` | MUST | If validation of the server's preferred address succeeds, the client must abandon validation of the original address and migrate to the preferred address. <br>_(client [not-client])_ | If path validation of the server's preferred address succeeds, the client MUST abandon validation of the original address and migrate to using the server's preferred address. | N/A — conditional on unimplemented preferred-address migration; binds future work only. |
| `9-60` | MAY | If preferred-address validation fails but original-address validation succeeds, the client may migrate to its new local address while continuing to send to the server's original address. <br>_(client [not-client])_ | If path validation of the server's preferred address fails but validation of the server's original address succeeds, the client MAY migrate to its new address | N/A — conditional on unimplemented client migration. |
| `9-61` | MUST | If packets at the server's preferred address show a different client source address than seen in the handshake, the server must apply the anti-spoofing protections of 9.3.1/9.3.2. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | the server MUST protect against potential attacks as described in Sections 9.3.1 and 9.3.2 | Server-role only; no quic/ action. |
| `9-62` | SHOULD | Servers should initiate path validation to a client's new address upon receiving a probe packet from a different address. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers SHOULD initiate path validation to the client's new address upon receiving a probe packet from a different address | Server-role only; no quic/ action. |
| `9-63` | SHOULD | A client that migrates to a new local address should use a server preferred address from the same address family. <br>_(client [not-client])_ | A client that migrates to a new address SHOULD use a preferred address from the same address family for the server. | N/A — conditional on unimplemented migration. |
| `9-64` | MAY | The connection ID in the preferred_address transport parameter is not bound to the provided addresses; the client may use it on any path. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client MAY use this connection ID on any path | quic/connid.go — if the preferred_address-embedded CID is ever ingested, it counts against the CID set like any NEW_CONNECTION_ID (sequence number 1 per §5.1.1) and is usable on the current path; check whether transport_params.go feeds it to connid.go or drops it. |
| `9-65` | SHOULD | Endpoints sending over IPv6 should apply an IPv6 flow label per RFC 6437, unless the local API does not allow setting flow labels. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints that send data using IPv6 SHOULD apply an IPv6 flow label in compliance with [RFC6437], unless the local API does not allow setting IPv6 flow labels. | quic/conn.go socket setup (and gro/gso) — Go's net.UDPConn does not expose flow-label control portably; the OS-assigned label satisfies the escape clause. Note as accepted, no code change. |
| `9-66` | MUST | IPv6 flow label generation must be designed to minimize linkability with previously used flow labels, since a stable label enables cross-path correlation. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The flow label generation MUST be designed to minimize the chances of linkability with a previously used flow label | quic/ socket layer — only binds an implementation that sets flow labels itself; since quic/ delegates to the OS (9-65), no action, but flag if explicit flow-label setting is ever added. |
| `9-67` | informative | Including the Destination Connection ID together with source/destination addresses when generating flow labels synchronizes label changes with other observable identifiers (e.g., via keyed hash). <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | Including the Destination Connection ID field in addition to source and destination addresses when generating flow labels ensures that changes are synchronized with changes in other observable identifiers. | N/A — implementation guidance for explicit flow-label generation, which quic/ does not do. |

### 9000 section 10.1-10.2 - Connection Termination: Idle Timeout, Liveness, Immediate Close, Closing and Draining States

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `10.1-10.2-1` | informative | A QUIC connection terminates by exactly one of three mechanisms: idle timeout, immediate close, or stateless reset. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An established QUIC connection can be terminated in one of three ways | quic/close.go + quic/conn.go — verify all teardown paths map to one of: silent idle-timeout discard, CONNECTION_CLOSE close, or stateless-reset detection. |
| `10.1-10.2-2` | MAY | An endpoint may discard connection state if it has no validated path on which it can send packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY discard connection state if it does not have a validated path on which it can send packets | quic/close.go — largely N/A for this client (no migration; path validated by handshake), but confirm no state is held for a conn whose handshake never validated a path. |
| `10.1-10.2-3` | informative | If either endpoint specified max_idle_timeout, the connection is silently closed and state discarded after being idle longer than the minimum of both advertised values. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the connection is silently closed and its state is discarded when it remains idle for longer than the minimum of the max_idle_timeout value advertised by both endpoints | quic/conn.go + quic/transport_params.go — idle timer must use min(local, peer) max_idle_timeout and close SILENTLY (no CONNECTION_CLOSE sent) on expiry. |
| `10.1-10.2-4` | informative | The effective idle timeout is the minimum of the two advertised values, or the sole advertised value if only one endpoint advertises a non-zero value (zero/absent means that endpoint imposes no timeout). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the effective value at an endpoint is computed as the minimum of the two advertised values (or the sole advertised value, if only one endpoint advertises a non-zero value) | quic/transport_params.go + quic/conn.go — check zero/absent max_idle_timeout is treated as 'no limit from that side', NOT as min(...)=0 which would kill the connection instantly. |
| `10.1-10.2-5` | informative | By advertising max_idle_timeout an endpoint commits to sending an immediate close (CONNECTION_CLOSE) if it abandons the connection before the effective idle timeout expires. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint commits to initiating an immediate close (Section 10.2) if it abandons the connection prior to the effective value | quic/close.go — if the client advertises max_idle_timeout, Conn.Close/teardown before that deadline must send CONNECTION_CLOSE, never silently drop the socket. |
| `10.1-10.2-6` | informative | The idle timer restarts when a packet from the peer is received AND processed successfully. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint restarts its idle timer when a packet from its peer is received and processed successfully. | quic/conn_recv.go — restart the idle timer only after successful decryption/processing; undecryptable or discarded packets must not refresh it. |
| `10.1-10.2-7` | informative | The idle timer also restarts when sending an ack-eliciting packet, but only if no other ack-eliciting packet has been sent since the last received-and-processed packet. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint also restarts its idle timer when sending an ack-eliciting packet if no other ack-eliciting packets have been sent since last receiving and processing a packet. | quic/packet_write.go + quic/conn.go — verify the send path restarts the idle timer exactly for the FIRST ack-eliciting packet since last receipt (a first-since-receipt flag). |
| `10.1-10.2-8` | MUST | Endpoints must raise the effective idle timeout period to at least three times the current Probe Timeout (PTO). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | endpoints MUST increase the idle timeout period to be at least three times the current Probe Timeout (PTO) | quic/conn.go + quic/pto.go — idle deadline = max(effective_idle_timeout, 3*PTO); PTO is 'current' (RTT-dependent), so recompute rather than freezing at handshake. |
| `10.1-10.2-9` | informative | An endpoint can probe liveness with a PING or other ack-eliciting frame when the peer could idle-time-out soon (e.g. within a PTO), especially when pending application data is not safely retryable. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint can send a PING or another ack-eliciting frame to test the connection for liveness if the peer could time out soon, such as within a PTO | quic/conn.go + quic/frame_write.go — optional: check whether the client probes with PING before the peer's idle deadline when it has non-retryable data in flight. |
| `10.1-10.2-10` | informative | A QUIC implementation may offer applications an option to defer idle timeout by periodically sending PING frames to keep the connection alive. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An implementation of QUIC might provide applications with an option to defer an idle timeout. | quic/conn.go + http3/ — optional keepalive API; if absent, document that long-idle H3 conns will silently expire at min(max_idle_timeout). |
| `10.1-10.2-11` | informative | Sending a PING restarts the sender's own idle timeout if it is the first ack-eliciting packet since receiving a packet, and the elicited acknowledgment restarts it again. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Sending a packet containing a PING frame restarts the idle timeout for this endpoint also if this is the first ack-eliciting packet sent since receiving a packet. | quic/conn.go — if keepalive PINGs are implemented, verify they go through the normal ack-eliciting send path so both timer-restart rules (send and ack receipt) fire. |
| `10.1-10.2-12` | SHOULD | Application protocols using QUIC should provide guidance on when deferring an idle timeout is appropriate; unnecessary PINGs harm performance. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Application protocols that use QUIC SHOULD provide guidance on when deferring an idle timeout is appropriate. | http3/ — requirement is on protocol specs, not this code; if adding H3 keepalive, follow RFC 9114 guidance rather than inventing policy. |
| `10.1-10.2-13` | informative | Middlebox NAT state for UDP flows commonly expires early; sending packets every 30 seconds is needed in practice to keep most middleboxes from dropping flow state. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | experience shows that sending packets every 30 seconds is necessary to prevent the majority of middleboxes from losing state for UDP flows | quic/conn.go — if a keepalive interval option exists, default should be <=30s; otherwise note this constraint in docs for long-lived client conns. |
| `10.1-10.2-14` | informative | A CONNECTION_CLOSE frame terminates the connection immediately; all streams instantly become closed, and open streams are treated as implicitly reset. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A CONNECTION_CLOSE frame causes all streams to immediately become closed; open streams can be assumed to be implicitly reset. | quic/close.go + quic/stream.go — on sending or receiving CONNECTION_CLOSE, every open stream must fail pending Read/Write with a reset-like connection-closed error; no stream lingers. |
| `10.1-10.2-15` | informative | Immediately after sending a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame, an endpoint enters the closing state. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | After sending a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame, an endpoint immediately enters the closing state | quic/close.go — verify the state transition to closing happens atomically with the close send (under c.mu), so no ordinary packet can be emitted after the close packet. |
| `10.1-10.2-16` | informative | After receiving a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame, an endpoint enters the draining state. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | After receiving a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame, endpoints enter the draining state | quic/conn_recv.go + quic/close.go — parsing CONNECTION_CLOSE (0x1c or 0x1d) must transition the conn to draining, not closing. |
| `10.1-10.2-17` | informative | Protocol violations are handled with an immediate close (CONNECTION_CLOSE), not silent teardown. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Violations of the protocol lead to an immediate close. | quic/errors.go + quic/close.go — every transport-error path (PROTOCOL_VIOLATION, FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR, etc.) must emit CONNECTION_CLOSE and enter closing, except where §10.2.3 permits discarding unauthenticated packets. |
| `10.1-10.2-18` | SHOULD | The closing and draining states should persist for at least three times the current PTO interval. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | These states SHOULD persist for at least three times the current PTO interval | quic/close.go + quic/pto.go — drain/close timer must be 3*PTO computed from current RTT estimates, not a hardcoded constant. |
| `10.1-10.2-19` | MAY | An endpoint with an alternative way to ensure late packets get no response (e.g. it can close the UDP socket) may end the closing/draining states early for faster resource recovery. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | such as those that are able to close the UDP socket, MAY end these states earlier to allow for faster resource recovery | quic/close.go — client owns a dedicated connected UDP socket, so closing it early is a legitimate shortcut; verify whichever behavior is implemented (full 3*PTO wait vs socket close) is deliberate. |
| `10.1-10.2-20` | SHOULD NOT | Servers that keep an open socket for accepting new connections should not end the closing or draining state early. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers that retain an open socket for accepting new connections SHOULD NOT end the closing or draining state early. | N/A for client-only implementation; relevant only if the in-repo test peer shares a listening socket. |
| `10.1-10.2-21` | SHOULD | When the closing or draining state ends, the endpoint should discard all connection state. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Once its closing or draining state ends, an endpoint SHOULD discard all connection state. | quic/close.go — after the drain timer fires, free the conn (keys, streams, ack tracker, timers) and stop the receive loop; check for goroutine/state leaks. |
| `10.1-10.2-22` | MAY | After discarding state, the endpoint may respond to further packets for that connection with a Stateless Reset. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint MAY send a Stateless Reset in response to any further incoming packets belonging to this connection. | quic/close.go — MAY that this client deliberately declines (no stateless reset generation per design); confirm late packets after teardown are simply dropped. |
| `10.1-10.2-23` | informative | In the closing state an endpoint needs to retain only enough information to generate a CONNECTION_CLOSE packet and to identify packets as belonging to the connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint retains only enough information to generate a packet containing a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame and to identify packets as belonging to the connection | quic/close.go — permission to drop stream/flow-control/CC state on entering closing; check what is actually retained and that identification (conn ID match) still works. |
| `10.1-10.2-24` | informative | An endpoint in the closing state sends a packet containing a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in response to any incoming packet it attributes to the connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint in the closing state sends a packet containing a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in response to any incoming packet that it attributes to the connection. | quic/close.go + quic/conn_recv.go — verify packets arriving while closing trigger re-transmission of the close packet (subject to the -25 rate limit), so a peer that missed it still learns of the close. |
| `10.1-10.2-25` | SHOULD | An endpoint should rate-limit the packets it generates in the closing state, e.g. via progressively increasing packet-count or time thresholds before responding. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD limit the rate at which it generates packets in the closing state. | quic/close.go — check close-response path has exponential backoff (packet count or elapsed time), not a 1:1 reply to every incoming packet. |
| `10.1-10.2-26` | MAY | The endpoint's selected connection ID plus the QUIC version suffice to identify packets for a closing connection; all other connection state may be discarded. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint's selected connection ID and the QUIC version are sufficient information to identify packets for a closing connection; the endpoint MAY discard all other connection state. | quic/close.go + quic/connid.go — closing-state packet attribution needs only conn-ID match (client uses zero-length or its chosen DCID on a connected socket); other state droppable. |
| `10.1-10.2-27` | informative | A closing endpoint is not required to process any received frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that is closing is not required to process any received frame. | quic/conn_recv.go — while closing, frame processing may be skipped entirely, except optionally detecting CONNECTION_CLOSE to move to draining (fact -39). |
| `10.1-10.2-28` | MAY | A closing endpoint may retain packet protection keys for incoming packets so it can read and process a peer CONNECTION_CLOSE frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY retain packet protection keys for incoming packets to allow it to read and process a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame. | quic/conn_crypto.go + quic/close.go — if closing->draining transition is implemented, read keys must be kept in closing; check keys are not dropped prematurely. |
| `10.1-10.2-29` | MAY | Alternatively, an endpoint may drop packet protection keys on entering the closing state and answer every received UDP datagram with a CONNECTION_CLOSE packet. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY drop packet protection keys when entering the closing state and send a packet containing a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in response to any UDP datagram that is received. | quic/close.go — if this key-dropping shortcut is taken, the 3x amplification cap of fact -30 becomes a hard MUST; verify which variant the code implements. |
| `10.1-10.2-30` | MUST | An endpoint that dropped packet protection keys in closing must cap the cumulative size of packets it sends at three times the cumulative size of packets received and attributed to the connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | such endpoints MUST limit the cumulative size of packets it sends to three times the cumulative size of the packets that are received and attributed to the connection. | quic/close.go — applies only if keys are dropped while closing (fact -29); if keys are retained and packets validated, this cap is not required. |
| `10.1-10.2-31` | MAY | To minimize closing-state bookkeeping, an endpoint may send the exact same (identical bytes) close packet in response to any received packet. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | endpoints MAY send the exact same packet in response to any received packet. | quic/close.go + quic/packet_write.go — a cached pre-sealed close datagram may be re-sent verbatim; simplest conforming implementation of fact -24. |
| `10.1-10.2-32` | informative | Retransmitting the identical closing packet is an explicit exception to the rule that every packet uses a new packet number (§12.3). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Allowing retransmission of a closing packet is an exception to the requirement that a new packet number be used for each packet | quic/packet_write.go + quic/close.go — do not assert/enforce monotonic packet numbers for a cached close packet; the pn-reuse is legal only here. |
| `10.1-10.2-33` | MUST | In the closing state, packets from an unvalidated (new) source address must either be discarded, or responses to that address capped at three times the bytes received from it. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint in the closing state MUST either discard packets received from an unvalidated address or limit the cumulative size of packets it sends to an unvalidated address | quic/conn_recv.go — a connected UDP socket makes the OS discard other-source datagrams, satisfying the discard branch; verify the client socket is connect()ed (no migration support). |
| `10.1-10.2-34` | informative | A closing endpoint is not expected to handle key updates; an unprocessed key update may merely prevent the closing-to-draining transition. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint is not expected to handle key updates when it is closing | quic/conn_crypto.go — key-update machinery may be frozen once closing; no need to rotate keys to read a post-update CONNECTION_CLOSE. |
| `10.1-10.2-35` | MUST NOT | An endpoint in the draining state must not send any packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint in the draining state MUST NOT send any packets. | quic/close.go + quic/packet_write.go — hard gate: once draining, suppress ALL sends (ACKs, PTO probes, close re-sends, pending stream data); check every send path consults the draining flag under c.mu. |
| `10.1-10.2-36` | informative | Packet protection keys need not be retained once the connection is in the draining state. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Retaining packet protection keys is unnecessary once a connection is in the draining state. | quic/conn_crypto.go + quic/close.go — keys may be zeroized/freed on draining entry; incoming packets can be ignored unread. |
| `10.1-10.2-37` | MAY | On receiving a CONNECTION_CLOSE, an endpoint may send one single packet containing a CONNECTION_CLOSE (using NO_ERROR if appropriate) before entering draining. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that receives a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame MAY send a single packet containing a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame before entering the draining state, using a NO_ERROR code if appropriate. | quic/close.go — optional single close-reply on peer close; if implemented it must be exactly one packet, then draining. |
| `10.1-10.2-38` | MUST NOT | Beyond that optional single close packet, the endpoint must not send further packets, to avoid a perpetual CONNECTION_CLOSE exchange. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT send further packets. | quic/close.go — verify receiving a peer CONNECTION_CLOSE can trigger at most one outbound packet total; no repeated replies (close-loop prevention). |
| `10.1-10.2-39` | MAY | An endpoint already in the closing state may transition to draining if it receives a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame (peer is also closing/draining). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY enter the draining state from the closing state if it receives a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame | quic/close.go + quic/conn_recv.go — optional closing->draining transition; ceases the close-response behavior of fact -24. |
| `10.1-10.2-40` | informative | When transitioning from closing to draining, the draining state ends at the time the closing state would have ended — the timer is not restarted, only transmission ceases. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the draining state ends when the closing state would have ended. | quic/close.go — do NOT re-arm the 3*PTO drain timer on the closing->draining transition; reuse the existing deadline. |
| `10.1-10.2-41` | MUST | After the handshake is confirmed, any CONNECTION_CLOSE frame must be sent in a 1-RTT packet. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | After the handshake is confirmed (see Section 4.1.2 of [QUIC-TLS]), an endpoint MUST send any CONNECTION_CLOSE frames in a 1-RTT packet | quic/close.go + quic/conn_seal.go — once HANDSHAKE_DONE is received (client-side confirmation), close packets must be built at the 1-RTT level only, never Initial/Handshake. |
| `10.1-10.2-42` | MAY | Before the handshake is confirmed, an additional CONNECTION_CLOSE frame may be sent in a packet at a lower packet protection level, since the peer may lack the higher keys. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | another CONNECTION_CLOSE frame MAY be sent in a packet that uses a lower packet protection level | quic/close.go + quic/packet_write.go — pre-confirmation close may be duplicated at Initial/Handshake levels in one coalesced datagram. |
| `10.1-10.2-43` | SHOULD | A server unsure whether the client has Handshake keys should send CONNECTION_CLOSE in both Handshake and Initial packets so at least one is processable. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | a server SHOULD send a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in both Handshake and Initial packets to ensure that at least one of them is processable by the client | quic/conn_recv.go — server-side rule, but the client receive path must accept CONNECTION_CLOSE (0x1c) in Initial and Handshake packets during the handshake and enter draining. |
| `10.1-10.2-44` | informative | A client closing during 0-RTT cannot assume the server accepted 0-RTT; sending CONNECTION_CLOSE additionally in an Initial packet makes the close signal more likely to be received. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that sends a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in a 0-RTT packet cannot be assured that the server has accepted 0-RTT. | quic/close.go — N/A in practice: this client implements no 0-RTT, so it never sends CONNECTION_CLOSE in 0-RTT packets. |
| `10.1-10.2-45` | SHOULD | Before the handshake is confirmed, an endpoint should send its CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in both Handshake and 1-RTT packets, since the peer might not yet process 1-RTT. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Prior to confirming the handshake, a peer might be unable to process 1-RTT packets, so an endpoint SHOULD send a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in both Handshake and 1-RTT packets. | quic/close.go + quic/packet_write.go — client closing between handshake-complete and handshake-confirmed should coalesce the close at Handshake AND 1-RTT levels in one datagram. |
| `10.1-10.2-46` | SHOULD | Before handshake confirmation a server should additionally send the CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in an Initial packet. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server SHOULD also send a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame in an Initial packet. | N/A for client role; client receive path coverage handled under fact -43 (accept close at Initial level). |
| `10.1-10.2-47` | MUST | A CONNECTION_CLOSE of type 0x1d (application close) must be converted to type 0x1c (transport close) when sent in Initial or Handshake packets, to avoid exposing application state. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A CONNECTION_CLOSE of type 0x1d MUST be replaced by a CONNECTION_CLOSE of type 0x1c when sending the frame in Initial or Handshake packets. | quic/close.go + quic/frame_write.go — if the app closes (0x1d) before the handshake confirms, the Initial/Handshake copies must be rewritten as 0x1c; only the 1-RTT copy may carry 0x1d. |
| `10.1-10.2-48` | MUST | When converting a 0x1d close to 0x1c for Initial/Handshake packets, the Reason Phrase field must be cleared (emptied). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints MUST clear the value of the Reason Phrase field | quic/close.go — the converted 0x1c frame must carry an empty Reason Phrase (length 0); check the conversion path does not copy the application reason string. |
| `10.1-10.2-49` | SHOULD | When converting to a 0x1c CONNECTION_CLOSE, the error code should be APPLICATION_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | SHOULD use the APPLICATION_ERROR code when converting to a CONNECTION_CLOSE of type 0x1c | quic/close.go + quic/errors.go — converted frame's error code should be APPLICATION_ERROR (0x0c); verify the constant exists in errors.go and is used here. |
| `10.1-10.2-50` | informative | CONNECTION_CLOSE frames sent in multiple packet types (levels) can be coalesced into a single UDP datagram. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | CONNECTION_CLOSE frames sent in multiple packet types can be coalesced into a single UDP datagram | quic/packet_write.go — verify the coalescing writer can build one datagram carrying the close at Handshake + 1-RTT (and Initial where applicable) levels. |
| `10.1-10.2-51` | MAY | When errors are detected in packets that lack authentication, endpoints may discard those packets instead of immediately closing the connection, hardening against off-path DoS. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | endpoints MAY discard packets rather than immediately close if errors are detected in packets that lack authentication | quic/conn_recv.go — malformed/undecryptable unauthenticated packets (e.g. bogus Initials) may be silently dropped rather than raising CONNECTION_CLOSE; check which policy the receive path applies. |
| `10.1-10.2-52` | informative | An endpoint with no established/connection state (e.g. a server rejecting an Initial) does not enter a closing or draining period after sending CONNECTION_CLOSE. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | An endpoint that has no state for the connection does not enter a closing or draining period on sending a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame. | N/A for this client — the client always creates connection state before it can send CONNECTION_CLOSE; server/test-peer concern only. |

### 9000 section 10.3-11 - RFC 9000 §10.3–§11: Stateless Reset (detection, token calculation, looping) and Error Handling (connection errors, stream errors)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `10.3-11-1` | MAY | An endpoint may send a Stateless Reset when it receives a packet it cannot associate with any active connection. <br>_(endpoint,sender [not-client])_ | An endpoint MAY send a Stateless Reset in response to receiving a packet that it cannot associate with an active connection. | quic/: client-only impl deliberately has no stateless reset generation path — confirm nothing in conn_recv.go emits one. |
| `10.3-11-2` | MUST | An endpoint that wants to signal a fatal connection error must use a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame whenever it is able to, not a stateless reset. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that wishes to communicate a fatal connection error MUST use a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame if it is able. | quic/close.go: every fatal-error path while the connection has keys/state must send CONNECTION_CLOSE, never silently drop the connection. |
| `10.3-11-3` | informative | A stateless reset token is a 16-byte hard-to-guess value issued per connection ID. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint issues a stateless reset token, which is a 16-byte value that is hard to guess | quic/connid.go: token storage and comparison must use exactly 16 bytes. |
| `10.3-11-4` | informative | Tokens are issued in the Stateless Reset Token field of NEW_CONNECTION_ID frames; servers may additionally issue one via the stateless_reset_token transport parameter for the handshake-selected connection ID. <br>_(endpoint,server [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint issues a stateless reset token by including the value in the Stateless Reset Token field of a NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame. | quic/connid.go + frame.go (NEW_CONNECTION_ID parse) + transport_params.go: client must record tokens from BOTH sources and associate each with the right server CID. |
| `10.3-11-5` | informative | Clients cannot use the stateless_reset_token transport parameter because client transport parameters are not confidentiality-protected. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | clients cannot use the stateless_reset_token transport parameter because their transport parameters do not have confidentiality protection | quic/transport_params.go: verify the client never encodes stateless_reset_token in its own transport parameters. |
| `10.3-11-6` | informative | A stateless reset token is invalidated when its associated connection ID is retired via a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Tokens are invalidated when their associated connection ID is retired via a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame | quic/connid.go: when the client sends RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID for a server CID, drop the stored token from the detection set. |
| `10.3-11-7` | informative | Wire layout of a Stateless Reset: 2 fixed bits set to 1 (0b01), at least 38 unpredictable bits, then a 128-bit stateless reset token; it occupies an entire UDP datagram. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | Fixed Bits (2) = 1, Unpredictable Bits (38..), Stateless Reset Token (128), | N/A for generation (client never sends one); detection in conn_recv.go relies only on the trailing 16 bytes, not this layout. |
| `10.3-11-8` | SHOULD | In a Stateless Reset, the remainder of the first byte and all bytes before the token should be indistinguishable from random. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | The remainder of the first byte and an arbitrary number of bytes following it are set to values that SHOULD be indistinguishable from random. | N/A — client does not generate stateless resets. |
| `10.3-11-9` | informative | The stateless reset token occupies the last 16 bytes of the UDP datagram (not of an individual packet). <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | The last 16 bytes of the datagram contain a stateless reset token. | quic/conn_recv.go: token comparison must read the final 16 bytes of the whole received datagram, even when it contains coalesced packets. |
| `10.3-11-10` | informative | For a Stateless Reset to look like a valid QUIC packet, the Unpredictable Bits field needs at least 38 bits, giving a minimum Stateless Reset size of 21 bytes. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | the Unpredictable Bits field needs to include at least 38 bits of data (or 5 bytes, less the two fixed bits) | N/A generation-side; the 21-byte validity floor for received packets is fact 10.3-11-15. |
| `10.3-11-11` | SHOULD | An endpoint should pad every packet it sends to at least 22 bytes longer than the minimum connection ID length it asks the peer to include, so peer stateless resets stay indistinguishable. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | SHOULD ensure that all packets it sends are at least 22 bytes longer than the minimum connection ID length that it requests the peer to include in its packets | quic/packet_write.go: with the client's source CID length (likely zero) the floor is 22 bytes total — verify smallest short-header packets (ACK-only) meet it or PADDING is added. |
| `10.3-11-12` | SHOULD | A Stateless Reset sent in response to a packet of 43 bytes or shorter should be exactly one byte shorter than that packet. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | An endpoint that sends a Stateless Reset in response to a packet that is 43 bytes or shorter SHOULD send a Stateless Reset that is one byte shorter | N/A — client does not generate stateless resets. |
| `10.3-11-13` | informative | The 21/22-byte size figures assume the token equals the minimum AEAD expansion; more unpredictable bytes are needed if a larger-expansion AEAD could have been negotiated. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | Additional unpredictable bytes are necessary if the endpoint could have negotiated a packet protection scheme with a larger minimum expansion. | N/A — QUIC v1 AEADs (AES-GCM/ChaCha20) all have 16-byte expansion; no generation path. |
| `10.3-11-14` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not send a Stateless Reset that is three times or more the size of the packet it received (anti-amplification). <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT send a Stateless Reset that is three times or more larger than the packet it receives | N/A — client does not generate stateless resets. |
| `10.3-11-15` | MUST | Endpoints must discard packets too small to be valid QUIC packets; with QUIC-TLS AEADs, short-header packets smaller than 21 bytes are never valid. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints MUST discard packets that are too small to be valid QUIC packets. | quic/conn_recv.go / packet.go: verify short-header datagrams under 21 bytes are rejected before decryption is attempted. |
| `10.3-11-16` | MUST | Stateless Resets must be formatted as short-header packets. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | Endpoints MUST send Stateless Resets formatted as a packet with a short header. | N/A — client does not generate stateless resets. |
| `10.3-11-17` | MUST | Any received packet ending in a valid stateless reset token must be treated as a Stateless Reset, even if it has a long header (other QUIC versions may allow that). <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | endpoints MUST treat any packet ending in a valid stateless reset token as a Stateless Reset | quic/conn_recv.go: the token check must not be gated on the header form bit — long-header datagrams ending in a known token count too. |
| `10.3-11-18` | MAY | An endpoint may send a Stateless Reset in response to a long-header packet, though ignoring it is often equally effective since tokens are unavailable before handshake completion. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | An endpoint MAY send a Stateless Reset in response to a packet with a long header. | N/A — client does not generate stateless resets. |
| `10.3-11-19` | informative | A Stateless Reset carries a random Destination Connection ID (the sender cannot recover the SCID from a short header), so it will not match the connection IDs the receiver expects. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The Destination Connection ID will therefore differ from the value used in previous packets. | quic/conn_recv.go: a datagram must NOT be dropped for unknown/mismatched DCID before the stateless-reset token comparison runs. |
| `10.3-11-20` | informative | Detection algorithm: an endpoint remembers all stateless reset tokens associated with connection IDs and remote addresses of datagrams it recently sent (from NEW_CONNECTION_ID frames and server transport parameters), excluding tokens for unused or retired connection IDs. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint remembers all stateless reset tokens associated with the connection IDs and remote addresses for datagrams it has recently sent. | quic/connid.go: maintain the active token set keyed by (server CID in use, remote address); exclude unused and retired CIDs. |
| `10.3-11-21` | informative | A datagram is identified as a Stateless Reset by comparing its last 16 bytes against all stateless reset tokens associated with the remote address it was received from. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | comparing the last 16 bytes of the datagram with all stateless reset tokens associated with the remote address on which the datagram was received | quic/conn_recv.go: comparison scope is tokens for that remote address only (for a single-path client, the server address). |
| `10.3-11-22` | MAY | The stateless-reset token check may be skipped for a datagram if any packet from that datagram is successfully processed. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints MAY skip this check if any packet from a datagram is successfully processed. | quic/conn_recv.go: acceptable optimization — run the token check only on datagrams whose packets fail to parse/decrypt. |
| `10.3-11-23` | MUST | The token comparison must be performed whenever the first packet of an incoming datagram cannot be associated with a connection or cannot be decrypted. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | the comparison MUST be performed when the first packet in an incoming datagram either cannot be associated with a connection or cannot be decrypted | quic/conn_recv.go: both failure paths (unknown DCID and AEAD decrypt failure) must fall through to the stateless-reset token check, not just drop. |
| `10.3-11-24` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not check received datagrams against tokens for connection IDs it has never used or that it has retired. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT check for any stateless reset tokens associated with connection IDs it has not used or for connection IDs that have been retired. | quic/connid.go: only tokens whose CID the client has actually used in sent packets and not retired may be in the comparison set. |
| `10.3-11-25` | MUST | Token comparison must not leak information about token values (e.g., use constant-time comparison or compare keyed-PRF-transformed values). <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | endpoints MUST perform the comparison without leaking information about the value of the token | quic/conn_recv.go or connid.go: use crypto/subtle.ConstantTimeCompare (or HMAC-transformed storage) for the 16-byte token match. |
| `10.3-11-26` | MUST | If the last 16 bytes of a datagram match a stored stateless reset token, the endpoint must enter the draining period and send no further packets on the connection. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | the endpoint MUST enter the draining period and not send any further packets on this connection | quic/close.go + conn_recv.go: on token match transition straight to draining (no CONNECTION_CLOSE sent) and surface a stateless-reset error to the caller. |
| `10.3-11-27` | MUST | Any stateless reset token an endpoint issues must be difficult to guess (e.g., random per connection, or PRF of a static key and the connection ID, truncated to 16 bytes). <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | The stateless reset token MUST be difficult to guess. | quic/connid.go: applies only if the client ever issues NEW_CONNECTION_ID frames with tokens — verify it does not (client-only, no migration). |
| `10.3-11-28` | MUST | An endpoint using the static-key token design must use the same connection ID length for all connections or encode the length recoverably without state, and cannot use zero-length connection IDs. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | MUST either use the same connection ID length for all connections or encode the length of the connection ID such that it can be recovered without state | N/A — token-generation design constraint; client issues no tokens. |
| `10.3-11-29` | informative | Revealing a stateless reset token lets any entity kill the connection, so a token value can only be used once. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | Revealing the stateless reset token allows any entity to terminate the connection, so a value can only be used once. | N/A — issuer-side property; client only consumes tokens. |
| `10.3-11-30` | MUST NOT | A given (connection ID, static key) combination must not be used for another connection when generating tokens. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | the combination of connection ID and static key MUST NOT be used for another connection | N/A — static-key token generation; not implemented client-side. |
| `10.3-11-31` | MUST NOT | A connection ID from a connection that was reset by revealing its stateless reset token must not be reused for new connections at nodes sharing the same static key. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | A connection ID from a connection that is reset by revealing the stateless reset token MUST NOT be reused for new connections at nodes that share a static key | N/A — server-cluster token generation concern. |
| `10.3-11-32` | MUST NOT | The same stateless reset token must not be issued for multiple connection IDs. <br>_(endpoint,sender [not-client])_ | The same stateless reset token MUST NOT be used for multiple connection IDs. | N/A issuer-side; the receiver-side option is fact 10.3-11-33. |
| `10.3-11-33` | MAY | An endpoint receiving a duplicate stateless reset token value (same token for multiple connection IDs) may treat it as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION; comparing against all previous values is not required. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | a duplicate value MAY be treated as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION | quic/connid.go: optional duplicate-token check when processing NEW_CONNECTION_ID; if implemented, raise PROTOCOL_VIOLATION connection error (errors.go). |
| `10.3-11-34` | informative | Stateless Resets carry no cryptographic protection; the token match is the only authentication. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Note that Stateless Resets do not have any cryptographic protection. | quic/conn_recv.go: treat a matched reset as unauthenticated signal — enter draining but do not trust any other content of the datagram. |
| `10.3-11-35` | MUST | Every Stateless Reset an endpoint sends must be smaller than the packet that triggered it, unless the endpoint keeps state sufficient to prevent reset loops. <br>_(endpoint,sender [not-client])_ | An endpoint MUST ensure that every Stateless Reset that it sends is smaller than the packet that triggered it, unless it maintains state sufficient to prevent looping. | N/A — client does not generate stateless resets. |
| `10.3-11-36` | informative | An endpoint can rate-limit stateless reset generation by counting resets sent and stopping at a limit, ideally with separate limits per remote address. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | An endpoint can remember the number of Stateless Resets that it has sent and stop generating new Stateless Resets once a limit is reached. | N/A — client does not generate stateless resets. |
| `10.3-11-37` | SHOULD | An endpoint that detects an error should signal that error to its peer. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that detects an error SHOULD signal the existence of that error to its peer. | quic/close.go + errors.go: detected transport errors should produce CONNECTION_CLOSE (or RESET_STREAM for stream-level), not silent local teardown. |
| `10.3-11-38` | informative | Both transport-level and application-level errors can affect an entire connection, but only application-level errors can be isolated to a single stream. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Only application-level errors can be isolated to a single stream | quic/stream.go vs close.go: transport error codes must escalate to connection close; only app error codes flow through RESET_STREAM/STOP_SENDING. |
| `10.3-11-39` | SHOULD | The most appropriate error code should be included in the frame that signals the error. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The most appropriate error code (Section 20) SHOULD be included in the frame that signals the error. | quic/errors.go + close.go: each detection site should carry its specific transport error code into the CONNECTION_CLOSE frame. |
| `10.3-11-40` | MAY | An endpoint may use any applicable error code when it detects an error condition; a generic code such as PROTOCOL_VIOLATION or INTERNAL_ERROR can always substitute for a specific one. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint MAY use any applicable error code when it detects an error condition | quic/errors.go: generic PROTOCOL_VIOLATION/INTERNAL_ERROR fallback is conformant where the specific code is awkward to plumb. |
| `10.3-11-41` | MUST NOT | A stateless reset must not be used by an endpoint that still has the state necessary to send a CONNECTION_CLOSE or RESET_STREAM frame on the connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A stateless reset MUST NOT be used by an endpoint that has the state necessary to send a frame on the connection. | quic/close.go: trivially satisfied (no reset generation), but confirm all error paths with live state route to CONNECTION_CLOSE/RESET_STREAM. |
| `10.3-11-42` | MUST | Errors that render the connection unusable (obvious protocol-semantics violations, connection-wide state corruption) must be signaled with a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | corruption of state that affects an entire connection, MUST be signaled using a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame | quic/close.go: connection-fatal detections (frame/flow-control/state violations) must all trigger the immediate-close CONNECTION_CLOSE path. |
| `10.3-11-43` | informative | Application-specific protocol errors are carried in the CONNECTION_CLOSE frame variant with frame type 0x1d. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Application-specific protocol errors are signaled using the CONNECTION_CLOSE frame with a frame type of 0x1d. | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: 0x1d CONNECTION_CLOSE codec (no frame-type field, app error code) — used when http3 closes with an H3 error. |
| `10.3-11-44` | informative | Transport-specific errors, including all errors defined in RFC 9000, are carried in the CONNECTION_CLOSE frame variant with frame type 0x1c. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Errors that are specific to the transport, including all those described in this document, are carried in the CONNECTION_CLOSE frame with a frame type of 0x1c | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go + close.go: transport error codes must go out as 0x1c (with offending-frame-type field), never 0x1d. |
| `10.3-11-45` | SHOULD | An endpoint should be prepared to retransmit a packet containing CONNECTION_CLOSE if it keeps receiving packets on a terminated connection, with limits on retransmission count and duration. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD be prepared to retransmit a packet containing a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame if it receives more packets on a terminated connection. | quic/close.go: during the closing period, incoming packets should trigger a (rate-limited) re-send of the CONNECTION_CLOSE packet. |
| `10.3-11-46` | MAY | Because the Initial AEAD provides no strong authentication, an endpoint may discard an invalid Initial packet, even where the spec otherwise mandates a connection error. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | As the AEAD for Initial packets does not provide strong authentication, an endpoint MAY discard an invalid Initial packet. | quic/conn_recv.go + initial.go: Initial packets that fail decryption or validation may be dropped silently instead of raising a connection error. |
| `10.3-11-47` | informative | A packet may only be discarded (instead of raising the mandated error) if the endpoint does not process its frames or fully reverts the effects of any processing. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint can only discard a packet if it does not process the frames in the packet or reverts the effects of any processing. | quic/conn_recv.go: frame processing must be all-or-nothing per packet — no partially applied state before a mid-packet discard. |
| `10.3-11-48` | informative | An application-level error confined to a single stream, with the connection otherwise recoverable, is signaled by sending RESET_STREAM with an appropriate error code for just that stream. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the endpoint can send a RESET_STREAM frame (Section 19.4) with an appropriate error code to terminate just the affected stream | quic/stream.go: per-stream abort path emits RESET_STREAM with the caller-supplied application error code, leaving the connection open. |
| `10.3-11-49` | MUST | RESET_STREAM must only be instigated by the application protocol using QUIC, never by the transport on its own initiative. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | RESET_STREAM MUST only be instigated by the application protocol that uses QUIC. | quic/stream.go: RESET_STREAM should originate only from the public cancel/reset API or the STOP_SENDING-triggered path — check no transport-internal code resets streams for transport reasons. |
| `10.3-11-50` | informative | The semantics of the application error code carried in RESET_STREAM are defined by the application protocol; the transport treats it as opaque. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The semantics of the application error code carried in RESET_STREAM are defined by the application protocol. | quic/stream.go + http3/: transport passes the app error code through unmodified; http3 maps it to/from H3 error codes. |
| `10.3-11-51` | informative | A remote application instance terminates a peer's sending on a stream via STOP_SENDING, which triggers an automatic RESET_STREAM from the receiver of the STOP_SENDING. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | a remote instance uses the STOP_SENDING frame, which triggers an automatic RESET_STREAM | quic/stream.go: on receiving STOP_SENDING for a stream the client is sending on, automatically send RESET_STREAM (echoing the app error code per §3.5). |
| `10.3-11-52` | SHOULD | Application protocols should define rules for handling streams that are prematurely canceled by either endpoint. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Application protocols SHOULD define rules for handling streams that are prematurely canceled by either endpoint. | http3/: verify the H3 layer handles streams reset mid-request (e.g., H3_REQUEST_CANCELLED semantics) rather than treating any reset as a transport failure. |

### 9000 section 12 - Packets and Frames

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `12-1` | informative | Version Negotiation packets carry no cryptographic protection and must be parsed without AEAD or header protection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Version Negotiation packets have no cryptographic protection | quic/conn_recv.go — parse Version Negotiation packets without AEAD/header-protection removal. |
| `12-2` | informative | Retry packets are protected by an AEAD function against accidental modification (integrity tag). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Retry packets use an AEAD function [AEAD] to protect against accidental modification. | quic/retry.go — verify the Retry integrity tag AEAD before accepting a Retry. |
| `12-3` | informative | Initial packets use an AEAD whose keys derive from a wire-visible value (client DCID), so they have no effective confidentiality, only path/modification protection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Initial packets use an AEAD function, the keys for which are derived using a value that is visible on the wire. | quic/initial.go + conn_crypto.go — Initial secrets derived from original DCID + version salt. |
| `12-4` | informative | Handshake, 0-RTT, and 1-RTT packets are protected with keys derived from the TLS cryptographic handshake. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | All other packets are protected with keys derived from the cryptographic handshake. | quic/conn_crypto.go + handshake.go — install Handshake/1-RTT keys from TLS secrets per level. |
| `12-5` | informative | The Packet Number field is confidentiality-protected separately via header protection, and the underlying packet number increases with each packet sent in a space. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The Packet Number field that appears in some packet types has alternative confidentiality protection that is applied as part of header protection | quic/crypto*.go + conn_seal.go — header-protection mask applied on seal, removed before PN decode. |
| `12-6` | informative | Initial, 0-RTT, and Handshake packets carry a Length field delimiting the packet; Length covers both the Packet Number and Payload fields. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | packets contain a Length field that determines the end of the packet. The length includes both the Packet Number and Payload fields | quic/packet.go + packet_write.go — long-header Length = PN length + payload length; conn_recv.go uses it to delimit coalesced packets. |
| `12-7` | informative | A sender can coalesce multiple QUIC packets into one UDP datagram using the Length field (handshake acceleration, PMTU probes). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Using the Length field, a sender can coalesce multiple QUIC packets into one UDP datagram. | quic/packet_write.go — coalescing writer (Initial+Handshake flights, PMTU probes). |
| `12-8` | MUST | Receivers must be able to process coalesced packets in a single UDP datagram. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receivers MUST be able to process coalesced packets. | quic/conn_recv.go — datagram loop splits by Length and processes every contained packet. |
| `12-9` | informative | Coalescing packets in increasing encryption-level order (Initial, 0-RTT, Handshake, 1-RTT) makes single-pass processing by the receiver more likely. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Coalescing packets in order of increasing encryption levels (Initial, 0-RTT, Handshake, 1-RTT; see Section 4.1.4 of [QUIC-TLS]) makes it more likely that the receiver will be able to process | quic/packet_write.go — emit coalesced packets in Initial → Handshake → 1-RTT order. |
| `12-10` | informative | A short-header packet has no Length field and therefore can only be the last packet in a UDP datagram. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A packet with a short header does not include a length, so it can only be the last packet included in a UDP datagram. | quic/packet_write.go — 1-RTT packet must be last when coalescing; conn_recv.go — short header consumes the datagram remainder. |
| `12-11` | SHOULD | An endpoint should pack multiple frames into one packet at the same encryption level instead of coalescing multiple same-level packets. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD include multiple frames in a single packet if they are to be sent at the same encryption level | quic/packet_write.go + frame_write.go — frame packing fills one packet per level per datagram. |
| `12-12` | MAY | Receivers may route/demultiplex a datagram based on the information in its first packet. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receivers MAY route based on the information in the first packet contained in a UDP datagram. | quic/conn_recv.go — demux by first packet's DCID; trivially satisfied if one conn per UDP socket. |
| `12-13` | MUST NOT | Senders must not coalesce packets with different connection IDs into a single UDP datagram. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Senders MUST NOT coalesce QUIC packets with different connection IDs into a single UDP datagram. | quic/packet_write.go — all packets coalesced into one datagram must share the same DCID. |
| `12-14` | SHOULD | Receivers should ignore subsequent coalesced packets whose Destination Connection ID differs from the first packet in the datagram. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receivers SHOULD ignore any subsequent packets with a different Destination Connection ID than the first packet in the datagram. | quic/conn_recv.go — compare each coalesced packet's DCID against the first; skip mismatches. |
| `12-15` | MUST | Each coalesced packet must be individually processed and separately acknowledged, as if received in different datagrams. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The receiver of coalesced QUIC packets MUST individually process each QUIC packet and separately acknowledge them, as if they were received as the payload of different UDP datagrams. | quic/conn_recv.go + ack_tracker.go — per-packet processing and per-space ACK tracking for every coalesced packet. |
| `12-16` | MUST | If decryption of one coalesced packet fails, the receiver may discard or buffer it but must attempt to process the remaining packets in the datagram. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | the receiver MAY either discard or buffer the packet for later processing and MUST attempt to process the remaining packets. | quic/conn_recv.go — decrypt failure (e.g. keys not yet available) must not abort the datagram loop. |
| `12-17` | informative | Retry, Version Negotiation, and short-header packets have no Length field and cannot be followed by other packets in the same datagram; Retry/VN are never coalesced. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | packets with a short header (Section 17.3) do not contain a Length field and so cannot be followed by other packets in the same UDP datagram. | quic/conn_recv.go — Retry/VN/short-header terminate datagram parsing. |
| `12-18` | informative | The packet number is an integer in the range 0 to 2^62-1, used in the cryptographic nonce. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The packet number is an integer in the range 0 to 2^62-1. | quic/packet.go + conn_seal.go — PN as uint64 capped at 2^62-1; feeds AEAD nonce. |
| `12-19` | informative | Each endpoint maintains separate packet numbers for sending and for receiving. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Each endpoint maintains a separate packet number for sending and receiving. | quic/conn_seal.go (send PN counters) vs ack_tracker.go (received largest PN) — independent per direction. |
| `12-20` | informative | In long and short headers, packet numbers are reduced and encoded in 1 to 4 bytes (per Section 17.1). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | packet numbers are reduced and encoded in 1 to 4 bytes | quic/packet.go + packet_write.go — PN truncation on encode and window-based expansion on decode (§17.1 algorithm). |
| `12-21` | informative | Version Negotiation and Retry packets do not include a packet number. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Version Negotiation (Section 17.2.1) and Retry (Section 17.2.5) packets do not include a packet number. | quic/packet.go + retry.go — no PN parse/assignment for VN and Retry. |
| `12-22` | informative | Packet numbers are divided into three spaces: Initial (Initial packets), Handshake (Handshake packets), and Application data (0-RTT and 1-RTT packets). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Packet numbers are divided into three spaces in QUIC | quic/conn_seal.go + loss.go + ack_tracker.go — three per-space PN/loss/ACK states; 0-RTT and 1-RTT share app-data space. |
| `12-23` | informative | Packets can only be acknowledged in the same packet number space/encryption level in which they were sent (Initial acks Initial, Handshake acks Handshake). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Initial packets can only be sent with Initial packet protection keys and acknowledged in packets that are also Initial packets. | quic/ack_tracker.go + packet_write.go — ACK frames generated and sent only at the matching encryption level. |
| `12-24` | MUST | Packet numbers in each space start at 0, and each subsequent packet in a space must increase the packet number by at least one. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Packet numbers in each space start at packet number 0. Subsequent packets sent in the same packet number space MUST increase the packet number by at least one. | quic/conn_seal.go — per-space send PN starts at 0 and strictly increments. |
| `12-25` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must never reuse a packet number within the same packet number space in one connection (retransmissions get new packet numbers). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A QUIC endpoint MUST NOT reuse a packet number within the same packet number space in one connection. | quic/conn_seal.go + loss.go — retransmitted frames go in new packets with fresh PNs; PN never reused. |
| `12-26` | MUST | If the send packet number reaches 2^62-1, the sender must close the connection without sending CONNECTION_CLOSE or any further packets. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | If the packet number for sending reaches 2^62-1, the sender MUST close the connection without sending a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame or any further packets | quic/conn_seal.go + close.go — on PN exhaustion, silent close path (no CONNECTION_CLOSE emitted). |
| `12-27` | MAY | After packet-number exhaustion, an endpoint may send a Stateless Reset in response to further received packets. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | an endpoint MAY send a Stateless Reset (Section 10.3) in response to further packets that it receives. | N/A — client scope excludes stateless reset generation. |
| `12-28` | MUST | A receiver must discard a newly unprotected packet unless certain it has not already processed a packet with the same packet number in that space. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver MUST discard a newly unprotected packet unless it is certain that it has not processed another packet with the same packet number | quic/ack_tracker.go + conn_recv.go — duplicate-PN detection per space before frame processing. |
| `12-29` | MUST | Duplicate suppression must happen after removing packet protection, not on the unauthenticated header packet number. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Duplicate suppression MUST happen after removing packet protection | quic/conn_recv.go — dedupe check placed after successful AEAD decryption. |
| `12-30` | informative | Duplicate-detection state can be bounded by keeping a minimum packet number below which packets are immediately dropped, accounting for RTT variation. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The data required for detecting duplicates can be limited by maintaining a minimum packet number below which all packets are immediately dropped. | quic/ack_tracker.go — bounded received-PN range state (minimum-PN floor / range compaction). |
| `12-31` | informative | Version Negotiation, Stateless Reset, and Retry packets do not contain frames; packet payloads otherwise consist of a sequence of complete frames. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Version Negotiation, Stateless Reset, and Retry packets do not contain frames. | quic/conn_recv.go — never run the frame parser on VN/Retry/stateless-reset-shaped packets. |
| `12-32` | MUST | A packet payload that contains frames must contain at least one frame and may contain multiple frames and frame types. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The payload of a packet that contains frames MUST contain at least one frame, and MAY contain multiple frames and multiple frame types. | quic/packet_write.go — never seal a packet with an empty plaintext payload. |
| `12-33` | MUST | Receipt of a packet containing no frames is a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST treat receipt of a packet containing no frames as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/conn_recv.go + errors.go — empty decrypted payload → connection error PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. |
| `12-34` | informative | Frames always fit within a single QUIC packet and cannot span multiple packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Frames always fit within a single QUIC packet and cannot span multiple packets. | quic/frame.go — a frame truncated at packet end is FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR; no cross-packet frame reassembly. |
| `12-35` | informative | Every frame begins with a Frame Type field followed by type-dependent fields (generic frame layout, Figure 12). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Each frame begins with a Frame Type, indicating its type, followed by additional type-dependent fields | quic/frame.go — parse varint frame type, then per-type field parser. |
| `12-36` | informative | PADDING is frame type 0x00, permitted in Initial, Handshake, 0-RTT, and 1-RTT packets; marked N (not ack-eliciting) and P (probing). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x00 \| PADDING \| Section 19.1 \| IH01 \| NP \| | quic/frame.go codepoint 0x00; conn_recv.go allow in all frame-bearing packet types; ack_tracker.go excludes from ack-eliciting. |
| `12-37` | informative | PING is frame type 0x01, permitted in Initial, Handshake, 0-RTT, and 1-RTT packets, and is ack-eliciting. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x01 \| PING \| Section 19.2 \| IH01 \| \| | quic/frame.go 0x01; pto.go uses PING as probe at any level; conn_recv.go legality. |
| `12-38` | informative | ACK uses frame types 0x02-0x03, permitted in Initial, Handshake, and 1-RTT but never 0-RTT; marked N (not ack-eliciting) and C (not counted in bytes in flight). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x02-0x03 \| ACK \| Section 19.3 \| IH_1 \| NC \| | quic/frame.go 0x02-0x03; conn_recv.go legality; cc.go/loss.go exclude ACK-only packets from bytes in flight and ack-eliciting. |
| `12-39` | informative | RESET_STREAM is frame type 0x04, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets (application data space). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x04 \| RESET_STREAM \| Section 19.4 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x04; conn_recv.go reject in Initial/Handshake; stream.go handles receive/send. |
| `12-40` | informative | STOP_SENDING is frame type 0x05, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x05 \| STOP_SENDING \| Section 19.5 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x05; conn_recv.go reject in Initial/Handshake; stream.go. |
| `12-41` | informative | CRYPTO is frame type 0x06, permitted in Initial, Handshake, and 1-RTT packets but not 0-RTT. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x06 \| CRYPTO \| Section 19.6 \| IH_1 \| | quic/frame.go 0x06; conn_crypto.go/handshake.go consume CRYPTO at Initial/Handshake/1-RTT. |
| `12-42` | informative | NEW_TOKEN is frame type 0x07, permitted only in 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x07 \| NEW_TOKEN \| Section 19.7 \| ___1 \| | quic/frame.go 0x07; conn_recv.go reject outside 1-RTT; client receives tokens (retry.go/token storage if any). |
| `12-43` | informative | STREAM uses frame types 0x08-0x0f (low bits carry OFF/LEN/FIN flags), permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT; contents are flow controlled (F). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x08-0x0f \| STREAM \| Section 19.8 \| __01 \| F \| | quic/frame.go 0x08-0x0f flag decoding; conn_recv.go reject in Initial/Handshake; recvflow.go/stream.go flow-control accounting. |
| `12-44` | informative | MAX_DATA is frame type 0x10, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x10 \| MAX_DATA \| Section 19.9 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x10; conn.go/recvflow.go connection send-credit update on receipt; legality in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-45` | informative | MAX_STREAM_DATA is frame type 0x11, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x11 \| MAX_STREAM_DATA \| Section 19.10 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x11; stream.go per-stream send credit; legality in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-46` | informative | MAX_STREAMS uses frame types 0x12-0x13 (bidirectional/unidirectional), permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x12-0x13 \| MAX_STREAMS \| Section 19.11 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x12-0x13; conn.go stream-limit gating; legality in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-47` | informative | DATA_BLOCKED is frame type 0x14, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x14 \| DATA_BLOCKED \| Section 19.12 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x14; frame_write.go emits when connection flow-control blocked; legality in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-48` | informative | STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED is frame type 0x15, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x15 \| STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED \| Section 19.13 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x15; stream.go blocked signaling; legality in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-49` | informative | STREAMS_BLOCKED uses frame types 0x16-0x17, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x16-0x17 \| STREAMS_BLOCKED \| Section 19.14 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x16-0x17; conn.go stream-limit blocked signaling; legality in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-50` | informative | NEW_CONNECTION_ID is frame type 0x18, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets; marked P (probing). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x18 \| NEW_CONNECTION_ID \| Section 19.15 \| __01 \| P \| | quic/frame.go 0x18; connid.go processes received NEW_CONNECTION_ID; legality in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-51` | informative | RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID is frame type 0x19, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x19 \| RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID \| Section 19.16 \| __01 \| | quic/frame.go 0x19; connid.go retirement send/receive; legality in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-52` | informative | PATH_CHALLENGE is frame type 0x1a, permitted only in 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets; marked P (probing). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x1a \| PATH_CHALLENGE \| Section 19.17 \| __01 \| P \| | quic/frame.go 0x1a; conn_recv.go — client must still echo PATH_RESPONSE on receipt even without migration support. |
| `12-53` | informative | PATH_RESPONSE is frame type 0x1b, permitted only in 1-RTT packets; marked P (probing). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x1b \| PATH_RESPONSE \| Section 19.18 \| ___1 \| P \| | quic/frame.go 0x1b; frame_write.go sends PATH_RESPONSE only in 1-RTT; conn_recv.go legality. |
| `12-54` | informative | CONNECTION_CLOSE uses frame types 0x1c-0x1d, permitted in Initial/Handshake (0x1c only), 0-RTT, and 1-RTT; marked N (not ack-eliciting). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x1c-0x1d \| CONNECTION_CLOSE \| Section 19.19 \| ih01 \| N \| | quic/close.go send/receive; ack_tracker.go excludes from ack-eliciting; conn_recv.go legality per packet type. |
| `12-55` | informative | HANDSHAKE_DONE is frame type 0x1e, permitted only in 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | \| 0x1e \| HANDSHAKE_DONE \| Section 19.20 \| ___1 \| | quic/frame.go 0x1e; handshake.go — client receives it in 1-RTT to confirm handshake; reject outside 1-RTT in conn_recv.go. |
| `12-56` | informative | The Frame Type field itself carries frame-specific flags for ACK, STREAM, MAX_STREAMS, STREAMS_BLOCKED, and CONNECTION_CLOSE; for all other frames it only identifies the frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The Frame Type in ACK, STREAM, MAX_STREAMS, STREAMS_BLOCKED, and CONNECTION_CLOSE frames is used to carry other frame-specific flags. | quic/frame.go — decode type ranges and extract flags (ECN, OFF/LEN/FIN, bidi/uni, transport/app) from type value. |
| `12-57` | informative | In Initial and Handshake packets, only CONNECTION_CLOSE of type 0x1c may appear (0x1d is prohibited there). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Only a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame of type 0x1c can appear in Initial or Handshake packets. | quic/close.go — use 0x1c when closing at Initial/Handshake level; conn_recv.go reject 0x1d in Initial/Handshake. |
| `12-58` | informative | All frame types can appear in 1-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Note that all frames can appear in 1-RTT packets. | quic/conn_recv.go — 1-RTT frame allowlist permits every defined type. |
| `12-59` | MUST | Receipt of a frame in a packet type where it is not permitted (per Table 3) is a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST treat receipt of a frame in a packet type that is not permitted as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/conn_recv.go — per-packet-type frame allowlist enforcing Table 3; errors.go PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. |
| `12-60` | informative | Packets containing only N-marked frames (PADDING, ACK, CONNECTION_CLOSE) are not ack-eliciting. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Packets containing only frames with this marking are not ack-eliciting; see Section 13.2. | quic/ack_tracker.go — ack-eliciting classification: any frame other than ACK/PADDING/CONNECTION_CLOSE. |
| `12-61` | informative | Packets containing only C-marked frames (ACK) do not count toward bytes in flight for congestion control. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Packets containing only frames with this marking do not count toward bytes in flight for congestion control purposes | quic/cc.go + loss.go — ACK-only packets excluded from bytes-in-flight accounting. |
| `12-62` | informative | Packets containing only P-marked frames (PADDING, NEW_CONNECTION_ID, PATH_CHALLENGE, PATH_RESPONSE) can be used to probe new network paths during migration. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | Packets containing only frames with this marking can be used to probe new network paths during connection migration | N/A — connection migration/path probing out of client scope. |
