| `12-63` | informative | The contents of F-marked frames (STREAM) are subject to flow control. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The contents of frames with this marking are flow controlled; see Section 4. | quic/recvflow.go + stream.go — STREAM data charged against stream and connection flow-control limits. |
| `12-64` | MUST | Receipt of a frame of unknown type is a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST treat the receipt of a frame of unknown type as a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. | quic/frame.go — unknown frame type in parser → connection error FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR (errors.go). |
| `12-65` | informative | All frames are idempotent in QUIC v1: a valid frame received more than once causes no undesirable side effects or errors. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | All frames are idempotent in this version of QUIC. | quic/stream.go + conn.go — handlers must tolerate re-delivered frames (duplicate RESET_STREAM, stale MAX_DATA, etc.) harmlessly. |
| `12-66` | informative | The Frame Type field is encoded as a variable-length integer (Section 16), with the shortest-encoding exception below. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The Frame Type field uses a variable-length integer encoding (see Section 16), with one exception. | quic/frame.go + internal/bytesx — frame type read/written via QUIC varint codec. |
| `12-67` | MUST | A frame type must be sent using the shortest possible varint encoding (single byte for all types defined in RFC 9000); applies to all current and future frame types. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | a frame type MUST use the shortest possible encoding | quic/frame_write.go — emit frame types as minimal-length varints (single byte for 0x00-0x1e). |
| `12-68` | MAY | An endpoint may treat receipt of a frame type encoded longer than necessary as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY treat the receipt of a frame type that uses a longer encoding than necessary as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/frame.go — decide whether the parser enforces minimal-length frame-type encoding; document the choice. |
| `12-69` | MAY | PADDING, PING, and CRYPTO frames may appear in any packet number space. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | PADDING, PING, and CRYPTO frames MAY appear in any packet number space. | quic/packet_write.go — permit PADDING/PING/CRYPTO in Initial and Handshake payloads. |
| `12-70` | MAY | CONNECTION_CLOSE frames of type 0x1c (QUIC-layer errors) may appear in any packet number space. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | CONNECTION_CLOSE frames signaling errors at the QUIC layer (type 0x1c) MAY appear in any packet number space. | quic/close.go — 0x1c CONNECTION_CLOSE may be sent at Initial/Handshake/1-RTT depending on available keys. |
| `12-71` | MUST | CONNECTION_CLOSE frames of type 0x1d (application errors) must only appear in the application data packet number space. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | CONNECTION_CLOSE frames signaling application errors (type 0x1d) MUST only appear in the application data packet number space. | quic/close.go — app close before 1-RTT keys must be converted to 0x1c (per Section 10.2.3); conn_recv.go reject 0x1d in Initial/Handshake. |
| `12-72` | MAY | ACK frames may appear in any packet number space but can only acknowledge packets from that same space; 0-RTT packets cannot contain ACK frames. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | ACK frames MAY appear in any packet number space but can only acknowledge packets that appeared in that packet number space. | quic/ack_tracker.go — strictly per-space ACK generation; never acknowledge across spaces. |
| `12-73` | MUST | All frame types other than PADDING, PING, CRYPTO, ACK, and CONNECTION_CLOSE(0x1c) must only be sent in the application data packet number space. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | All other frame types MUST only be sent in the application data packet number space. | quic/packet_write.go + frame_write.go — Initial/Handshake payloads restricted to PADDING/PING/ACK/CRYPTO/CONNECTION_CLOSE(0x1c). |
| `12-74` | informative | ACK, CRYPTO, HANDSHAKE_DONE, NEW_TOKEN, PATH_RESPONSE, and RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames cannot be sent in 0-RTT packets. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | it is not possible to send the following frames in 0-RTT packets for various reasons: ACK, CRYPTO, HANDSHAKE_DONE, NEW_TOKEN, PATH_RESPONSE, and RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID. | N/A — 0-RTT out of client scope; nothing to enforce on send path. |
| `12-75` | MAY | A server may treat receipt of those frames in 0-RTT packets as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MAY treat receipt of these frames in 0-RTT packets as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | N/A — server-side rule; client sends no 0-RTT packets. |

### 9000 section 13.1-13.2 - RFC 9000 §13, 13.1, 13.2.1–13.2.7: Packetization and Reliability — Packet Processing and Generating Acknowledgments

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `13.1-13.2-1` | MAY | A sender may wait briefly to collect multiple frames before sending a packet that is not maximally packed, to avoid many small packets. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender MAY wait for a short period of time to collect multiple frames before sending a packet that is not maximally packed | quic/packet_write.go — check whether the writer coalesces pending frames into one datagram before flush; no delay timer needed (MAY). |
| `13.1-13.2-2` | MAY | An implementation may use application-sending-behavior knowledge or heuristics to decide whether and how long to wait before sending an underfilled packet. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | An implementation MAY use knowledge about application sending behavior or heuristics to determine whether and for how long to wait. | quic/packet_write.go — optional pacing/nagle-style heuristic; absence is conformant. |
| `13.1-13.2-3` | informative | A single QUIC packet can carry multiple STREAM frames from one or more streams (stream multiplexing by interleaving). <br>_(sender,receiver [CLIENT])_ | A single QUIC packet can include multiple STREAM frames from one or more streams. | quic/conn_recv.go + quic/packet_write.go — receive path must accept multiple STREAM frames per packet; write path may pack several. |
| `13.1-13.2-4` | informative | Implementations are advised to include as few streams as necessary per outgoing packet (to limit head-of-line blocking on loss) without underfilling packets. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Implementations are advised to include as few streams as necessary in outgoing packets without losing transmission efficiency to underfilled packets. | quic/packet_write.go — check stream-scheduling order when filling a packet; advisory only. |
| `13.1-13.2-5` | MUST NOT | A packet must not be acknowledged until packet protection has been removed successfully and every frame in it has been processed. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A packet MUST NOT be acknowledged until packet protection has been successfully removed and all frames contained in the packet have been processed. | quic/conn_recv.go + quic/ack_tracker.go — verify the packet number is recorded in the ACK tracker only after AEAD open succeeds AND all frames parse/process without error. |
| `13.1-13.2-6` | informative | For STREAM frames, 'processed' means the data has been enqueued for the application protocol; delivery/consumption by the application is not required before acknowledging. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | this means the data has been enqueued in preparation to be received by the application protocol, but it does not require that data be delivered and consumed. | quic/stream.go + quic/conn_recv.go — ACK eligibility should follow recv-buffer enqueue, not application Read(). |
| `13.1-13.2-7` | informative | After a packet is fully processed, the receiver acknowledges it by sending one or more ACK frames containing that packet number. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | a receiver acknowledges receipt by sending one or more ACK frames containing the packet number of the received packet. | quic/ack_tracker.go — processed packet numbers must enter the tracked ranges emitted in subsequent ACK frames. |
| `13.1-13.2-8` | SHOULD | An endpoint should treat an acknowledgment for a packet it never sent as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION, when detectable. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD treat receipt of an acknowledgment for a packet it did not send as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION | quic/loss.go — check ACK processing rejects Largest Acknowledged >= next unsent packet number in that space with PROTOCOL_VIOLATION (errors.go code). |
| `13.1-13.2-9` | informative | Only ack-eliciting packets force an ACK frame within the maximum ack delay; non-ack-eliciting packets are only acknowledged when an ACK is sent for other reasons. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | only ack-eliciting packets cause an ACK frame to be sent within the maximum ack delay. | quic/ack_tracker.go — verify the delayed-ACK timer is armed only by ack-eliciting packets; non-ack-eliciting ones are recorded but do not arm it. |
| `13.1-13.2-10` | SHOULD | When sending a packet for any reason, an endpoint should attempt to bundle an ACK frame if one has not been sent recently. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | When sending a packet for any reason, an endpoint SHOULD attempt to include an ACK frame if one has not been sent recently. | quic/packet_write.go — check outgoing packet assembly opportunistically prepends a fresh ACK frame from ack_tracker.go. |
| `13.1-13.2-11` | SHOULD | Every packet received and processed should be acknowledged at least once. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Every packet SHOULD be acknowledged at least once | quic/ack_tracker.go — ranges must cover all processed packets, including non-ack-eliciting ones, until safely retired. |
| `13.1-13.2-12` | MUST | Ack-eliciting packets must be acknowledged at least once within the delay the endpoint advertised in its max_ack_delay transport parameter. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | ack-eliciting packets MUST be acknowledged at least once within the maximum delay an endpoint communicated using the max_ack_delay transport parameter | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/transport_params.go — verify the delayed-ACK timer never exceeds the client's own advertised max_ack_delay. |
| `13.1-13.2-13` | informative | max_ack_delay is an explicit contract: the endpoint promises never to intentionally delay acknowledgment of an ack-eliciting packet beyond that value; excess pollutes the peer's RTT estimate. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint promises to never intentionally delay acknowledgments of an ack-eliciting packet by more than the indicated value | quic/transport_params.go — advertised max_ack_delay must match the actual ack timer bound used in ack_tracker.go. |
| `13.1-13.2-14` | informative | A sender uses the receiver's max_ack_delay value when computing timer-based retransmission timeouts (PTO). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender uses the receiver's max_ack_delay value in determining timeouts for timer-based retransmission | quic/pto.go — verify PTO for the ApplicationData space adds the peer's max_ack_delay from transport_params.go. |
| `13.1-13.2-15` | MUST | An endpoint must acknowledge all ack-eliciting Initial and Handshake packets immediately, and all ack-eliciting 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets within its advertised max_ack_delay (except when decryption keys are unavailable pre-confirmation). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST acknowledge all ack-eliciting Initial and Handshake packets immediately and all ack-eliciting 0-RTT and 1-RTT packets within its advertised max_ack_delay | quic/ack_tracker.go — verify Initial/Handshake spaces use no ack delay (immediate ACK), while the 1-RTT space may delay up to max_ack_delay. |
| `13.1-13.2-16` | informative | Before handshake confirmation an endpoint lacking keys may buffer undecryptable Handshake/0-RTT/1-RTT packets and acknowledge them once the keys become available. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | It might therefore buffer them and acknowledge them when the requisite keys become available. | quic/conn_recv.go + quic/conn_crypto.go — check undecryptable-packet buffering per level and re-processing (into ack_tracker) when keys install. |
| `13.1-13.2-17` | MUST NOT | Because ACK-only packets are not congestion controlled, an endpoint must not send more than one ACK-only packet in response to a single received ack-eliciting packet. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint MUST NOT send more than one such packet in response to receiving an ack-eliciting packet. | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/packet_write.go — verify at most one ACK-only packet is emitted per ack-eliciting receipt (ack-pending flag cleared on send). |
| `13.1-13.2-18` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not send a non-ack-eliciting packet in response to a non-ack-eliciting packet, even if there are packet gaps preceding it (prevents infinite ACK loops). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT send a non-ack-eliciting packet in response to a non-ack-eliciting packet, even if there are packet gaps that precede the received packet. | quic/ack_tracker.go — verify receiving an ACK-only (non-ack-eliciting) packet never triggers sending an ACK-only packet, even with gaps. |
| `13.1-13.2-19` | SHOULD | An endpoint should bundle an ACK frame with other outgoing frames when there are new ack-eliciting packets to acknowledge. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD send an ACK frame with other frames when there are new ack-eliciting packets to acknowledge. | quic/packet_write.go — outgoing STREAM/CRYPTO packets should piggyback pending ACKs from ack_tracker.go. |
| `13.1-13.2-20` | MAY | When only non-ack-eliciting packets need acknowledgment, an endpoint may withhold the ACK frame from outgoing packets until an ack-eliciting packet is received. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint MAY choose not to send an ACK frame with outgoing frames until an ack-eliciting packet has been received. | quic/ack_tracker.go — optional optimization; check whether ack-pending distinguishes ack-eliciting from non-ack-eliciting receipts. |
| `13.1-13.2-21` | MUST NOT | An endpoint that occasionally adds ack-eliciting frames to otherwise non-ack-eliciting packets (to elicit ACKs) must not do so in every such packet, to avoid an infinite ACK feedback loop. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint MUST NOT send an ack-eliciting frame in all packets that would otherwise be non-ack-eliciting, to avoid an infinite feedback loop of acknowledgments. | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/pto.go — if PING is ever bundled into ACK-only packets, verify it is rate-limited (e.g. once per RTT), not on every one. |
| `13.1-13.2-22` | SHOULD | An endpoint should generate and send an ACK without delay when a received ack-eliciting packet has a packet number lower than another already-received ack-eliciting packet (reordering). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | when the received packet has a packet number less than another ack-eliciting packet that has been received | quic/ack_tracker.go — verify immediate-ACK trigger on out-of-order (lower-numbered) ack-eliciting arrivals. |
| `13.1-13.2-23` | SHOULD | An endpoint should generate and send an ACK without delay when a received ack-eliciting packet is above the highest received and there are missing packets between them (gap detected). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | when the packet has a packet number larger than the highest-numbered ack-eliciting packet that has been received and there are missing packets between that packet and this packet. | quic/ack_tracker.go — verify immediate-ACK trigger when a new largest packet creates a gap in the tracked ranges. |
| `13.1-13.2-24` | SHOULD | Packets marked with the ECN Congestion Experienced (CE) codepoint in the IP header should be acknowledged immediately. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | packets marked with the ECN Congestion Experienced (CE) codepoint in the IP header SHOULD be acknowledged immediately | quic/conn_recv.go + quic/gro — check whether ECN bits are read from the socket at all; if ECN is unsupported (no ECN counts sent), this is N/A but should be documented. |
| `13.1-13.2-25` | SHOULD | A receiver should send an ACK frame after receiving at least two ack-eliciting packets. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver SHOULD send an ACK frame after receiving at least two ack-eliciting packets. | quic/ack_tracker.go — verify the every-2-ack-eliciting-packets immediate-ACK threshold alongside the max_ack_delay timer. |
| `13.1-13.2-26` | MAY | A receiver may process multiple available packets (e.g., a coalesced datagram or GRO batch) before deciding whether to send an ACK frame. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver MAY process multiple available packets before determining whether to send an ACK frame in response. | quic/conn_recv.go + quic/gro — batching the ACK decision after a full datagram/GRO batch is permitted; check ack decision runs after the batch, not per-packet. |
| `13.1-13.2-27` | SHOULD | ACK frames should always acknowledge the most recently received packets; the more out of order packets are, the more urgent an updated ACK is. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | ACK frames SHOULD always acknowledge the most recently received packets | quic/ack_tracker.go — generated ACK must always include the newest ranges; older ranges are what get dropped under size pressure. |
| `13.1-13.2-28` | informative | An ACK frame is expected to fit in a single QUIC packet; if it does not, the oldest ranges (smallest packet numbers) are omitted. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | If it does not, then older ranges (those with the smallest packet numbers) are omitted. | quic/frame_write.go + quic/ack_tracker.go — when serializing an ACK that would overflow the packet, verify truncation drops smallest-numbered ranges first. |
| `13.1-13.2-29` | SHOULD | After the receiver's own ACK frame is acknowledged, it should stop tracking the ACK Ranges that ACK covered. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | the receiver SHOULD stop tracking those acknowledged ACK Ranges. | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/loss.go — verify sent-ACK Largest Acknowledged is recorded per packet and ranges are pruned when that packet is acked (see 13.2.4). |
| `13.1-13.2-30` | informative | Discarding unacknowledged ACK Ranges to bound ACK frame size is necessary when the ACK frame would otherwise be too large to fit in a packet. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | This is necessary if an ACK frame would be too large to fit in a packet. | quic/ack_tracker.go — verify a hard cap on tracked range count exists so ACK serialization can never exceed the packet. |
| `13.1-13.2-31` | MAY | Receivers may further limit ACK frame size below the packet limit to preserve space for other frames or bound acknowledgment overhead. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receivers MAY also limit ACK frame size further to preserve space for other frames or to limit the capacity that acknowledgments consume. | quic/ack_tracker.go — optional; a fixed max-ranges constant satisfies this. |
| `13.1-13.2-32` | MUST | A receiver must retain an ACK Range unless it can guarantee it will never subsequently accept packets with numbers in that range. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver MUST retain an ACK Range unless it can ensure that it will not subsequently accept packets with numbers in that range. | quic/ack_tracker.go — when discarding a range, verify a rising floor (minimum packet number) exists and conn_recv.go drops/ignores packets below it rather than re-accepting. |
| `13.1-13.2-33` | MUST | Even if all ACK Ranges are discarded, the receiver must retain the largest successfully processed packet number, since it is needed to recover (decode) subsequent packet numbers. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | they MUST retain the largest packet number that has been successfully processed, as that is used to recover packet numbers from subsequent packets | quic/packet.go + quic/ack_tracker.go — largest-received per PN space must persist independently of range pruning and feed packet-number decoding (§17.1). |
| `13.1-13.2-34` | SHOULD | A receiver should include an ACK Range containing the largest received packet number in every ACK frame it sends. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver SHOULD include an ACK Range containing the largest received packet number in every ACK frame. | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/frame_write.go — Largest Acknowledged in every emitted ACK must be the true largest received; truncation must never drop the newest range. |
| `13.1-13.2-35` | informative | Sending a Largest Acknowledged lower than a previously sent one could cause the peer's ECN validation to unnecessarily disable ECN. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | including a lower value than what was included in a previous ACK frame could cause ECN to be unnecessarily disabled | quic/ack_tracker.go — Largest Acknowledged must be monotonically non-decreasing across generated ACKs. |
| `13.1-13.2-36` | informative | Range-limiting algorithm: save the Largest Acknowledged of each sent ACK; when that packet is acknowledged, stop acknowledging packets <= that Largest Acknowledged value. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | the receiver can stop acknowledging packets less than or equal to the Largest Acknowledged field in the sent ACK frame. | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/loss.go — check sent packets record the ACK's Largest Acknowledged and OnAck prunes tracker ranges at that watermark. |
| `13.1-13.2-37` | informative | A receiver sending only non-ack-eliciting packets can occasionally send a PING or other small ack-eliciting frame (e.g., once per round trip) to elicit an ACK and bound its ACK-range state. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | a receiver could send a PING or other small ack-eliciting frame occasionally, such as once per round trip, to elicit an ACK from the peer. | quic/ack_tracker.go — optional; if implemented, must respect fact 21's rate limit. |
| `13.1-13.2-38` | informative | Without ACK loss this tracking algorithm tolerates a minimum of 1 RTT of reordering; with ACK loss plus reordering, some acknowledgments may never be seen and spurious retransmissions can occur. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | In cases without ACK frame loss, this algorithm allows for a minimum of 1 RTT of reordering. | quic/ack_tracker.go — design note only; no code obligation. |
| `13.1-13.2-39` | informative | The endpoint measures the intentional delay between receiving the largest-numbered packet and sending its acknowledgment, and encodes it in the ACK frame's ACK Delay field. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint encodes this acknowledgment delay in the ACK Delay field of an ACK frame | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/frame_write.go — verify ACK Delay is measured from receipt of largest packet to ACK send, scaled by our ack_delay_exponent. |
| `13.1-13.2-40` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not include delays it does not control (e.g., OS kernel hold time) in the ACK Delay field. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT include delays that it does not control when populating the ACK Delay field in an ACK frame. | quic/ack_tracker.go — ACK Delay clock must start at packet processing (or key-buffer entry per fact 41), not at socket enqueue time it cannot observe. |
| `13.1-13.2-41` | SHOULD | Endpoints should include buffering delays caused by unavailable decryption keys in the ACK Delay field, since these are large and non-repeating. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | endpoints SHOULD include buffering delays caused by unavailability of decryption keys, since these delays can be large and are likely to be non-repeating. | quic/conn_recv.go + quic/ack_tracker.go — if undecryptable packets are buffered, timestamp them at arrival so ACK Delay covers the key-wait. |
| `13.1-13.2-42` | SHOULD | When the measured acknowledgment delay exceeds the endpoint's advertised max_ack_delay (notably during the handshake), it should report the measured delay rather than clamping it. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | When the measured acknowledgment delay is larger than its max_ack_delay, an endpoint SHOULD report the measured delay. | quic/ack_tracker.go — verify ACK Delay is not clamped to max_ack_delay on the sending side. |
| `13.1-13.2-43` | MUST | ACK frames must only be carried in packets of the same packet number space as the packets being acknowledged. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | ACK frames MUST only be carried in a packet that has the same packet number space as the packet being acknowledged | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/packet_write.go — per-space ACK state; Initial ACKs go only in Initial packets, Handshake in Handshake, 1-RTT in 1-RTT (conn_seal.go levels). |
| `13.1-13.2-44` | MUST | Packets protected with 1-RTT keys must be acknowledged in packets that are also protected with 1-RTT keys. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | packets that are protected with 1-RTT keys MUST be acknowledged in packets that are also protected with 1-RTT keys. | quic/packet_write.go + quic/conn_seal.go — verify ApplicationData-space ACKs are sealed only at the 1-RTT level, never Handshake. |
| `13.1-13.2-45` | MUST | A client's 0-RTT packets must be acknowledged by the server in packets protected by 1-RTT keys (server obligation; N/A here since this client does no 0-RTT). <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Packets that a client sends with 0-RTT packet protection MUST be acknowledged by the server in packets protected by 1-RTT keys. | quic/ — N/A: project excludes 0-RTT; nothing to check beyond confirming no 0-RTT send path exists. |
| `13.1-13.2-46` | informative | Packets containing PADDING frames count as in flight for congestion control; PADDING-only packets consume congestion window without generating window-opening acknowledgments. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Packets containing PADDING frames are considered to be in flight for congestion control purposes | quic/cc.go + quic/bbr.go — verify bytes_in_flight includes PADDING-only packets (e.g., padded Initial datagrams) even though they are not ack-eliciting. |
| `13.1-13.2-47` | SHOULD | To avoid a congestion-window deadlock, a sender should periodically send other (ack-eliciting) frames in addition to PADDING frames so the receiver's acknowledgments open the window. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | a sender SHOULD ensure that other frames are sent periodically in addition to PADDING frames to elicit acknowledgments from the receiver. | quic/packet_write.go + quic/pto.go — if any PADDING-only packets are ever emitted (padding of Initials), verify they carry or are followed by ack-eliciting frames (CRYPTO/PING). |

### 9000 section 13.3-13.4 - Retransmission of Information (per-frame-type rules) and Explicit Congestion Notification

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `13.3-13.4-1` | informative | Lost QUIC packets and their frames are not retransmitted whole; the information they carried is sent again in new frames as needed. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | QUIC packets that are determined to be lost are not retransmitted whole. | quic/loss.go — verify loss detection re-queues frame-level information for fresh packetization, never re-sends identical packet bytes. |
| `13.3-13.4-2` | informative | Information is re-sent when a packet carrying it is declared lost, and re-sending stops once a packet carrying it is acknowledged. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | information is sent again when a packet containing that information is determined to be lost, and sending ceases when a packet containing that information is acknowledged | quic/loss.go — check per-frame retransmit state is cleared on ACK of any packet carrying that information. |
| `13.3-13.4-3` | informative | CRYPTO frame data is retransmitted per the loss-recovery rules until all of it has been acknowledged. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Data sent in CRYPTO frames is retransmitted according to the rules in [QUIC-RECOVERY], until all data has been acknowledged. | quic/handshake.go + quic/loss.go — verify unacked CRYPTO ranges are re-sent until fully acknowledged. |
| `13.3-13.4-4` | informative | Pending CRYPTO data for Initial and Handshake packets is discarded when the keys for that packet number space are discarded. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Data in CRYPTO frames for Initial and Handshake packets is discarded when keys for the corresponding packet number space are discarded. | quic/conn_seal.go + quic/conn_crypto.go — key-discard path must also drop queued/unacked CRYPTO retransmissions for that space. |
| `13.3-13.4-5` | informative | Lost application data is retransmitted in new STREAM frames unless the endpoint has already sent RESET_STREAM for that stream. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Application data sent in STREAM frames is retransmitted in new STREAM frames unless the endpoint has sent a RESET_STREAM for that stream. | quic/stream.go + quic/loss.go — on loss, re-queue stream data only if send side has not been reset. |
| `13.3-13.4-6` | informative | After sending RESET_STREAM, an endpoint stops sending STREAM frames for that stream entirely. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Once an endpoint sends a RESET_STREAM frame, no further STREAM frames are needed. | quic/stream.go — RESET_STREAM send must cancel all pending/retransmit STREAM frames for the stream. |
| `13.3-13.4-7` | informative | ACK frames always carry the most recent set of acknowledgments and the ack delay measured from the largest acknowledged packet, never stale copies. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | ACK frames carry the most recent set of acknowledgments and the acknowledgment delay from the largest acknowledged packet | **VERIFY** quic/ack_tracker.go — verify ACK frames are built fresh from current ranges at send time, never queued for retransmit. -- Quote, level (informative) and roles are all correct, but the requirement text overstates the source. RFC 9000 §13.3 says only that 'ACK frames carry the most recent set of acknowledgments and the acknowledgment delay from the largest acknowledged packet, as described in Section 13.2.1', and then warns that 'resending old ACK frames CAN cause the peer to generate an inflated RTT sample or unnecessarily disable ECN'. The same section explicitly states 'it is not forbidden to retransmit copies of frames from lost packets' (line 4423-4424), so replaying an old ACK frame is discouraged, not prohibited. Fix: reword the requirement to 'ACK frames are assembled to carry the current acknowledgment set and an ack delay measured from the largest acknowledged packet; resending old ACK frames is discouraged (see 13.3-13.4-8), not forbidden' and drop the absolute 'never stale copies'. The reconcile note should likewise read 'ACK frames SHOULD be built fresh at send time' rather than asserting a prohibition on replay. |
| `13.3-13.4-8` | informative | Delaying ACK-bearing packets or resending old ACK frames can inflate the peer's RTT sample or make it disable ECN — avoid both. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Delaying the transmission of packets containing ACK frames or resending old ACK frames can cause the peer to generate an inflated RTT sample or unnecessarily disable ECN. | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/packet_write.go — ensure lost ACK frames are never replayed verbatim; ack delay recomputed per transmission. |
| `13.3-13.4-9` | informative | RESET_STREAM is re-sent until it is acknowledged or until all stream data is acknowledged (sending part reaches Reset Recvd or Data Recvd). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Cancellation of stream transmission, as carried in a RESET_STREAM frame, is sent until acknowledged or until all stream data is acknowledged by the peer | quic/stream.go + quic/loss.go — RESET_STREAM must be retransmitted on loss until acked or send state terminal. |
| `13.3-13.4-10` | MUST NOT | The content of a retransmitted RESET_STREAM frame (error code, final size) must be identical to the original. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The content of a RESET_STREAM frame MUST NOT change when it is sent again. | quic/stream.go — store reset error code + final size once at reset time; retransmit path must reuse the stored values. |
| `13.3-13.4-11` | informative | STOP_SENDING is re-sent until the receiving part of the stream enters Data Recvd or Reset Recvd state. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | a request to cancel stream transmission, as encoded in a STOP_SENDING frame, is sent until the receiving part of the stream enters either a "Data Recvd" or "Reset Recvd" state | quic/stream.go + quic/loss.go — STOP_SENDING retransmit gated on receive-side state, not just frame ACK. |
| `13.3-13.4-12` | informative | CONNECTION_CLOSE-bearing packets are not retransmitted when loss is detected; close-signal resending follows Section 10 (respond to incoming packets during closing). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Connection close signals, including packets that contain CONNECTION_CLOSE frames, are not sent again when packet loss is detected. | quic/close.go — CONNECTION_CLOSE must be excluded from loss-based retransmission; closing-state resend logic is packet-arrival driven. |
| `13.3-13.4-13` | informative | A MAX_DATA frame with the current (possibly updated) value is sent when the packet with the most recent MAX_DATA is declared lost or the endpoint raises the limit; avoid sending it too often. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | An updated value is sent in a MAX_DATA frame if the packet containing the most recently sent MAX_DATA frame is declared lost | quic/recvflow.go + quic/loss.go — on loss of latest MAX_DATA, re-send with current limit; check rate-limiting of updates. **DONE (#471)**: `regrantAfterLoss`, called from `detectLost` for the application space. Classing this informative was the costly part of the judgement — the omission was a hard deadlock, not a performance nicety: a dropped grant left the peer permanently behind, and the next grant is only produced by consuming data the peer is now blocked from sending. Reproduced at ~20% of runs through 10% loss, fixed, gated in `quic/regrant_test.go` and in the loss interop suite. |
| `13.3-13.4-14` | informative | A MAX_STREAM_DATA frame with the current value is sent when the packet with the most recent MAX_STREAM_DATA for that stream is lost or the limit is updated. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | an updated value is sent when the packet containing the most recent MAX_STREAM_DATA frame for a stream is lost or when the limit is updated | quic/recvflow.go — per-stream MAX_STREAM_DATA loss triggers re-send carrying the current (not old) offset. **DONE (#471)**: same `regrantAfterLoss`, over the set of streams that have actually been granted, carrying `s.recvMax`. The peer's DATA_BLOCKED / STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED is also answered now (`regrantConnLimit` / `regrantStreamLimit`), but measurement showed that half alone is not enough — the peer's signal is dropped by the same loss and is sent once per limit. |
| `13.3-13.4-15` | SHOULD | An endpoint should stop sending MAX_STREAM_DATA once the receiving part of the stream reaches Size Known or Reset Recvd. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD stop sending MAX_STREAM_DATA frames when the receiving part of the stream enters a "Size Known" or "Reset Recvd" state. | quic/recvflow.go + quic/stream.go — suppress MAX_STREAM_DATA after FIN offset known or RESET_STREAM received. |
| `13.3-13.4-16` | informative | A MAX_STREAMS frame with the current value is sent when the packet with the most recent MAX_STREAMS for that stream type is declared lost or the limit is updated. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | an updated value is sent when a packet containing the most recent MAX_STREAMS for a stream type frame is declared lost or when the limit is updated | quic/recvflow.go (or conn.go stream-limit accounting) — per-type MAX_STREAMS loss triggers re-send with current limit. |
| `13.3-13.4-17` | informative | Blocked signals are scoped: DATA_BLOCKED to the connection, STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED to a stream, STREAMS_BLOCKED to a stream type. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | DATA_BLOCKED frames have connection scope, STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frames have stream scope, and STREAMS_BLOCKED frames are scoped to a specific stream type. | quic/frame_write.go + quic/stream.go — verify the client emits blocked frames at the correct scope (or document if not sent at all). |
| `13.3-13.4-18` | informative | A new blocked frame for a scope is sent when the packet with the most recent one is lost, but only while the endpoint is still blocked on that limit. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A new frame is sent if a packet containing the most recent frame for a scope is lost, but only while the endpoint is blocked on the corresponding limit. | quic/loss.go — blocked-frame retransmit must re-check blocked condition at re-send time, not blindly replay. |
| `13.3-13.4-19` | informative | Blocked frames always carry the limit that is causing blocking at the moment they are transmitted. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | These frames always include the limit that is causing blocking at the time that they are transmitted. | quic/frame_write.go — DATA_BLOCKED/STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED/STREAMS_BLOCKED encode the current limit at write time. |
| `13.3-13.4-20` | informative | PATH_CHALLENGE frames are sent periodically until a matching PATH_RESPONSE arrives or the liveness/path-validation need ends. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | A liveness or path validation check using PATH_CHALLENGE frames is sent periodically until a matching PATH_RESPONSE frame is received | quic/ — client does no migration/liveness probing, so no PATH_CHALLENGE send path expected; confirm frame_write.go has none or it follows this rule. |
| `13.3-13.4-21` | informative | Each transmission of a PATH_CHALLENGE frame uses a different payload. <br>_(sender [not-client])_ | PATH_CHALLENGE frames include a different payload each time they are sent. | quic/ — N/A unless PATH_CHALLENGE sending is added; then payload must be fresh random per send. |
| `13.3-13.4-22` | informative | PATH_RESPONSE frames are sent exactly once and never retransmitted; the peer re-issues PATH_CHALLENGE to evoke another response. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Responses to path validation using PATH_RESPONSE frames are sent just once. | **VERIFY** quic/conn_recv.go — client answering a server PATH_CHALLENGE must send one PATH_RESPONSE and must NOT queue it for loss-based retransmit. -- Quote is verbatim (line 4396: "Responses to path validation using PATH_RESPONSE frames are sent just once.") and the level (informative, no RFC 2119 keyword) is right, but applies_to is wrong. The rule governs the endpoint TRANSMITTING the PATH_RESPONSE frame — a send-side retransmission rule in the §13.3 sender list — not a receive-side rule. Tagging it "receiver" is also inconsistent with the rest of the catalog (e.g. 13.3-13.4-27 uses "receiver" for genuine receive-side handling). Fix: applies_to = ["sender"] (or ["endpoint"]); client_relevant true and the reconcile note (client answers a server PATH_CHALLENGE exactly once, never queued for loss-based retransmit) are both correct. |
| `13.3-13.4-23` | informative | NEW_CONNECTION_ID frames are retransmitted if the packet carrying them is lost. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | New connection IDs are sent in NEW_CONNECTION_ID frames and retransmitted if the packet containing them is lost. | quic/connid.go + quic/loss.go — if the client issues its own CIDs to the server, lost NEW_CONNECTION_ID must be re-sent. |
| `13.3-13.4-24` | informative | A retransmitted NEW_CONNECTION_ID carries the same sequence number value as the original. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Retransmissions of this frame carry the same sequence number value. | quic/connid.go — retransmit path must reuse the stored sequence number, never allocate a new one. |
| `13.3-13.4-25` | informative | RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames are retransmitted if the packet carrying them is lost. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | retired connection IDs are sent in RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames and retransmitted if the packet containing them is lost. | quic/connid.go + quic/loss.go — client retiring server CIDs must re-send RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID on loss until acked. |
| `13.3-13.4-26` | informative | NEW_TOKEN frames are retransmitted by the sender if the packet carrying them is lost. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | NEW_TOKEN frames are retransmitted if the packet containing them is lost. | quic/ — server-only send; relevant only to the test peer if it issues tokens. |
| `13.3-13.4-27` | informative | Reordered or duplicated NEW_TOKEN frames are detected only by direct comparison of frame contents — the receiver must tolerate duplicates. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | No special support is made for detecting reordered and duplicated NEW_TOKEN frames other than a direct comparison of the frame contents. | quic/conn_recv.go — client NEW_TOKEN handling must accept duplicates gracefully (dedupe by content if tokens are stored). |
| `13.3-13.4-28` | informative | Lost PING and PADDING frames carry no information and are never repaired/retransmitted. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | PING and PADDING frames contain no information, so lost PING or PADDING frames do not require repair. | quic/loss.go + quic/pto.go — PING/PADDING must be excluded from retransmit queues (PTO probes send fresh PINGs, not repairs). |
| `13.3-13.4-29` | MUST | HANDSHAKE_DONE must be retransmitted until acknowledged. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The HANDSHAKE_DONE frame MUST be retransmitted until it is acknowledged. | quic/ — server-only; client merely receives/acks it. Applies to the test peer only. |
| `13.3-13.4-30` | SHOULD | Endpoints should prioritize retransmitting lost data over sending new data unless application priorities say otherwise. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints SHOULD prioritize retransmission of data over sending new data, unless priorities specified by the application indicate otherwise | quic/packet_write.go — frame scheduling order should drain retransmit queues before new stream data. |
| `13.3-13.4-31` | informative | A sender that retransmits verbatim frame copies (permitted) must handle the payload shrinking due to changed packet number length, connection ID length, or path MTU. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender that retransmits copies of frames needs to handle decreases in available payload size due to changes in packet number length, connection ID length, and path MTU. | quic/packet_write.go — if frames are replayed as-is, verify they still fit after PN-length/CID/MTU changes (re-chunk STREAM/CRYPTO if needed). |
| `13.3-13.4-32` | MUST | A receiver must accept packets containing outdated frames, e.g. a MAX_DATA carrying a smaller limit than one already received. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver MUST accept packets containing an outdated frame, such as a MAX_DATA frame carrying a smaller maximum data value than one found in an older packet. | quic/conn_recv.go + quic/stream.go — non-increasing MAX_DATA/MAX_STREAM_DATA/MAX_STREAMS must be treated as no-ops, never as errors. |
| `13.3-13.4-33` | SHOULD | A sender should not retransmit information from packets that were acknowledged — including packets acked after being declared lost (spurious loss under reordering). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD avoid retransmitting information from packets once they are acknowledged. | quic/loss.go — spurious-loss handling: late ACK of a declared-lost packet should cancel its pending retransmission. |
| `13.3-13.4-34` | informative | State retained about declared-lost packets (to detect late ACKs) may be discarded after a reordering-safe period such as a PTO, or on events like a memory limit. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender can discard this information after a period of time elapses that adequately allows for reordering, such as a PTO | quic/loss.go + quic/pto.go — check how long lost-packet metadata is retained before being freed. |
| `13.3-13.4-35` | MUST | Upon detecting losses a sender must take appropriate congestion control action per the recovery spec. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Upon detecting losses, a sender MUST take appropriate congestion control action. | quic/loss.go → quic/cc.go / quic/bbr.go — every loss event must feed the congestion controller (NewReno/BBR). |
| `13.3-13.4-36` | informative | Endpoints using ECN react to reported congestion (ECN-CE marks) by reducing their sending rate per the recovery spec. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints react to reported congestion by reducing their sending rate in response | quic/cc.go / quic/bbr.go — if ECN implemented, ECN-CE count increases in ACKs must trigger a congestion response. |
| `13.3-13.4-37` | informative | Before enabling ECN, a sender first determines that the path supports ECN marking and that the peer reports received ECN values (via validation, 13.4.2). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | a sending QUIC endpoint first determines whether a path supports ECN marking and whether the peer reports the ECN values in received IP headers | quic/conn.go — check whether ECN is implemented at all; if ECT is ever set, a validation state machine is required first. |
| `13.3-13.4-38` | informative | An endpoint that does not implement ECN or cannot access received ECN fields simply does not report ECN counts. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | If an endpoint does not implement ECN support or does not have access to received ECN fields, it does not report ECN counts for packets it receives. | quic/gro (socket layer) — if cmsg/oob ECN bits are not read, sending ACKs without ECN counts (type 0x02) is conformant. |
| `13.3-13.4-39` | MUST | Even an endpoint that never sets ECT itself must report ECN markings it receives, if the platform makes them accessible. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | the endpoint MUST provide feedback about ECN markings it receives, if these are accessible. | quic/gro + quic/conn_recv.go — if the recv path reads ECN from cmsgs, those counts MUST flow into ack_tracker.go and be emitted in ACK-ECN frames. |
| `13.3-13.4-40` | informative | On receiving an IP packet marked ECT(0), ECT(1), or ECN-CE, an ECN-enabled endpoint increments the corresponding per-space count. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | an ECN-enabled endpoint accesses the ECN field and increases the corresponding ECT(0), ECT(1), or ECN-CE count. | quic/ack_tracker.go — three counters (ect0/ect1/ce) incremented from the IP header codepoint of each processed packet. |
| `13.3-13.4-41` | informative | Accumulated ECN counts are included in subsequent ACK frames (ACK type 0x03 with ECN section). <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | These ECN counts are included in subsequent ACK frames | quic/frame_write.go — ACK-ECN (0x03) emission if counts nonzero; quic/frame.go must at minimum parse peer 0x03 ACKs even without ECN support. |
| `13.3-13.4-42` | informative | ECN counts, like acknowledgment state, are maintained separately per packet number space. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Each packet number space maintains separate acknowledgment state and separate ECN counts. | quic/ack_tracker.go — ECN counters must be fields of the per-space tracker (Initial/Handshake/AppData), not connection-global. |
| `13.3-13.4-43` | informative | Coalesced QUIC packets share one IP header, so the ECN count is incremented once for EACH coalesced QUIC packet processed from that datagram. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | the ECN counts are incremented once for each coalesced QUIC packet. | quic/conn_recv.go — the coalescing loop must apply the datagram's single ECN codepoint to every packet's own space counter. |
| `13.3-13.4-44` | informative | ECN counts are only incremented for QUIC packets actually processed; duplicate packets are not processed and do not increase counts. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | duplicate QUIC packets are not processed and do not increase ECN counts | quic/ack_tracker.go + quic/conn_recv.go — duplicate-PN drop must occur before ECN count increment. |
| `13.3-13.4-45` | informative | An endpoint validates ECN counts per network path and disables ECN on that path when errors are detected. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint validates the ECN counts for each network path and disables the use of ECN on that path if errors are detected | quic/conn.go — if ECT is set on outgoing packets, a per-path (single path here, no migration) validation state is required. |
| `13.3-13.4-46` | informative | To validate a new path, the endpoint sets ECT(0) in the IP header of early outgoing packets on that path. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint sets an ECT(0) codepoint in the IP header of early outgoing packets sent on a new path to the peer | quic/packet_write.go / gso socket layer — check whether outgoing ECN bits can be set; if ECN unimplemented this is N/A. |
| `13.3-13.4-47` | informative | Validation fails if all packets sent with an ECT codepoint are eventually deemed lost. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint monitors whether all packets sent with an ECT codepoint are eventually deemed lost | quic/loss.go — ECN validation needs per-packet sent-codepoint tracking cross-referenced with loss outcomes. |
| `13.3-13.4-48` | informative | A cautious strategy: mark only the first ten outgoing packets on a path, or mark for three PTOs; if all marked packets are lost, disable marking. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | the endpoint could set an ECT codepoint for only the first ten outgoing packets on a path, or for a period of three PTOs | quic/conn.go — if ECN is added, the Appendix A.4-style testing-window (10 packets / 3 PTOs) is the recommended shape. |
| `13.3-13.4-49` | informative | ECN is attempted and validated for each new connection, on switching to a server preferred address, and on active migration (for this client: new connections only). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint thus attempts to use ECN and validates this for each new connection, when switching to a server's preferred address, and on active connection migration to a new path. | quic/conn.go — no migration/preferred-address in this client, so validation state resets only at connection establishment. |
| `13.3-13.4-50` | MAY | Implementations may use other RFC-defined ECN probing methods and marking strategies. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Implementations MAY use other methods defined in RFCs | quic/ — optional; only relevant if ECN support is implemented with a non-default strategy. |
| `13.3-13.4-51` | informative | Implementations that use the ECT(1) codepoint must perform ECN validation using the reported ECT(1) counts. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Implementations that use the ECT(1) codepoint need to perform ECN validation using the reported ECT(1) counts. | quic/ — only if ECT(1) (e.g. L4S) is ever used; default ECT(0)-only validation ignores this. |
| `13.3-13.4-52` | informative | An endpoint receiving an ACK with ECN counts validates them before use, by comparing newly received counts against those from the last successfully processed ACK frame. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | It performs this validation by comparing newly received counts against those from the last successfully processed ACK frame. | **VERIFY** quic/loss.go (ACK processing) — keep last-validated ECN counts per space; deltas computed against them, only committed on success. -- Quote is verbatim (RFC 9000 §13.4.2.1, line 4534-4535) and the level (informative — 'It performs this validation by...') is right, but applies_to is mislabelled. The endpoint performing this validation is the one that SENT the ECT-marked packets and is now processing an incoming ACK — i.e. the data sender, exactly the actor labelled 'sender' in the sibling facts 13.3-13.4-53 through -59 drawn from the same subsection (and the same reconcile target, quic/loss.go ACK processing). Labelling it 'receiver' collides with the catalog's own convention, where 'receiver' denotes the endpoint that receives data/IP packets and reports ECN counts (facts -38 through -44). Fix: applies_to should be ["sender"] (or "endpoint", matching the RFC's own wording 'An endpoint that receives an ACK frame with ECN counts therefore validates the counts before using them'). client_relevant: true is correct. \|\| Quote is verbatim (line 4534-4535) and the level (informative) is right, but applies_to is wrong. The endpoint that validates ECN counts in a received ACK is the endpoint that SENT the ECT-marked packets — the data sender — which is exactly how the sibling facts from the same subsection §13.4.2.1 are tagged (13.3-13.4-53 … -59 all use "sender"). Tagging -52 "receiver" (receiver of the ACK) is inconsistent and mislabels the role. Fix: applies_to = ["sender"]; client_relevant true and the reconcile note (last-validated ECN counts kept per space in the ACK-processing path) are correct. |
| `13.3-13.4-53` | informative | Validation fails if an ACK newly acknowledges a packet sent with ECT(0)/ECT(1) but the ACK carries no ECN counts (detects zeroing network or non-reporting peer). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | ECN validation fails if the corresponding ECN counts are not present in the ACK frame. | quic/loss.go — ACK type 0x02 (no ECN section) acking an ECT-marked packet must fail validation and disable ECN. |
| `13.3-13.4-54` | informative | Validation fails if the increase in ECT(0)+ECN-CE counts is less than the number of newly acknowledged packets originally sent with ECT(0). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | ECN validation also fails if the sum of the increase in ECT(0) and ECN-CE counts is less than the number of newly acknowledged packets | quic/loss.go — needs count of newly-acked ECT(0)-sent packets per ACK to compare against the ECT(0)+CE delta. |
| `13.3-13.4-55` | informative | Validation likewise fails if the increase in ECT(1)+ECN-CE counts is less than the number of newly acknowledged packets sent with ECT(1). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | ECN validation fails if the sum of the increases to ECT(1) and ECN-CE counts is less than the number of newly acknowledged packets sent with an ECT(1) marking. | quic/loss.go — symmetric ECT(1) check; only needed if ECT(1) marking is ever used. |
| `13.3-13.4-56` | informative | ECN counts are permitted to exceed the number of packets acknowledged by a given ACK, because earlier ACK frames may have been lost. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | This is why ECN counts are permitted to be larger than the total number of packets that are acknowledged. | quic/loss.go — validation must not treat count-delta greater than newly-acked as an error (only smaller fails). |
| `13.3-13.4-57` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not fail ECN validation because of an ACK frame that does not increase the largest acknowledged packet number (reordered ACKs). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT fail ECN validation as a result of processing an ACK frame that does not increase the largest acknowledged packet number. | quic/loss.go — gate ECN validation on largest-acked advancing; skip validation for reordered/stale ACKs. |
| `13.3-13.4-58` | informative | Validation fails if the received total ECT(0) or ECT(1) count exceeds the total number of packets the endpoint sent with that codepoint. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | ECN validation can fail if the received total count for either ECT(0) or ECT(1) exceeds the total number of packets sent with each corresponding ECT codepoint. | quic/loss.go — track total packets sent per codepoint; reported totals above that fail validation. |
| `13.3-13.4-59` | informative | In particular, a non-zero reported count for an ECT codepoint the endpoint never applied fails validation (detects network remarking to ECT). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | validation will fail when an endpoint receives a non-zero ECN count corresponding to an ECT codepoint that it never applied. | quic/loss.go — e.g. any nonzero ECT(1) count when only ECT(0) was ever set must fail validation. |
| `13.3-13.4-60` | MUST | If ECN validation fails, the endpoint must disable ECN: it stops setting the ECT codepoint in IP packets it sends. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | If validation fails, then the endpoint MUST disable ECN. | quic/conn.go + packet_write.go/socket layer — validation failure must clear the outgoing ECN marking flag immediately. |
| `13.3-13.4-61` | MAY | Even after a validation failure, an endpoint may revalidate ECN for the same path at any later time (e.g. periodic re-attempts). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Even if validation fails, an endpoint MAY revalidate ECN for the same path at any later time in the connection. | quic/conn.go — optional revalidation timer; fine to omit (fail-permanently is conformant). |
| `13.3-13.4-62` | MUST | After successful validation an endpoint may keep setting ECT on subsequent packets, but must disable ECN if validation later fails mid-connection (paths change). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint MUST disable ECN if validation later fails. | quic/loss.go + quic/conn.go — validation checks must run continuously on every ACK with counts, not only during the initial testing window. |

### 9000 section 14-16 - Datagram Size (Initial size, PMTU, DPLPMTUD, PMTU probes), Versions, and Variable-Length Integer Encoding

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `14-16-1` | MUST NOT | QUIC must not be used at all on a network path that cannot carry a maximum UDP datagram payload of at least 1200 bytes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | QUIC MUST NOT be used if the network path cannot support a maximum datagram size of at least 1200 bytes. | quic/send.go + quic/initial.go: verify the 1200-byte floor is a hard constant and nothing ever configures a smaller max datagram size. |
| `14-16-2` | informative | Datagram size means the total UDP payload (QUIC packet headers plus protected payloads), excluding UDP and IP headers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The datagram size includes one or more QUIC packet headers and protected payloads, but not the UDP or IP headers. | quic/send.go: confirm size accounting for datagram limits is UDP-payload bytes only, not including IP/UDP header overhead. |
| `14-16-3` | informative | QUIC assumes a 1280-byte minimum IP packet, which yields an expected maximum datagram size of 1232 bytes on IPv6 and 1252 bytes on IPv4. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | this results in a maximum datagram size of 1232 bytes for IPv6 and 1252 bytes for IPv4 | quic/send.go: if any per-family default above 1200 is used, it must not exceed 1232 (IPv6) / 1252 (IPv4) without PMTU discovery. |
| `14-16-4` | informative | A maximum datagram size above 1200 bytes may only be discovered via PMTUD or DPLPMTUD. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Any maximum datagram size larger than 1200 bytes can be discovered using Path Maximum Transmission Unit Discovery (PMTUD) | quic/send.go: if the client sends datagrams >1200, verify a PMTUD/DPLPMTUD mechanism justifies it; otherwise cap at 1200. |
| `14-16-5` | informative | The peer's max_udp_payload_size transport parameter is an additional cap on datagram size once known; before it is known, datagrams over 1200 bytes risk loss. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Enforcement of the max_udp_payload_size transport parameter (Section 18.2) might act as an additional limit on the maximum datagram size. | quic/transport_params.go + quic/send.go: verify the sender clamps outgoing datagram size to the peer's max_udp_payload_size once received. |
| `14-16-6` | MUST NOT | UDP datagrams carrying QUIC must never be fragmented at the IP layer. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | UDP datagrams MUST NOT be fragmented at the IP layer. | quic/gro-gso / UDP socket setup: verify datagram sizes never exceed the enforced max so the kernel is not asked to fragment; check socket options. |
| `14-16-7` | MUST | On IPv4 the Don't Fragment (DF) bit must be set when the platform allows it. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | the Don't Fragment (DF) bit MUST be set if possible, to prevent fragmentation on the path. | quic/gro-gso or socket setup code: check IP_MTU_DISCOVER/IP_DONTFRAG (or platform equivalent) is set on the IPv4 UDP socket. |
| `14-16-8` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not close the connection merely because a received datagram fails a size constraint (datagram size is unauthenticated). <br>_(receiver,endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint MUST NOT close a connection when it receives a datagram that does not meet size constraints | quic/close.go + receive path: verify no CONNECTION_CLOSE / error path is triggered by an undersized incoming datagram. |
| `14-16-9` | MAY | An endpoint may silently discard datagrams that do not meet size constraints. <br>_(receiver,endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the endpoint MAY discard such datagrams. | quic/ receive path: dropping undersized datagrams is permitted; confirm drop (not close) is the behavior if a size check exists. |
| `14-16-10` | MUST | A client must pad or coalesce every UDP datagram carrying an Initial packet up to at least 1200 bytes of UDP payload. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST expand the payload of all UDP datagrams carrying Initial packets to at least the smallest allowed maximum datagram size of 1200 bytes | quic/initial.go + quic/packet_write.go: verify EVERY datagram containing an Initial (including PTO retransmits and Initial ACKs) is padded/coalesced to >=1200, not just the first flight. |
| `14-16-11` | informative | The expansion of an Initial datagram may be achieved by coalescing, even with invalid packets that the receiver will simply discard. <br>_(client,sender [CLIENT])_ | Initial packets can even be coalesced with invalid packets, which a receiver will discard. | quic/packet_write.go: coalescing is a valid alternative to PADDING for reaching 1200; also receive path must tolerate/discard trailing garbage after valid coalesced packets. |
| `14-16-12` | MUST | A server must expand UDP datagrams carrying ack-eliciting Initial packets to at least 1200 bytes. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a server MUST expand the payload of all UDP datagrams carrying ack-eliciting Initial packets to at least the smallest allowed maximum datagram size of 1200 bytes. | Server-side rule; relevant only to the quic/ test peer if it sends ack-eliciting Initials — check test-peer send path, otherwise N/A. |
| `14-16-13` | MAY | Datagrams containing Initial packets may exceed 1200 bytes if the sender believes both path and peer support the larger size. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Datagrams containing Initial packets MAY exceed 1200 bytes if the sender believes that the network path and peer both support the size that it chooses. | quic/initial.go: exceeding 1200 in the first flight is optional and only safe with prior knowledge; default should stay at 1200. |
| `14-16-14` | MUST | A server must discard any Initial packet arriving in a UDP datagram whose payload is smaller than 1200 bytes. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST discard an Initial packet that is carried in a UDP datagram with a payload that is smaller than the smallest allowed maximum datagram size of 1200 bytes. | Server-only; matters indirectly — quic/initial.go must never emit an undersized Initial datagram or the server will drop it. Check test peer too. |
| `14-16-15` | MAY | A server may additionally close the connection with PROTOCOL_VIOLATION on receipt of an undersized Initial datagram. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MAY also immediately close the connection by sending a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame with an error code of PROTOCOL_VIOLATION | Server-only. quic/errors.go: PROTOCOL_VIOLATION code must exist; client should tolerate receiving such a close during handshake. |
| `14-16-16` | MUST | A server must limit the bytes it sends before validating the client address (anti-amplification). <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server MUST also limit the number of bytes it sends before validating the address of the client | Server-only (§8 anti-amplification); client-side implication is 14-16-10's padding requirement. N/A for quic/ client path. |
| `14-16-17` | SHOULD | An endpoint should use DPLPMTUD or PMTUD to learn whether the path supports a desired maximum datagram size without fragmentation. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint SHOULD use DPLPMTUD (Section 14.3) or PMTUD (Section 14.2.1) to determine whether the path to a destination will support a desired maximum datagram size without fragmentation. | quic/send.go: if no PMTU discovery is implemented, document the deviation (SHOULD) and ensure 14-16-18's cap applies instead. |
| `14-16-18` | SHOULD NOT | Without PMTUD/DPLPMTUD, an endpoint should not send datagrams larger than 1200 bytes (the smallest allowed maximum datagram size). <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | QUIC endpoints SHOULD NOT send datagrams larger than the smallest allowed maximum datagram size. | quic/send.go: with no PMTU discovery, verify the hard send cap is 1200 bytes per datagram (also constrains gro/gso GSO segment size). |
| `14-16-19` | SHOULD | All packets that are not PMTU probes should fit within the current maximum datagram size to avoid fragmentation or drop. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | All QUIC packets that are not sent in a PMTU probe SHOULD be sized to fit within the maximum datagram size | quic/send.go + quic/packet_write.go: verify packet assembly never exceeds the current max datagram size on the normal (non-probe) path. |
| `14-16-20` | MUST | If the PMTU for a path drops below 1200 bytes, the endpoint must immediately stop sending on that path except PMTU probes and CONNECTION_CLOSE packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | it MUST immediately cease sending QUIC packets, except for those in PMTU probes or those containing CONNECTION_CLOSE frames, on the affected path. | quic/send.go: check how persistent EMSGSIZE/ICMP-derived socket errors are handled — sending must stop (except close) if the path can't carry 1200. |
| `14-16-21` | MAY | An endpoint may terminate the connection when no alternative path supporting 1200 bytes can be found. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MAY terminate the connection if an alternative path cannot be found. | quic/close.go: with no migration support, terminating on a sub-1200 path is the natural client action; verify a clean close path exists. |
| `14-16-22` | SHOULD | Implementations doing any PMTU discovery should track a separate maximum datagram size per (local IP, remote IP) pair. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | SHOULD maintain a maximum datagram size for each combination of local and remote IP addresses. | quic/send.go: only applies if PMTU discovery exists; a fixed 1200 cap with no migration makes this vacuous — confirm and note. |
| `14-16-23` | MAY | An implementation may compute the maximum datagram size more conservatively to allow for tunnels or IP options/extensions. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A QUIC implementation MAY be more conservative in computing the maximum datagram size to allow for unknown tunnel overheads or IP header options/extensions. | quic/send.go: a conservative fixed cap (e.g. exactly 1200) is explicitly permitted; no action beyond confirming the constant. |
| `14-16-24` | MUST | An endpoint must ignore any ICMP message claiming the PMTU has dropped below 1200 bytes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST ignore an ICMP message that claims the PMTU has decreased below QUIC's smallest allowed maximum datagram size. | quic/send.go: Go surfaces ICMP as write errors on connected UDP sockets — verify such errors never shrink the datagram cap below 1200 or tear down the connection by themselves. |
| `14-16-25` | SHOULD | Endpoints using PMTUD should validate ICMP messages to guard against off-path packet injection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | QUIC endpoints using PMTUD SHOULD validate ICMP messages to protect from packet injection | Conditional on PMTUD support; quic/ has no raw-ICMP processing today — confirm none exists, else validation is required. |
| `14-16-26` | SHOULD | ICMP validation should use the quoted packet in the ICMP payload to associate the message with a specific transport connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | This validation SHOULD use the quoted packet supplied in the payload of an ICMP message to associate the message with a corresponding transport connection | Only if raw ICMP is ever consumed; quic/connid.go connection IDs would be the matching key. Confirm N/A currently. |
| `14-16-27` | MUST | ICMP validation must at minimum match IP addresses and UDP ports, and where possible connection IDs, against an active QUIC session. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | ICMP message validation MUST include matching IP addresses and UDP ports [RFC8085] and, when possible, connection IDs to an active QUIC session. | Conditional: binding only if the client processes ICMP for PMTUD. quic/: confirm no ICMP consumption, else implement the 4-tuple + CID match. |
| `14-16-28` | SHOULD | ICMP messages failing validation should be ignored entirely. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The endpoint SHOULD ignore all ICMP messages that fail validation. | Conditional on ICMP processing in quic/; connected-socket error handling in send.go should already treat unverifiable errors as advisory. |
| `14-16-29` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must never increase its PMTU estimate based on ICMP messages. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT increase the PMTU based on ICMP messages | quic/send.go: verify no code path raises the datagram size cap from a socket/ICMP error signal. |
| `14-16-30` | MAY | A PMTU reduction triggered by ICMP may be kept provisional until loss detection confirms the quoted packet was actually lost. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Any reduction in QUIC's maximum datagram size in response to ICMP messages MAY be provisional until QUIC's loss detection algorithm determines that the quoted packet has actually been lost. | Conditional on PMTUD; would couple send.go datagram cap with loss-recovery state. Confirm N/A if the client never reduces below/changes its cap. |
| `14-16-31` | informative | DPLPMTUD PMTU probes built from PADDING frames implement 'Probing using padding data' per RFC 8899 §4.1. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | PMTU probes for DPLPMTUD that use the PADDING frame implement "Probing using padding data", as defined in Section 4.1 of [DPLPMTUD]. | quic/frame_write.go: PADDING frame writer is the building block if DPLPMTUD probes are ever added; N/A while no probing exists. |
| `14-16-32` | SHOULD | Endpoints implementing DPLPMTUD should set the initial BASE_PLPMTU consistent with QUIC's 1200-byte smallest allowed maximum datagram size. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints SHOULD set the initial value of BASE_PLPMTU (Section 5.1 of [DPLPMTUD]) to be consistent with QUIC's smallest allowed maximum datagram size. | Only if DPLPMTUD is implemented in quic/send.go; the base/floor would be the same 1200 constant. |
| `14-16-33` | informative | For QUIC's DPLPMTUD profile, MIN_PLPMTU equals BASE_PLPMTU. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The MIN_PLPMTU is the same as the BASE_PLPMTU. | DPLPMTUD parameterization detail; N/A unless quic/ grows DPLPMTUD. |
| `14-16-34` | informative | Endpoints implementing DPLPMTUD maintain a Maximum Packet Size (MPS) per (local IP, remote IP) combination, corresponding to the maximum datagram size. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | QUIC endpoints implementing DPLPMTUD maintain a DPLPMTUD Maximum Packet Size (MPS) (Section 4.4 of [DPLPMTUD]) for each combination of local and remote IP addresses. | Same as 14-16-22 for the DPLPMTUD case; N/A without DPLPMTUD in quic/. |
| `14-16-35` | informative | As an acknowledged packetization layer, a QUIC sender can enter the DPLPMTUD BASE state once the handshake completes. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A QUIC sender can therefore enter the DPLPMTUD BASE state (Section 5.2 of [DPLPMTUD]) when the QUIC connection handshake has been completed. | DPLPMTUD state-machine entry point (handshake-complete gate) if probing is ever added to quic/send.go; N/A now. |
| `14-16-36` | informative | A QUIC sender does not run a DPLPMTUD CONFIRMATION_TIMER in the SEARCH_COMPLETE state, because QUIC is an acknowledged PL. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | a QUIC sender does not implement a DPLPMTUD CONFIRMATION_TIMER while in the SEARCH_COMPLETE state | DPLPMTUD profile detail; N/A unless quic/ implements DPLPMTUD. |
| `14-16-37` | informative | An endpoint using DPLPMTUD must validate any received ICMP PTB message (UDP ports plus PL info such as connection IDs in the quoted packet) before using its PTB value. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint using DPLPMTUD requires the validation of any received ICMP PTB message before using the PTB information | Conditional on DPLPMTUD+ICMP use; quic/connid.go CIDs would be the validation key. Confirm N/A currently. |
| `14-16-38` | informative | PMTU probes are ack-eliciting packets. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | PMTU probes are ack-eliciting packets. | quic/ack_tracker.go + loss recovery: if probes are added, they must be tracked as ack-eliciting; also affects PTO arming. |
| `14-16-39` | informative | Probe content can be limited to PING and PADDING frames, since oversized packets are likely dropped. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints could limit the content of PMTU probes to PING and PADDING frames | quic/frame_write.go: PING+PADDING composition is the recommended probe payload if probing is ever built. |
| `14-16-40` | SHOULD NOT | Loss of a packet carried in a PMTU probe should not trigger a congestion control reaction (though probes still consume congestion window). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | Loss of a QUIC packet that is carried in a PMTU probe is therefore not a reliable indication of congestion and SHOULD NOT trigger a congestion control reaction | quic/ congestion controller (NewReno/BBR): if probes are added, mark probe packets so their loss bypasses cwnd reduction; N/A without probing. |
| `14-16-41` | informative | A PMTU probe can be built by coalescing a (possibly invalid) long-header packet with a short-header packet so the Source Connection ID appears in any quoted ICMP payload. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | coalesce (see Section 12.2) a packet with a long header, such as a Handshake or 0-RTT packet (Section 17.2), with a short header packet in a single UDP datagram | quic/packet_write.go: probe-construction technique only; relevant if DPLPMTUD with ICMP routing is ever added. N/A now. |
| `14-16-42` | informative | QUIC versions are 32-bit unsigned numbers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | QUIC versions are identified using a 32-bit unsigned number. | quic/packet.go: Version field must be parsed/emitted as a fixed 32-bit big-endian value (not a varint). |
| `14-16-43` | informative | Version 0x00000000 is reserved to identify Version Negotiation packets. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The version 0x00000000 is reserved to represent version negotiation. | quic/packet.go: a long header with Version==0 must be classified as a Version Negotiation packet, never as v1; client must never send version 0. |
| `14-16-44` | informative | QUIC version 1 (this specification) is identified by the value 0x00000001. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This version of the specification is identified by the number 0x00000001. | quic/packet.go + quic/packet_write.go: verify the version constant is exactly 0x00000001 in all emitted long headers. |
| `14-16-45` | informative | QUIC version 0x00000001 uses TLS as its cryptographic handshake protocol. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Version 0x00000001 of QUIC uses TLS as a cryptographic handshake protocol | quic/ TLS 1.3 handshake integration (RFC 9001) — already the design; no per-file check beyond confirming no non-TLS handshake path. |
| `14-16-46` | informative | Version numbers whose most significant 16 bits are zero are reserved for future IETF consensus documents. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Versions with the most significant 16 bits of the version number cleared are reserved for use in future IETF consensus documents. | quic/packet.go: no action beyond not treating other 0x0000xxxx values as v1; unknown versions are simply not selectable. |
| `14-16-47` | informative | Version numbers matching the pattern 0x?a?a?a?a (low nibble of every byte = 0b1010) are reserved for forcing version negotiation and never represent a real protocol. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Versions that follow the pattern 0x?a?a?a?a are reserved for use in forcing version negotiation to be exercised | quic/packet.go version-negotiation handling: client must skip 0x?a?a?a?a greased entries when scanning a server's supported-version list. |
| `14-16-48` | MAY | A client or server may advertise support for any of the reserved (grease) version numbers. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | A client or server MAY advertise support for any of these reserved versions. | quic/packet.go: optional grease behavior; the client currently offers only v1 — permitted, no action required. |
| `14-16-49` | MAY | A client may deliberately dial with a reserved version number expecting the server to respond with Version Negotiation. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MAY use one of these version numbers with the expectation that the server will initiate version negotiation | quic/packet.go / retry.go: optional VN-exercise feature; not needed for a v1-only client, but VN packet parsing must exist to survive peers doing this. |
| `14-16-50` | MAY | A server may advertise a reserved version in its supported list and expect clients to ignore that value. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | a server MAY advertise support for one of these versions and can expect that clients ignore the value. | quic/packet.go: when parsing a Version Negotiation packet's version list, the client MUST-in-practice ignore reserved/unknown entries rather than error. |
| `14-16-51` | informative | In the varint encoding, the two most significant bits of the first byte encode log2 of the total encoded length in bytes. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | reserves the two most significant bits of the first byte to encode the base-2 logarithm of the integer encoding length in bytes | internal/bytesx varint codec: verify length is derived as 1<<(first_byte>>6) and the 2 prefix bits are masked off the value. |
| `14-16-52` | informative | The varint value occupies the remaining bits after the 2-bit prefix, in network (big-endian) byte order. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The integer value is encoded on the remaining bits, in network byte order. | internal/bytesx: verify big-endian assembly of the 6/14/30/62 value bits on both encode and decode. |
| `14-16-53` | informative | Varints are 1, 2, 4, or 8 bytes long, encoding 6-, 14-, 30-, or 62-bit values respectively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | integers are encoded on 1, 2, 4, or 8 bytes and can encode 6-, 14-, 30-, or 62-bit values, respectively | internal/bytesx: confirm exactly four encodings exist and the encoder picks a width wide enough for the value (max 2^62-1; larger values must be rejected/unencodable). |
| `14-16-54` | informative | Varint prefix-to-range table: 00→1 byte, 0–63; 01→2 bytes, 0–16383; 10→4 bytes, 0–1073741823; 11→8 bytes, 0–4611686018427387903. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | \| 11 \| 8 \| 62 \| 0-4611686018427387903 \| | internal/bytesx varint tests: assert all four boundary values (63/16383/1073741823/4611686018427387903) round-trip and 2^62 is rejected on encode. |
| `14-16-55` | informative | Varint values need not be minimally encoded — a receiver must accept non-minimal encodings — with the sole exception of the Frame Type field (§12.4). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Values do not need to be encoded on the minimum number of bytes necessary, with the sole exception of the Frame Type field | internal/bytesx decoder must accept non-minimal varints everywhere; quic/frame.go must additionally enforce minimal encoding ONLY for the frame-type varint (FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR per §12.4). |
