| `19.1-19.8-2` | informative | PADDING may be used to grow a packet, notably to bring an Initial packet up to the minimum required datagram size. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | Padding can be used to increase an Initial packet to the minimum required size | quic/initial.go + packet_write.go: client Initial datagrams padded to 1200 bytes via PADDING. |
| `19.1-19.8-3` | informative | A PING frame is type 0x01 with no content; endpoints use it to verify peer liveness/reachability. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Endpoints can use PING frames (type=0x01) to verify that their peers are still alive | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: type 0x01 has no fields; sending side in send.go for liveness probes. |
| `19.1-19.8-4` | informative | The receiver of a PING frame only needs to acknowledge the packet that carried it; no other response is required. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The receiver of a PING frame simply needs to acknowledge the packet containing this frame. | quic/ack_tracker.go: a received PING is ack-eliciting and needs no reply frame. |
| `19.1-19.8-5` | informative | PING can be used as a keepalive to stop the idle timeout from expiring when the application wants the connection kept open. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | The PING frame can be used to keep a connection alive when an application or application protocol wishes to prevent the connection from timing out | quic/send.go: keepalive PING scheduling versus idle timeout, if implemented. |
| `19.1-19.8-6` | MUST | Implementations must correctly parse and process both ACK frame types, 0x02 (no ECN) and 0x03 (with ECN counts). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | QUIC implementations MUST properly handle both types | quic/frame.go: decoder accepts type 0x03 and parses the trailing ECN Counts, not just 0x02. |
| `19.1-19.8-7` | SHOULD | An endpoint that has enabled ECN on packets it sends should feed the ACK frame's ECN counts into its congestion state. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | if they have enabled ECN for packets they send, they SHOULD use the information in the ECN section to manage their congestion state | quic/send.go congestion control: N/A if the client never marks ECT; still must parse counts (fact 6). |
| `19.1-19.8-8` | informative | Acknowledgments are irrevocable: once a packet is acknowledged it stays acknowledged even if absent from later ACK frames (no SACK-style reneging). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Once acknowledged, a packet remains acknowledged, even if it does not appear in a future ACK frame. | quic loss recovery (send.go / sent-packet tracking): never un-acknowledge a packet missing from a later ACK. |
| `19.1-19.8-9` | informative | An ACK frame acknowledges only packet numbers in the same packet number space as the packet carrying the ACK frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | having each ACK frame only acknowledge packet numbers in the same space as the packet in which the ACK frame is contained | quic/ack_tracker.go: one tracker per PN space; ACKs generated and applied per-space, never cross-space. |
| `19.1-19.8-10` | informative | Version Negotiation and Retry packets carry no packet number and cannot be ACKed; they are implicitly acknowledged by the client's next Initial packet. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | these packets are implicitly acknowledged by the next Initial packet sent by the client | quic/retry.go + packet.go: do not expect or generate ACKs for VN/Retry; proceed with new Initial. |
| `19.1-19.8-11` | informative | ACK frame wire layout: Type (0x02..0x03), Largest Acknowledged (i), ACK Delay (i), ACK Range Count (i), First ACK Range (i), repeated ACK Range, optional ECN Counts. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Largest Acknowledged (i), ACK Delay (i), ACK Range Count (i), First ACK Range (i), ACK Range (..) ..., [ECN Counts (..)], | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: field order and varint encoding of the ACK frame. |
| `19.1-19.8-12` | informative | Largest Acknowledged is a full variable-length integer packet number and, unlike header packet numbers, is never truncated. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Unlike the packet number in the QUIC long or short header, the value in an ACK frame is not truncated. | quic/frame.go: Largest Acknowledged decoded as plain varint, no PN-window reconstruction applied. |
| `19.1-19.8-13` | informative | ACK Delay is in microseconds and is decoded by multiplying the field value by 2^ack_delay_exponent advertised by the ACK's sender. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | It is decoded by multiplying the value in the field by 2 to the power of the ack_delay_exponent transport parameter sent by the sender of the ACK frame | quic/frame.go + transport_params.go: apply peer's ack_delay_exponent (default 3) when consuming ACK Delay for RTT. |
| `19.1-19.8-14` | informative | ACK Range Count gives the number of ACK Range fields that follow the First ACK Range. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A variable-length integer specifying the number of ACK Range fields in the frame. | quic/frame.go: loop exactly ACK Range Count times; bound the count against remaining bytes (no over-allocation). |
| `19.1-19.8-15` | informative | First ACK Range counts contiguous packets before Largest Acknowledged; smallest acked in the first range = Largest Acknowledged - First ACK Range. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the smallest packet acknowledged in the range is determined by subtracting the First ACK Range value from the Largest Acknowledged field | quic/frame.go + ack processing: first-range arithmetic in ACK decode/encode. |
| `19.1-19.8-16` | informative | Each ACK Range is a Gap then an ACK Range Length varint pair, in descending packet number order. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Each ACK Range consists of alternating Gap and ACK Range Length values in descending packet number order. | quic/frame.go + ack_tracker.go: ranges emitted/consumed high-to-low as (Gap, Length) pairs. |
| `19.1-19.8-17` | informative | A parser must tolerate repeated (overlapping/duplicate) ACK Ranges in one frame. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | ACK Ranges can be repeated. | **VERIFY** quic/frame.go: do not reject an ACK whose ranges repeat already-acknowledged packet numbers. -- Quote is verbatim (line 5970-5971) but the requirement misreads it. In context -- 'Each ACK Range consists of alternating Gap and ACK Range Length values in descending packet number order. ACK Ranges can be repeated. The number of Gap and ACK Range Length values is determined by the ACK Range Count field; one of each value is present for each value in the ACK Range Count field.' -- 'ACK Ranges can be repeated' means the ACK Range STRUCTURE (the Gap + ACK Range Length pair) may appear multiple times in one frame, matching the 'ACK Range (..) ...,' repetition notation in Figure 25. It does NOT say a parser must tolerate overlapping or duplicate ranges; §19.3.1's descending-order relative encoding plus the 'gap is one higher than the encoded value' rule makes overlapping/duplicate ranges unencodable by construction. Fix: restate as 'The ACK Range structure repeats; the number of repetitions equals the ACK Range Count field', level informative, applies_to endpoint. The reconcile note about not rejecting already-acknowledged packet numbers is not supported by this sentence (the relevant support is §13.2 / the irrevocability text at line 5905-5908). \|\| The quote "ACK Ranges can be repeated." (line 5970-5971) is verbatim, but the requirement misreads it. In context -- "Each ACK Range consists of alternating Gap and ACK Range Length values in descending packet number order. ACK Ranges can be repeated. The number of Gap and ACK Range Length values is determined by the ACK Range Count field" -- "repeated" refers to the ACK Range STRUCTURE appearing multiple times in one frame (the "ACK Range (..) ...," repeated-field notation in Figure 25), not to duplicate/overlapping packet-number ranges. Section 19.3.1 explicitly requires ranges to be in descending packet number order with each Gap and Length subtracted from the previous, so ranges within a single ACK frame cannot overlap or repeat packet numbers by construction. Nothing in 19.1-19.8 says a parser must tolerate overlapping/duplicate ranges, so the reconcile note ("do not reject an ACK whose ranges repeat already-acknowledged packet numbers") is not grounded in this text. Fix: restate as "An ACK frame may contain multiple ACK Range structures; the count of (Gap, ACK Range Length) pairs is given by ACK Range Count" (informative, endpoint) -- and drop the overlapping/duplicate claim, which is already covered by fact 19.1-19.8-16 (descending order) and 19.1-19.8-18 (one pair per count). \|\| The quote 'ACK Ranges can be repeated.' is verbatim (RFC 9000 §19.3.1), but the requirement misreads it. In context ('Each ACK Range consists of alternating Gap and ACK Range Length values in descending packet number order. ACK Ranges can be repeated. The number of Gap and ACK Range Length values is determined by the ACK Range Count field; one of each value is present for each value in the ACK Range Count field.') the sentence states that the ACK Range *structure* repeats within one frame — as shown by 'ACK Range (..) ...,' in Figure 25 — with the repetition count given by ACK Range Count. It does NOT say ranges may overlap or duplicate already-acknowledged packet numbers; the mandated 'descending packet number order' plus the gap-of-at-least-one arithmetic (largest = previous_smallest - gap - 2) in fact make ranges non-overlapping. Fix: restate the requirement as 'The ACK Range structure may appear multiple times in one ACK frame; a parser must accept ACK Range Count repetitions of the (Gap, ACK Range Length) pair.' Drop the '(overlapping/duplicate)' gloss and the reconcile note about not rejecting ACKs whose ranges repeat already-acknowledged packet numbers — that tolerance is not what this text says (RFC 9000 §19.3 'acknowledgments are irrevocable' covers re-acknowledgment across frames, not repeated ranges within one). \|\| The quote is verbatim (line 5970-5971) but the requirement misreads it. In context -- 'Each ACK Range consists of alternating Gap and ACK Range Length values in descending packet number order. ACK Ranges can be repeated. The number of Gap and ACK Range Length values is determined by the ACK Range Count field; one of each value is present for each value in the ACK Range Count field.' -- 'ACK Ranges can be repeated' means the ACK Range *structure* (a Gap + ACK Range Length pair) may appear multiple times in one frame, i.e. it is a repeating field, not that overlapping or duplicate acknowledged ranges must be tolerated. RFC 9000 nowhere states a parser must tolerate overlapping/duplicate ranges; the encoding is strictly descending and non-overlapping by construction (Sec 19.3.1: 'the values are subtracted, so that each ACK Range describes progressively lower-numbered packets'). Fix: restate the requirement as 'The ACK Range field is a repeating structure; ACK Range Count instances of (Gap, ACK Range Length) follow the First ACK Range.' -- which duplicates fact 19.1-19.8-18. The only mandated parse-error condition here is a negative computed packet number (fact 26, FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR). |
| `19.1-19.8-18` | informative | Exactly one Gap and one ACK Range Length value is present per unit of the ACK Range Count field. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The number of Gap and ACK Range Length values is determined by the ACK Range Count field | quic/frame.go: parse loop pairs Gap+Length per count entry; truncated frame is FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. |
| `19.1-19.8-19` | informative | Gap encodes the number of contiguous unacknowledged packets preceding the packet number one lower than the smallest of the preceding range. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A variable-length integer indicating the number of contiguous unacknowledged packets preceding the packet number one lower than the smallest in the preceding ACK Range. | quic/frame.go + ack_tracker.go: Gap semantics in both encode (ack_tracker) and decode paths. |
| `19.1-19.8-20` | informative | ACK Range Length encodes the number of contiguous acknowledged packets preceding that range's largest packet number (set by the preceding Gap). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A variable-length integer indicating the number of contiguous acknowledged packets preceding the largest packet number, as determined by the preceding Gap. | quic/frame.go: range-length decode; ack_tracker.go range-length encode. |
| `19.1-19.8-21` | informative | Within a range, smallest = largest - ack_range; an ACK Range value of 0 acknowledges only the largest packet number. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A value of 0 indicates that only the largest packet number is acknowledged. | quic/frame.go: apply smallest = largest - ack_range when expanding ranges. |
| `19.1-19.8-22` | informative | An ACK Range acknowledges every packet from its smallest to its largest packet number, inclusive at both ends. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An ACK Range acknowledges all packets between the smallest packet number and the largest, inclusive. | quic loss recovery: inclusive-bounds iteration when marking sent packets acked. |
| `19.1-19.8-23` | informative | The largest packet number of each successive ACK Range is derived by cumulatively subtracting all preceding ACK Range Lengths and Gaps. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The largest value for an ACK Range is determined by cumulatively subtracting the size of all preceding ACK Range Lengths and Gaps. | quic/frame.go: running-subtraction decode of successive ranges. |
| `19.1-19.8-24` | informative | The number of unacknowledged packets a Gap represents is the encoded Gap value plus one. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The number of packets in the gap is one higher than the encoded value of the Gap field. | quic/frame.go + ack_tracker.go: off-by-one on Gap (encoded = gap_size - 1). |
| `19.1-19.8-25` | informative | The next range's largest packet number is computed as largest = previous_smallest - gap - 2. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | largest = previous_smallest - gap - 2 | quic/frame.go + ack_tracker.go: exact -2 formula in both encode and decode of subsequent ranges. |
| `19.1-19.8-26` | MUST | If ACK range arithmetic yields any negative packet number, the endpoint must raise a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | If any computed packet number is negative, an endpoint MUST generate a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. | quic/frame.go + errors.go: underflow check during range expansion closes conn with FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR (0x07). |
| `19.1-19.8-27` | informative | ECN Counts are present if and only if the ACK frame type is 0x03 (least significant bit of the type set). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | ECN counts are only present when the ACK frame type is 0x03. | quic/frame.go: parse ECN block solely on type 0x03; never on 0x02. |
| `19.1-19.8-28` | informative | ECN Counts wire layout is three varints in order: ECT0 Count, ECT1 Count, ECN-CE Count. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | ECT0 Count (i), ECT1 Count (i), ECN-CE Count (i), | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: field order of the ECN Counts block. |
| `19.1-19.8-29` | informative | Each ECN count is the cumulative total of packets received with that codepoint within the ACK frame's packet number space. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the total number of packets received with the ECT(0) codepoint in the packet number space of the ACK frame | quic/ack_tracker.go: counts are running totals per space, not deltas. |
| `19.1-19.8-30` | informative | ECN counts are tracked independently per packet number space. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | ECN counts are maintained separately for each packet number space. | quic/ack_tracker.go: per-space ECN counters, no shared accumulator across Initial/Handshake/1-RTT. |
| `19.1-19.8-31` | informative | RESET_STREAM (type 0x04) abruptly terminates the sending part of a stream; after sending it the endpoint stops sending and retransmitting STREAM frames on that stream. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | After sending a RESET_STREAM, an endpoint ceases transmission and retransmission of STREAM frames on the identified stream. | quic/stream.go + send.go: after client sends RESET_STREAM, no further STREAM (re)transmission for that stream. |
| `19.1-19.8-32` | informative | A receiver of RESET_STREAM is permitted to discard any data it already received on that stream. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver of RESET_STREAM can discard any data that it already received on that stream. | quic/stream.go: receive buffer may be dropped on inbound RESET_STREAM. |
| `19.1-19.8-33` | MUST | Receiving RESET_STREAM for a stream that is send-only for this endpoint is a connection error STREAM_STATE_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that receives a RESET_STREAM frame for a send-only stream MUST terminate the connection with error STREAM_STATE_ERROR | quic/stream.go + errors.go: RESET_STREAM on a client-initiated unidirectional stream (client's send-only) must close conn with STREAM_STATE_ERROR (0x05). |
| `19.1-19.8-34` | informative | RESET_STREAM wire layout: Type 0x04 then varints Stream ID, Application Protocol Error Code, Final Size. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Type (i) = 0x04, Stream ID (i), Application Protocol Error Code (i), Final Size (i), | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: RESET_STREAM field order and varint encoding. |
| `19.1-19.8-35` | informative | Final Size in RESET_STREAM is the stream's final size in bytes as asserted by the RESET_STREAM sender. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A variable-length integer indicating the final size of the stream by the RESET_STREAM sender, in units of bytes | quic/stream.go + recvflow.go: use Final Size for flow-control accounting and FINAL_SIZE_ERROR consistency checks (§4.5). |
| `19.1-19.8-36` | informative | STOP_SENDING (type 0x05) tells the peer that incoming data is being discarded and requests it cease transmission; it can be sent in Recv or Size Known states. <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | A STOP_SENDING frame can be sent for streams in the "Recv" or "Size Known" states | quic/stream.go: client emits STOP_SENDING only while stream receive part is in Recv/Size Known. |
| `19.1-19.8-37` | MUST | Receiving STOP_SENDING for a locally initiated stream that has not yet been created is a connection error STREAM_STATE_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receiving a STOP_SENDING frame for a locally initiated stream that has not yet been created MUST be treated as a connection error of type STREAM_STATE_ERROR. | quic/stream.go: STOP_SENDING naming a client-initiated stream ID above the highest opened must close conn with STREAM_STATE_ERROR. |
| `19.1-19.8-38` | MUST | Receiving STOP_SENDING for a stream that is receive-only for this endpoint is a connection error STREAM_STATE_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that receives a STOP_SENDING frame for a receive-only stream MUST terminate the connection with error STREAM_STATE_ERROR. | quic/stream.go: STOP_SENDING on a server-initiated unidirectional stream (client's receive-only) must close conn with STREAM_STATE_ERROR. |
| `19.1-19.8-39` | informative | STOP_SENDING wire layout: Type 0x05 then varints Stream ID and Application Protocol Error Code. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Type (i) = 0x05, Stream ID (i), Application Protocol Error Code (i), | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: STOP_SENDING field order and varint encoding. |
| `19.1-19.8-40` | informative | CRYPTO (type 0x06) carries handshake messages and may appear in every packet type except 0-RTT. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | It can be sent in all packet types except 0-RTT. | quic/frame.go: frame-type-per-packet-type validation must allow CRYPTO in Initial/Handshake/1-RTT (0-RTT unused by this client). |
| `19.1-19.8-41` | informative | CRYPTO frames are STREAM-like but carry no stream ID, are exempt from flow control, and have no optional offset/length or end-of-stream markers. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | they do not bear a stream identifier; they are not flow controlled; and they do not carry markers for optional offset, optional length, and the end of the stream | quic/frame.go: CRYPTO always has explicit Offset and Length; recvflow.go must not charge CRYPTO to connection flow control. |
| `19.1-19.8-42` | informative | CRYPTO wire layout: Type 0x06 then varints Offset and Length, then Crypto Data of Length bytes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Type (i) = 0x06, Offset (i), Length (i), Crypto Data (..), | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: CRYPTO field order; Length must not exceed remaining packet bytes. |
| `19.1-19.8-43` | informative | Each encryption level has its own independent CRYPTO byte stream, and each level's stream starts at offset 0. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | There is a separate flow of cryptographic handshake data in each encryption level, each of which starts at an offset of 0. | quic handshake reassembly (initial.go / crypto stream buffer): per-level offset tracking reset to 0, never shared across levels. |
| `19.1-19.8-44` | MUST | A CRYPTO frame whose offset plus data length exceeds 2^62-1 must be treated as a connection error of FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR or CRYPTO_BUFFER_EXCEEDED. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receipt of a frame that exceeds this limit MUST be treated as a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR or CRYPTO_BUFFER_EXCEEDED | quic/frame.go + errors.go: check offset+length <= 2^62-1 on CRYPTO parse; overflow-safe addition. |
| `19.1-19.8-45` | informative | The CRYPTO stream has no explicit end and CRYPTO frames have no FIN bit. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The stream does not have an explicit end, so CRYPTO frames do not have a FIN bit. | quic/frame.go: no FIN handling on CRYPTO; handshake completion signaled by TLS, not stream end. |
| `19.1-19.8-46` | informative | NEW_TOKEN (type 0x07) is sent by a server to give the client a token for the Initial packet header of a future connection. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | A server sends a NEW_TOKEN frame (type=0x07) to provide the client with a token to send in the header of an Initial packet for a future connection. | quic/frame.go: client must parse NEW_TOKEN; token storage for future connections is optional (may discard). |
| `19.1-19.8-47` | informative | NEW_TOKEN wire layout: Type 0x07 then Token Length varint and Token opaque bytes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Token Length (i), Token (..), | quic/frame.go: Token Length bounded against remaining packet bytes; token treated as opaque blob. |
| `19.1-19.8-48` | MUST NOT | A server must never send a NEW_TOKEN frame with an empty token. <br>_(server,sender [not-client])_ | The token MUST NOT be empty. | Test peer only: if the test server in quic/ tests ever emits NEW_TOKEN, token must be non-empty. |
| `19.1-19.8-49` | MUST | A client must treat a NEW_TOKEN frame with an empty Token field as a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST treat receipt of a NEW_TOKEN frame with an empty Token field as a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. | quic/frame.go + errors.go: Token Length == 0 on parse closes conn with FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR (0x07). |
| `19.1-19.8-50` | informative | Clients are responsible for discarding duplicate token values received in NEW_TOKEN frames, since duplicates could link connection attempts. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients are responsible for discarding duplicate values, which might be used to link connection attempts | quic client token store (if any): dedupe stored tokens; trivially satisfied if tokens are not stored. |
| `19.1-19.8-51` | MUST NOT | A client must never send a NEW_TOKEN frame. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients MUST NOT send NEW_TOKEN frames. | quic/frame_write.go + send.go: ensure no code path emits frame type 0x07 from the client. |
| `19.1-19.8-52` | MUST | A server must treat receipt of a NEW_TOKEN frame as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST treat receipt of a NEW_TOKEN frame as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | Test peer only: quic test server should reject NEW_TOKEN with PROTOCOL_VIOLATION (0x0a) if it validates frames. |
| `19.1-19.8-53` | informative | STREAM frame types occupy 0b00001XXX (0x08..0x0f); STREAM frames implicitly create the stream they reference. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The Type field in the STREAM frame takes the form 0b00001XXX (or the set of values from 0x08 to 0x0f). | quic/frame.go: type-range dispatch for 0x08-0x0f; stream.go: inbound STREAM implicitly opens peer-initiated streams. |
| `19.1-19.8-54` | informative | OFF bit (0x04): set means an Offset field is present; clear means no Offset field and the data starts at stream offset 0. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | When set to 1, the Offset field is present. When set to 0, the Offset field is absent and the Stream Data starts at an offset of 0 | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: OFF-bit conditional Offset field on both parse and emit. |
| `19.1-19.8-55` | informative | LEN bit (0x02): clear means no Length field and Stream Data extends to the end of the packet; set means a Length field is present. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | If this bit is set to 0, the Length field is absent and the Stream Data field extends to the end of the packet. | quic/frame.go: LEN=0 STREAM frame must consume all remaining payload bytes (must be the last frame parsed). |
| `19.1-19.8-56` | informative | FIN bit (0x01) marks the frame as the end of the stream. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The FIN bit (0x01) indicates that the frame marks the end of the stream. | quic/frame.go + stream.go: FIN bit surfaces end-of-stream to receive state machine. |
| `19.1-19.8-57` | informative | When FIN is set, the stream's final size is the frame's offset plus its data length. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The final size of the stream is the sum of the offset and the length of this frame. | quic/stream.go: final size fixed at offset+length of the FIN frame; later inconsistency is FINAL_SIZE_ERROR (§4.5). |
| `19.1-19.8-58` | MUST | Receiving a STREAM frame for a locally initiated stream not yet created, or for a send-only stream, must terminate the connection with STREAM_STATE_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | MUST terminate the connection with error STREAM_STATE_ERROR if it receives a STREAM frame for a locally initiated stream that has not yet been created, or for a send-only stream | quic/stream.go + errors.go: inbound STREAM naming an unopened client-initiated ID or a client send-only (client-initiated uni) stream closes conn with STREAM_STATE_ERROR. |
| `19.1-19.8-59` | informative | STREAM wire layout: Type 0x08..0x0f, Stream ID varint, optional Offset varint, optional Length varint, then Stream Data. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Type (i) = 0x08..0x0f, Stream ID (i), [Offset (i)], [Length (i)], Stream Data (..), | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: field order and bit-conditional presence of Offset/Length. |
| `19.1-19.8-60` | informative | A STREAM frame with zero-length Stream Data carries as its offset the offset of the next byte that would be sent (e.g., a pure FIN). <br>_(endpoint,sender [CLIENT])_ | When a Stream Data field has a length of 0, the offset in the STREAM frame is the offset of the next byte that would be sent. | quic/frame_write.go + stream.go: empty-data FIN frame carries current send offset; receiver accepts zero-length frames. |
| `19.1-19.8-61` | informative | The first byte of every stream is at offset 0. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The first byte in the stream has an offset of 0. | quic/stream.go: receive reassembly and send offsets start at 0. |
| `19.1-19.8-62` | MUST | A STREAM frame whose offset plus data length exceeds 2^62-1 must be treated as a connection error of FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR or FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receipt of a frame that exceeds this limit MUST be treated as a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR or FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR | quic/frame.go + errors.go: overflow-safe offset+length <= 2^62-1 check on STREAM parse. |

### 9000 section 19.9-19.21 - Frame Types and Formats: MAX_DATA, MAX_STREAM_DATA, MAX_STREAMS, DATA_BLOCKED, STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED, STREAMS_BLOCKED, NEW_CONNECTION_ID, RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID, PATH_CHALLENGE, PATH_RESPONSE, CONNECTION_CLOSE, HANDSHAKE_DONE, Extension Frames

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `19.9-19.21-1` | informative | MAX_DATA frame (type 0x10) consists of the type followed by a single varint Maximum Data field giving the connection-wide send limit in bytes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | MAX_DATA Frame { Type (i) = 0x10, Maximum Data (i), } | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: MAX_DATA parse/emit is type byte + one varint, units bytes. |
| `19.9-19.21-2` | informative | All data sent in STREAM frames counts toward the connection-level MAX_DATA limit. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | All data sent in STREAM frames counts toward this limit. | quic/send.go + recvflow.go: verify connection flow-control accounting counts STREAM payload bytes only (not CRYPTO or other frames). |
| `19.9-19.21-3` | MUST NOT | The sum of final sizes across all streams, including terminal-state streams, must never exceed the connection data limit the receiver advertised. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The sum of the final sizes on all streams -- including streams in terminal states -- MUST NOT exceed the value advertised by a receiver. | quic/send.go: sender must gate on peer MAX_DATA including bytes attributed to reset/finished streams (RESET_STREAM final size counts). |
| `19.9-19.21-4` | MUST | An endpoint must close the connection with FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR if it receives more connection-level data than the MAX_DATA value it advertised. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST terminate a connection with an error of type FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR if it receives more data than the maximum data value that it has sent. | quic/recvflow.go: on inbound STREAM data (and RESET_STREAM final size) exceeding advertised connection window, raise FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR connection close. |
| `19.9-19.21-5` | informative | MAX_STREAM_DATA frame (type 0x11) carries a varint Stream ID and a varint Maximum Stream Data limit in bytes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | MAX_STREAM_DATA Frame { Type (i) = 0x11, Stream ID (i), Maximum Stream Data (i), } | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: MAX_STREAM_DATA layout = type, stream ID varint, limit varint. |
| `19.9-19.21-6` | informative | MAX_STREAM_DATA frames are sent for streams in the Recv state. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A MAX_STREAM_DATA frame can be sent for streams in the "Recv" state | quic/stream.go: client emits MAX_STREAM_DATA only for streams where it is receiving (not after FIN consumed/reset). |
| `19.9-19.21-7` | MUST | Receiving MAX_STREAM_DATA for a locally initiated stream the endpoint has not yet created is a STREAM_STATE_ERROR connection error. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receiving a MAX_STREAM_DATA frame for a locally initiated stream that has not yet been created MUST be treated as a connection error of type STREAM_STATE_ERROR. | quic/stream.go: check MAX_STREAM_DATA for a client-initiated stream ID above the highest opened → STREAM_STATE_ERROR. |
| `19.9-19.21-8` | MUST | Receiving MAX_STREAM_DATA for a receive-only stream (from the frame receiver's perspective) is a STREAM_STATE_ERROR connection error. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that receives a MAX_STREAM_DATA frame for a receive-only stream MUST terminate the connection with error STREAM_STATE_ERROR. | quic/stream.go: MAX_STREAM_DATA on a server-initiated unidirectional stream (client receive-only) must produce STREAM_STATE_ERROR. |
| `19.9-19.21-9` | informative | Stream flow-control accounting uses the largest received offset on the stream, which can exceed total bytes received due to loss/reordering. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint accounts for the largest received offset of data that is sent or received on the stream. | quic/recvflow.go + stream.go: flow-control consumption must be measured by highest offset seen, not by bytes buffered/delivered. |
| `19.9-19.21-10` | MUST NOT | A sender must never send stream data beyond the largest Maximum Stream Data value the receiver advertised for that stream. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The data sent on a stream MUST NOT exceed the largest maximum stream data value advertised by the receiver. | quic/send.go + stream.go: outbound STREAM chunking must respect per-stream send window from peer MAX_STREAM_DATA / initial transport params. |
| `19.9-19.21-11` | MUST | An endpoint must close the connection with FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR if it receives stream data beyond the largest per-stream limit it advertised. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | MUST terminate a connection with an error of type FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR if it receives more data than the largest maximum stream data that it has sent | quic/recvflow.go: inbound STREAM offset+length past the stream window advertised → FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR connection close. |
| `19.9-19.21-12` | informative | MAX_STREAMS uses two type codepoints: 0x12 for bidirectional streams and 0x13 for unidirectional streams, each carrying one varint cumulative stream count. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A MAX_STREAMS frame with a type of 0x12 applies to bidirectional streams, and a MAX_STREAMS frame with a type of 0x13 applies to unidirectional streams. | quic/frame.go: MAX_STREAMS codec must key bidi vs uni off type 0x12/0x13; single Maximum Streams varint payload. |
| `19.9-19.21-13` | MUST | A MAX_STREAMS value permitting streams beyond the 2^60 cumulative-count cap must be treated as a FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR connection error. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receipt of a frame that permits opening of a stream larger than this limit MUST be treated as a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. | quic/recvflow.go or frame.go: validate received Maximum Streams <= 2^60, else FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. |
| `19.9-19.21-14` | MUST | A MAX_STREAMS frame that does not increase the current stream limit must be ignored (loss/reordering can deliver stale limits). <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | MAX_STREAMS frames that do not increase the stream limit MUST be ignored. | quic/recvflow.go: apply MAX_STREAMS only if value > current limit; never regress. |
| `19.9-19.21-15` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not open more streams than the peer's current cumulative stream limit allows. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT open more streams than permitted by the current stream limit set by its peer. | quic/stream.go + send.go: client stream-open path must block/queue until peer MAX_STREAMS (or initial_max_streams_* transport param) permits. |
| `19.9-19.21-16` | MUST | If a peer opens more streams than permitted, the endpoint must close the connection with STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST terminate a connection with an error of type STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR if a peer opens more streams than was permitted. | quic/recvflow.go + stream.go: server-initiated stream IDs above the limit the client advertised → STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR. |
| `19.9-19.21-17` | informative | The MAX_STREAMS limit is cumulative over the connection lifetime, counting closed streams as well as open ones — not a concurrency limit. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The limit includes streams that have been closed as well as those that are open. | quic/recvflow.go: stream-count accounting is cumulative-opened, never decremented on close. |
| `19.9-19.21-18` | SHOULD | A sender blocked by connection-level flow control should send a DATA_BLOCKED frame. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD send a DATA_BLOCKED frame (type=0x14) when it wishes to send data but is unable to do so due to connection-level flow control | quic/send.go + frame_write.go: verify client emits DATA_BLOCKED when the connection send window is exhausted. |
| `19.9-19.21-19` | informative | DATA_BLOCKED frame (type 0x14) carries one varint: the connection-level limit at which blocking occurred. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the connection-level limit at which blocking occurred | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: DATA_BLOCKED payload is a single varint equal to the current connection limit, not bytes wanted. |
| `19.9-19.21-20` | SHOULD | A sender blocked by stream-level flow control should send a STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD send a STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame (type=0x15) when it wishes to send data but is unable to do so due to stream-level flow control. | quic/send.go + stream.go: verify client emits STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED when a per-stream send window is exhausted. |
| `19.9-19.21-21` | MUST | Receiving STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED for a send-only stream (from the receiver's perspective) is a STREAM_STATE_ERROR connection error. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that receives a STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame for a send-only stream MUST terminate the connection with error STREAM_STATE_ERROR. | quic/recvflow.go or stream.go: STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED referencing a client-initiated unidirectional stream (client send-only) → STREAM_STATE_ERROR. |
| `19.9-19.21-22` | informative | STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED frame (type 0x15) carries a varint Stream ID and a varint offset at which blocking occurred. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED Frame { Type (i) = 0x15, Stream ID (i), Maximum Stream Data (i), } | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: STREAM_DATA_BLOCKED layout = type, stream ID varint, blocked-offset varint. |
| `19.9-19.21-23` | SHOULD | A sender blocked by the peer's stream limit should send a STREAMS_BLOCKED frame (0x16 bidirectional, 0x17 unidirectional). <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD send a STREAMS_BLOCKED frame (type=0x16 or 0x17) when it wishes to open a stream but is unable to do so due to the maximum stream limit | quic/send.go + recvflow.go: verify client emits STREAMS_BLOCKED (correct bidi/uni type) when stream-open is gated on peer MAX_STREAMS. |
| `19.9-19.21-24` | informative | STREAMS_BLOCKED (type 0x16..0x17) carries one varint Maximum Streams and does not itself open a stream — it only reports the limit hit. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A STREAMS_BLOCKED frame does not open the stream, but informs the peer that a new stream was needed and the stream limit prevented the creation of the stream. | quic/frame.go: STREAMS_BLOCKED payload = one varint (limit at send time); processing must not create stream state. |
| `19.9-19.21-25` | MUST | A STREAMS_BLOCKED Maximum Streams value exceeding 2^60 must be treated as a connection error of type STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR or FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receipt of a frame that encodes a larger stream ID MUST be treated as a connection error of type STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR or FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. | quic/frame.go or recvflow.go: validate inbound STREAMS_BLOCKED value <= 2^60. |
| `19.9-19.21-26` | informative | NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame (type 0x18): varint Sequence Number, varint Retire Prior To, 8-bit Length, Connection ID of 1..20 bytes (8..160 bits), and a 128-bit Stateless Reset Token. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | NEW_CONNECTION_ID Frame { Type (i) = 0x18, Sequence Number (i), Retire Prior To (i), Length (8), Connection ID (8..160), Stateless Reset Token (128), } | quic/frame.go + connid.go: NEW_CONNECTION_ID layout — two varints, 1-byte length, CID bytes, fixed 16-byte reset token. |
| `19.9-19.21-27` | MUST | A NEW_CONNECTION_ID Length field less than 1 or greater than 20 is invalid and must be treated as a FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR connection error. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Values less than 1 and greater than 20 are invalid and MUST be treated as a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. | quic/frame.go: validate 1 <= Length <= 20 on parse → FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR otherwise. |
| `19.9-19.21-28` | MUST NOT | An endpoint must not send NEW_CONNECTION_ID while it requires the peer to send packets with a zero-length Destination Connection ID. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint MUST NOT send this frame if it currently requires that its peer send packets with a zero-length Destination Connection ID. | quic/connid.go: if client uses a zero-length source CID it must never emit NEW_CONNECTION_ID. |
| `19.9-19.21-29` | MUST | An endpoint sending packets with a zero-length Destination Connection ID must treat receipt of NEW_CONNECTION_ID as PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that is sending packets with a zero-length Destination Connection ID MUST treat receipt of a NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/connid.go: if peer's CID is zero-length (client sends zero-length DCID), inbound NEW_CONNECTION_ID → PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. |
| `19.9-19.21-30` | MUST NOT | Receiving the same NEW_CONNECTION_ID frame multiple times (retransmission) must not be treated as a connection error; the sequence number deduplicates. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receipt of the same frame multiple times MUST NOT be treated as a connection error. | quic/connid.go: duplicate NEW_CONNECTION_ID with identical fields must be idempotent, not an error. |
| `19.9-19.21-31` | MAY | An endpoint may treat as PROTOCOL_VIOLATION a NEW_CONNECTION_ID that repeats a CID with a different token or sequence number, or reuses a sequence number for a different CID. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | or if a sequence number is used for different connection IDs, the endpoint MAY treat that receipt as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/connid.go: optional consistency check — same CID must keep same (seq, token); same seq must keep same CID. |
| `19.9-19.21-32` | MUST | When sending NEW_CONNECTION_ID, the Retire Prior To value must be less than or equal to the Sequence Number. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The value in the Retire Prior To field MUST be less than or equal to the value in the Sequence Number field. | quic/frame_write.go + connid.go: if client ever emits NEW_CONNECTION_ID, enforce RetirePriorTo <= SequenceNumber. |
| `19.9-19.21-33` | MUST | Receiving a NEW_CONNECTION_ID with Retire Prior To greater than Sequence Number must be treated as FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receiving a value in the Retire Prior To field that is greater than that in the Sequence Number field MUST be treated as a connection error of type FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. | quic/connid.go or frame.go: validate RetirePriorTo <= SequenceNumber on parse → FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR. |
| `19.9-19.21-34` | MUST | A receiver must ignore Retire Prior To values that do not increase the largest Retire Prior To value received so far. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | A receiver MUST ignore any Retire Prior To fields that do not increase the largest received Retire Prior To value. | quic/connid.go: track high-water Retire Prior To; smaller/equal values are no-ops. |
| `19.9-19.21-35` | MUST | On receiving a NEW_CONNECTION_ID whose sequence number is below a previously received Retire Prior To, the endpoint must send a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID retiring that CID (unless already sent). <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | MUST send a corresponding RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame that retires the newly received connection ID, unless it has already done so for that sequence number. | quic/connid.go: auto-retire late-arriving CIDs with seq < high-water Retire Prior To; dedupe per sequence number. |
| `19.9-19.21-36` | informative | RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame (type 0x19) carries a single varint: the sequence number of the connection ID being retired. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID Frame { Type (i) = 0x19, Sequence Number (i), } | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID layout = type + one varint. |
| `19.9-19.21-37` | informative | Sending RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID also acts as a request for the peer to issue more connection IDs, and retiring a CID invalidates its stateless reset token. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Retiring a connection ID invalidates the stateless reset token associated with that connection ID. | quic/connid.go: on retire, drop the CID's reset token from any stateless-reset matching set. |
| `19.9-19.21-38` | MUST | Receiving a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID whose sequence number is greater than any connection ID sequence number the endpoint ever issued must be treated as PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | Receipt of a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame containing a sequence number greater than any previously sent to the peer MUST be treated as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/connid.go: validate inbound RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID seq against the highest sequence the client has issued (0 if only the handshake CID). |
| `19.9-19.21-39` | MUST NOT | A RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame must not name the connection ID used as the Destination Connection ID of the packet carrying the frame. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The sequence number specified in a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame MUST NOT refer to the Destination Connection ID field of the packet in which the frame is contained. | quic/connid.go + send.go: when client retires a server CID, ensure the carrying packet's DCID is not the CID being retired. |
| `19.9-19.21-40` | MAY | The peer may treat a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID that refers to the carrying packet's own DCID as a PROTOCOL_VIOLATION connection error. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The peer MAY treat this as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/connid.go: optional check that a received RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID does not retire the DCID of its own packet. |
| `19.9-19.21-41` | informative | An endpoint that was given a zero-length connection ID by its peer cannot send RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID at all. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint cannot send this frame if it was provided with a zero-length connection ID by its peer. | quic/connid.go: never emit RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID when the server supplied a zero-length CID. |
| `19.9-19.21-42` | MUST | An endpoint that provides a zero-length connection ID must treat receipt of RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID as a PROTOCOL_VIOLATION connection error. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint that provides a zero-length connection ID MUST treat receipt of a RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frame as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | quic/connid.go: if client's own source CID is zero-length, inbound RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID → PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. |
| `19.9-19.21-43` | informative | PATH_CHALLENGE frame (type 0x1a) carries a fixed 8-byte (64-bit) field of arbitrary data. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | PATH_CHALLENGE Frame { Type (i) = 0x1a, Data (64), } | quic/frame.go: PATH_CHALLENGE payload is exactly 8 opaque bytes, no varint. |
| `19.9-19.21-44` | MUST | The recipient of a PATH_CHALLENGE must generate a PATH_RESPONSE containing the identical Data value. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | The recipient of this frame MUST generate a PATH_RESPONSE frame (Section 19.18) containing the same Data value. | quic/frame.go + send.go: even without migration support, client must echo any server PATH_CHALLENGE as PATH_RESPONSE with the same 8 bytes — verify the conn read loop does this. |
| `19.9-19.21-45` | informative | PATH_RESPONSE frame (type 0x1b) has a format identical to PATH_CHALLENGE: a fixed 8-byte Data field. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The format of a PATH_RESPONSE frame is identical to that of the PATH_CHALLENGE frame | quic/frame.go + frame_write.go: PATH_RESPONSE codec mirrors PATH_CHALLENGE (type 0x1b + 8 bytes). |
| `19.9-19.21-46` | MAY | An endpoint may raise PROTOCOL_VIOLATION if a received PATH_RESPONSE does not match any PATH_CHALLENGE it previously sent. <br>_(receiver [CLIENT])_ | does not match the content of a PATH_CHALLENGE frame previously sent by the endpoint, the endpoint MAY generate a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION | quic/frame.go handling: client never sends PATH_CHALLENGE (no migration), so any inbound PATH_RESPONSE is unmatched — decide (and document) whether to ignore or error; MAY permits ignoring. |
| `19.9-19.21-47` | informative | CONNECTION_CLOSE frame (types 0x1c and 0x1d): varint Error Code, optional varint Frame Type (0x1c only), varint Reason Phrase Length, then the reason phrase bytes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | CONNECTION_CLOSE Frame { Type (i) = 0x1c..0x1d, Error Code (i), [Frame Type (i)], Reason Phrase Length (i), Reason Phrase (..), } | quic/frame.go + close.go: CONNECTION_CLOSE parse/emit for both variants; the frame cannot be split between packets, so reason phrase is bounded by packet size. |
| `19.9-19.21-48` | informative | Type 0x1c signals QUIC-layer errors (or NO_ERROR) using transport error codes from Section 20.1; type 0x1d signals application errors using application-defined codes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | A CONNECTION_CLOSE frame of type 0x1c uses codes from the space defined in Section 20.1. A CONNECTION_CLOSE frame of type 0x1d uses codes defined by the application protocol | quic/close.go + errors.go: 0x1c ↔ transport error codes, 0x1d ↔ application (H3) error codes; keep the two code spaces distinct. |
| `19.9-19.21-49` | informative | In the Frame Type field of a 0x1c CONNECTION_CLOSE, the value 0 (PADDING) is used when the triggering frame type is unknown. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | A value of 0 (equivalent to the mention of the PADDING frame) is used when the frame type is unknown. | quic/close.go + frame_write.go: emit Frame Type 0 when the offending frame type is unknown/not applicable. |
| `19.9-19.21-50` | informative | The application variant (0x1d) omits the Frame Type field entirely — the parser must not read it for 0x1d. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | The application-specific variant of CONNECTION_CLOSE (type 0x1d) does not include this field. | quic/frame.go: CONNECTION_CLOSE parse path must branch on type — Frame Type varint present only for 0x1c. |
| `19.9-19.21-51` | SHOULD | The Reason Phrase should be a UTF-8 encoded string; it can be zero length. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | This SHOULD be a UTF-8 encoded string | quic/close.go + frame_write.go: reason phrases the client emits should be UTF-8; accept zero-length on parse. |
| `19.9-19.21-52` | informative | The application variant (0x1d) can only be sent in 0-RTT or 1-RTT packets, never in Initial or Handshake packets. <br>_(sender [CLIENT])_ | The application-specific variant of CONNECTION_CLOSE (type 0x1d) can only be sent using 0-RTT or 1-RTT packets | quic/close.go + send.go: never place a 0x1d CONNECTION_CLOSE in an Initial/Handshake packet. |
| `19.9-19.21-53` | informative | To abandon a connection during the handshake for an application reason, send a 0x1c CONNECTION_CLOSE with error code APPLICATION_ERROR in an Initial or Handshake packet. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint can send a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame (type 0x1c) with an error code of APPLICATION_ERROR in an Initial or Handshake packet | quic/close.go: pre-handshake-complete app-initiated close must be translated to 0x1c + APPLICATION_ERROR. |
| `19.9-19.21-54` | informative | Open streams not explicitly closed are implicitly closed when the connection closes. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | If there are open streams that have not been explicitly closed, they are implicitly closed when the connection is closed. | quic/close.go + stream.go: connection close must tear down all remaining stream state and unblock waiters. |
| `19.9-19.21-55` | informative | HANDSHAKE_DONE frame (type 0x1e) has no content — just the type varint. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | HANDSHAKE_DONE Frame { Type (i) = 0x1e, } | quic/frame.go: HANDSHAKE_DONE parse consumes zero payload bytes. |
| `19.9-19.21-56` | informative | HANDSHAKE_DONE is server-to-client only, signalling handshake confirmation to the client; a client must never send it. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | A HANDSHAKE_DONE frame can only be sent by the server. | quic/initial.go / handshake path: client never emits HANDSHAKE_DONE; receipt marks the handshake confirmed (drop Handshake keys per RFC 9001). |
| `19.9-19.21-57` | MUST NOT | Servers must not send HANDSHAKE_DONE before completing the handshake. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers MUST NOT send a HANDSHAKE_DONE frame before completing the handshake. | Server-only; relevant only to the quic/ test peer if it plays the server role. |
| `19.9-19.21-58` | MUST | A server must treat receipt of a HANDSHAKE_DONE frame as a PROTOCOL_VIOLATION connection error. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST treat receipt of a HANDSHAKE_DONE frame as a connection error of type PROTOCOL_VIOLATION. | Server-only receipt rule; for the client the corollary is fact 56 (never send it). Check quic/ test-peer server behavior if applicable. |
| `19.9-19.21-59` | MUST | An extension using a new frame type must first ensure the peer can understand it (e.g., negotiated via a transport parameter); frames are not self-describing. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An extension to QUIC that wishes to use a new type of frame MUST first ensure that a peer is able to understand the frame. | quic/frame.go + transport_params.go: client implements no extension frames; parser must reject unknown frame types (FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR per Section 12.4) rather than skip them. |
| `19.9-19.21-60` | SHOULD | Extensions that modify or replace core protocol functionality should define their interaction with previously defined extensions modifying the same components. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Such extensions SHOULD define their interaction with previously defined extensions modifying the same protocol components. | Spec-author guidance; no code impact for quic/ unless extensions are added. |
| `19.9-19.21-61` | MUST | Extension frames must be congestion controlled and must elicit an ACK, except extension frames that replace or supplement the ACK frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Extension frames MUST be congestion controlled and MUST cause an ACK frame to be sent. | quic/ack_tracker.go + send.go: if any extension frame is ever added, it must count as ack-eliciting and congestion-controlled by default. |
| `19.9-19.21-62` | informative | Extension frames are not subject to flow control unless the defining extension says otherwise. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Extension frames are not included in flow control unless specified in the extension. | quic/recvflow.go: flow-control accounting covers STREAM data only; extension frames (none today) would default to exempt. |

### 9000 section 20 - Error Codes

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `20-1` | informative | QUIC transport error codes and application error codes are 62-bit unsigned integers (i.e., variable-length-integer range). <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | QUIC transport error codes and application error codes are 62-bit unsigned integers. | quic/errors.go + internal/bytesx: error-code type must hold the full 62-bit range and be encoded/decoded as a varint, not truncated to uint32. |
| `20-2` | informative | Transport error codes are the codes usable in a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame of type 0x1c, and they apply to the entire connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | QUIC transport error codes that can be used in a CONNECTION_CLOSE frame with a type of 0x1c. These errors apply to the entire connection. | quic/close.go: CONNECTION_CLOSE type 0x1c must carry transport error codes and be treated as connection-fatal, distinct from type 0x1d. |
| `20-3` | informative | NO_ERROR (0x00) is used with CONNECTION_CLOSE to signal abrupt connection closure in the absence of any error. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | NO_ERROR (0x00): An endpoint uses this with CONNECTION_CLOSE to signal that the connection is being closed abruptly in the absence of any error. | quic/errors.go + close.go: define NO_ERROR=0x00 and use it when the client closes a healthy connection (e.g., pool eviction/shutdown). |
| `20-4` | informative | INTERNAL_ERROR (0x01) signals the endpoint hit an internal error and cannot continue the connection. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | INTERNAL_ERROR (0x01): The endpoint encountered an internal error and cannot continue with the connection. | quic/errors.go: define INTERNAL_ERROR=0x01; emit it for unrecoverable local failures rather than a more specific code. |
| `20-5` | informative | CONNECTION_REFUSED (0x02) signals the server refused to accept a new connection. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | CONNECTION_REFUSED (0x02): The server refused to accept a new connection. | quic/errors.go: define CONNECTION_REFUSED=0x02; client never sends it but must decode/report it when received in CONNECTION_CLOSE during connect. |
| `20-6` | informative | FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR (0x03) signals the peer sent more data than the endpoint permitted in its advertised data limits. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR (0x03): An endpoint received more data than it permitted in its advertised data limits; see Section 4. | quic/errors.go + recvflow.go: define FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR=0x03 and raise it when received STREAM/CRYPTO data exceeds advertised MAX_DATA/MAX_STREAM_DATA. |
| `20-7` | informative | STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR (0x04) signals receipt of a frame for a stream ID beyond the advertised stream limit for that stream type. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR (0x04): An endpoint received a frame for a stream identifier that exceeded its advertised stream limit for the corresponding stream type. | quic/errors.go + recvflow.go/stream.go: define STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR=0x04; raise it when a peer-opened stream ID exceeds the client's advertised MAX_STREAMS limit. |
| `20-8` | informative | STREAM_STATE_ERROR (0x05) signals receipt of a frame for a stream that was not in a state permitting that frame. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | STREAM_STATE_ERROR (0x05): An endpoint received a frame for a stream that was not in a state that permitted that frame; see Section 3. | quic/errors.go + stream.go: define STREAM_STATE_ERROR=0x05; raise it on frames illegal for the stream's current send/receive state (e.g., STREAM on a send-only stream). |
| `20-9` | informative | FINAL_SIZE_ERROR (0x06) signals: STREAM data exceeding the established final size, a STREAM/RESET_STREAM final size lower than data already received, or a final size differing from the one already established. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | an endpoint received a STREAM frame or a RESET_STREAM frame containing a different final size to the one already established. | quic/errors.go + stream.go: define FINAL_SIZE_ERROR=0x06 and check all three conditions when processing STREAM(FIN) and RESET_STREAM final sizes. |
| `20-10` | informative | FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR (0x07) signals a badly formatted frame, e.g., an unknown frame type or an ACK frame declaring more ranges than the packet remainder can carry. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An endpoint received a frame that was badly formatted -- for instance, a frame of an unknown type or an ACK frame that has more acknowledgment ranges than the remainder | quic/errors.go + frame.go: define FRAME_ENCODING_ERROR=0x07; frame parser must return it for unknown types, truncated fields, and over-declared ACK range counts (ack_tracker.go). |
| `20-11` | informative | TRANSPORT_PARAMETER_ERROR (0x08) signals transport parameters that were malformed, had an invalid value, omitted a mandatory parameter, included a forbidden parameter, or were otherwise in error. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | received transport parameters that were badly formatted, included an invalid value, omitted a mandatory transport parameter, included a forbidden transport parameter, or were otherwise in error. | quic/errors.go + transport_params.go: define TRANSPORT_PARAMETER_ERROR=0x08; validation of server transport params (bad value, missing mandatory, server sending client-forbidden params) must yield this code. |
| `20-12` | informative | CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR (0x09) signals the peer provided more connection IDs than the advertised active_connection_id_limit. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR (0x09): The number of connection IDs provided by the peer exceeds the advertised active_connection_id_limit. | quic/errors.go + connid.go: define CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR=0x09; enforce it when NEW_CONNECTION_ID frames push active CIDs past the client's advertised active_connection_id_limit. |
| `20-13` | informative | PROTOCOL_VIOLATION (0x0a) signals a protocol-compliance error not covered by a more specific error code. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | PROTOCOL_VIOLATION (0x0a): An endpoint detected an error with protocol compliance that was not covered by more specific error codes. | quic/errors.go: define PROTOCOL_VIOLATION=0x0a as the fallback for generic peer misbehavior (e.g., frames in wrong packet types). |
| `20-14` | informative | INVALID_TOKEN (0x0b) signals a server received a client Initial containing an invalid Token field. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | INVALID_TOKEN (0x0b): A server received a client Initial that contained an invalid Token field. | quic/errors.go: define INVALID_TOKEN=0x0b; client never generates it but must decode/report it if the server closes with it after a Retry token (retry.go). |
| `20-15` | informative | APPLICATION_ERROR (0x0c) signals the application or application protocol caused the connection to be closed. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | APPLICATION_ERROR (0x0c): The application or application protocol caused the connection to be closed. | quic/errors.go + close.go: define APPLICATION_ERROR=0x0c; used notably when an app-level close must be re-signaled in Initial/Handshake packets where type 0x1d is disallowed. |
| `20-16` | informative | CRYPTO_BUFFER_EXCEEDED (0x0d) signals the endpoint received more CRYPTO-frame data than it can buffer. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | CRYPTO_BUFFER_EXCEEDED (0x0d): An endpoint has received more data in CRYPTO frames than it can buffer. | quic/errors.go: define CRYPTO_BUFFER_EXCEEDED=0x0d; the CRYPTO reassembly buffer must be bounded and overflow must raise this code, not OOM. |
| `20-17` | informative | KEY_UPDATE_ERROR (0x0e) signals errors detected in performing key updates per QUIC-TLS Section 6. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | KEY_UPDATE_ERROR (0x0e): An endpoint detected errors in performing key updates; see Section 6 of [QUIC-TLS]. | quic/errors.go: define KEY_UPDATE_ERROR=0x0e; key-update violations (e.g., peer updates before prior update acknowledged) must use this code. |
| `20-18` | informative | AEAD_LIMIT_REACHED (0x0f) signals the endpoint reached the confidentiality or integrity limit of the connection's AEAD algorithm. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | AEAD_LIMIT_REACHED (0x0f): An endpoint has reached the confidentiality or integrity limit for the AEAD algorithm used by the given connection. | quic/errors.go: define AEAD_LIMIT_REACHED=0x0f; packet-protection layer must track encryption/failed-decryption counts and close with this code at the AEAD limits. |
| `20-19` | informative | NO_VIABLE_PATH (0x10) signals the endpoint determined the network path cannot support QUIC; it is unlikely to be received except when the path lacks a large enough MTU. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | NO_VIABLE_PATH (0x10): An endpoint has determined that the network path is incapable of supporting QUIC. | quic/errors.go + initial.go: define NO_VIABLE_PATH=0x10; use it if PMTU/datagram-size constraints show the path cannot carry the required minimum datagram size. |
| `20-20` | informative | CRYPTO_ERROR occupies the 256-value range 0x0100-0x01ff, carrying handshake-specific error codes; with TLS these are the TLS alert codes per QUIC-TLS Section 4.8. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | CRYPTO_ERROR (0x0100-0x01ff): The cryptographic handshake failed. A range of 256 values is reserved for carrying error codes specific to the cryptographic handshake | quic/errors.go: represent CRYPTO_ERROR as the range 0x0100+alert (not a single constant); TLS alerts from the handshake map to 0x0100+alert in CONNECTION_CLOSE, and received codes in this range decode back to the alert. |
| `20-21` | informative | Management of application error codes is left to application protocols; they are used in RESET_STREAM, STOP_SENDING, and CONNECTION_CLOSE frames of type 0x1d. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Application protocol error codes are used for the RESET_STREAM frame (Section 19.4), the STOP_SENDING frame (Section 19.5), and the CONNECTION_CLOSE frame with a type of 0x1d | quic/stream.go + close.go: RESET_STREAM/STOP_SENDING error-code fields and CONNECTION_CLOSE type 0x1d must carry opaque application codes (H3 codes from http3/), never transport codes; transport codes stay on type 0x1c. |

### 9000 section 21.1 - Overview of Security Properties (handshake anti-amplification, server-side DoS, on-path termination, parameter negotiation, protected packets, migration attacks)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `21.1-1` | informative | Prior to address validation, an endpoint is limited to sending no more than three times the data it has received from an unvalidated address. <br>_(endpoint,server [not-client])_ | Endpoints cannot send data toward an unvalidated address in excess of three times the data received from that address. | send.go: client never responds to unvalidated addresses so no amplification gate is needed on the client send path; only the in-repo test peer (server role) would need the 3x cap — confirm the client path has no such throttle and the test server doesn't send unbounded pre-validation data. |
| `21.1-2` | informative | The anti-amplification limit binds an endpoint only when it is responding to packets that arrived from an address it has not validated. <br>_(endpoint,server [not-client])_ | The anti-amplification limit only applies when an endpoint responds to packets received from an unvalidated address. | send.go: scoping clarifier for 21.1-1 — verify no code applies amplification accounting outside the respond-to-unvalidated-address case (client role never hits it). |
| `21.1-3` | informative | The anti-amplification limit does not constrain a client when it establishes a new connection or initiates connection migration. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The anti-amplification limit does not apply to clients when establishing a new connection or when initiating connection migration. | send.go + initial.go: confirm the client's Initial/Handshake flight is never throttled by any amplification counter — the client is explicitly exempt when opening a connection. |
| `21.1-4` | informative | The Retry packet is a cheap token-exchange mechanism letting a server validate the client's IP address before doing expensive crypto, at the cost of one round trip. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | the Retry packet provides a cheap token exchange mechanism that allows servers to validate a client's IP address prior to doing any expensive computations | retry.go: client must fully support the Retry flow this depends on — accept a Retry, verify its integrity tag, and retransmit the Initial carrying the server-supplied token. |
| `21.1-5` | informative | After a successful handshake a server can issue new tokens to the client, which allow future connection establishment without the Retry round trip. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | servers can issue new tokens to a client, which will allow new connection establishment without incurring this cost | frame.go: NEW_TOKEN frame must parse cleanly and never be treated as an error even if the client discards the token; storing/reusing tokens in later Initials is optional — check current NEW_TOKEN handling. |
| `21.1-6` | informative | Once valid Initial packets have been exchanged, all subsequent Handshake packets are protected with the Handshake keys, so an on-path attacker can no longer forge handshake content. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Once valid Initial packets have been exchanged, subsequent Handshake packets are protected with the Handshake keys | quic packet-protection/handshake code (packet.go + key schedule): verify Handshake-space packets are only ever sealed/opened with Handshake-level secrets, never Initial secrets. |
| `21.1-7` | informative | Initial packets are encrypted with per-version (publicly derivable) keys, while Handshake and later packets are encrypted with keys derived from the TLS key exchange. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | the Initial packets being encrypted with per-version keys and the Handshake and later packets being encrypted with keys derived from the TLS key exchange | packet.go/packet_write.go + TLS integration: Initial secrets must come from the QUIC v1 version-specific salt; 1-RTT/Handshake keys must come only from the TLS 1.3 schedule. |
| `21.1-8` | informative | Transport parameter negotiation is folded into the TLS transcript, giving it the same integrity guarantees as ordinary TLS negotiation. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | parameter negotiation is folded into the TLS transcript and thus provides the same integrity guarantees as ordinary TLS negotiation | transport_params.go: parameters must be carried only inside the quic_transport_parameters TLS extension (part of the transcript), never in any unauthenticated side channel. |
| `21.1-9` | informative | An attacker knowing the version-specific salt can read the client's transport parameters (sent under Initial keys) but cannot read the server's parameters or influence negotiation. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | An attacker can observe the client's transport parameters (as long as it knows the version-specific salt) but cannot observe the server's transport parameters | transport_params.go: client transport parameters are effectively public — ensure nothing secret (tokens with private meaning, identifiers) is ever encoded into the client's parameter set. |
| `21.1-10` | informative | Connection IDs are carried unencrypted in packet headers but are integrity protected in all packets. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Connection IDs are unencrypted but integrity protected in all packets. | packet.go: the header including CIDs must be fed to the AEAD as associated data (integrity), and header protection must not be expected to hide CIDs — verify AAD construction covers the full header. |
| `21.1-11` | informative | QUIC version 1 has no in-protocol version negotiation mechanism; endpoints implementing incompatible versions simply fail to establish a connection. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This version of QUIC does not incorporate a version negotiation mechanism; implementations of incompatible versions will simply fail to establish a connection. | packet.go (Version Negotiation handling): on receiving VN or an unsupported version the client should fail the connection attempt — there must be no downgrade/renegotiation logic. |
| `21.1-12` | informative | Packet protection applies authenticated encryption to every packet type except Version Negotiation packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | applies authenticated encryption to all packets except Version Negotiation packets | packet.go: Version Negotiation packets are completely unauthenticated — verify the client applies the separate §6 validation rules (echoed CIDs, ignore after other packets) rather than trusting VN content. |
| `21.1-13` | informative | Initial and Retry packets have only limited protection because their keying material is version-specific and publicly derivable. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | though Initial and Retry packets have limited protection due to the use of version-specific keying material | initial.go + retry.go: treat Initial-keyed and Retry content as spoofable by any observer — Retry integrity tag must still be verified, but neither may be used as proof of the genuine server (only the completed TLS handshake is). |
| `21.1-14` | informative | An active attacker that observes packets cannot produce packets the receiver deems valid, with the sole exception of Initial packets, which any observer can forge. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | should not be able to generate packets deemed valid by the receiving endpoint, other than Initial packets | initial.go: because Initial packets are forgeable by observers, the client must not take irreversible actions (e.g., permanent connection abort on arbitrary content) from Initial-keyed data beyond what the spec mandates pre-handshake. |
| `21.1-15` | informative | Only endpoints that completed the handshake can process packet payloads, and packets that fail authentication are ignored (dropped) by endpoints rather than acted upon. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | the packet payloads can only be processed by the endpoints that completed the handshake, and invalid packets are ignored by those endpoints | quic receive path (packet.go decrypt loop): a packet failing AEAD open must be silently discarded — it must never terminate the connection, generate an error frame, or advance any protocol state. |
| `21.1-16` | informative | Except for datagrams containing Initial packets (which require padding), how packets are arranged into UDP datagrams has no functional effect, so a receiver must tolerate arbitrary coalescing or splitting by the network. <br>_(endpoint,receiver [CLIENT])_ | Aside from datagrams containing Initial packets, which require padding, modification of how packets are arranged in datagrams has no functional effect on a connection | packet.go coalesced-packet parsing + gro/gso batching: the client must accept any valid arrangement of packets across datagrams (split or merged along packet boundaries); initial.go keeps the 1200-byte Initial-datagram padding rule intact. |
| `21.1-17` | informative | Connection migration lets endpoints move between addresses/ports over multiple paths, but only one path at a time is used to send and receive non-probing frames. <br>_(endpoint [not-client])_ | using one path at a time for transmission and receipt of non-probing frames | **VERIFY** This client deliberately does not implement migration (documented non-goal); no code change — if migration is ever added, non-probing frames must be confined to the single active path. -- Quote and level are correct (verbatim at RFC 9000 line 7012-7013, §21.1.3, no RFC 2119 keyword -> informative). The role classification is wrong: applies_to is ["endpoint"] with client_relevant=false, but connection migration in QUIC v1 is a client-only capability -- RFC 9000 §5.1 states "Only clients are able to migrate in this version of QUIC." The sole actor the requirement describes is therefore the client, and the fact's own reconcile note is entirely about client behaviour ("This client deliberately does not implement migration"). Fix: applies_to should be ["client"] (or ["endpoint","client"]) and client_relevant should be true; the "not implemented, documented non-goal" status belongs in the reconcile note as a scoping decision, not encoded as client_relevant=false. (Contrast 21.1-1/21.1-2, where client_relevant=false IS correct: §8.1 puts the anti-amplification MUST on servers -- "Prior to validating the client address, servers MUST NOT send more than three times as many bytes as the number of bytes they have received" -- and the client validates the server address via its first Initial Destination Connection ID.) |
| `21.1-18` | informative | Path validation establishes that a peer is both willing and able to receive packets on a particular path, limiting how much data can be directed at a spoofed address. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Path validation (Section 8.2) establishes that a peer is both willing and able to receive packets sent on a particular path. | frame.go: even a non-migrating client must correctly parse PATH_CHALLENGE and answer with PATH_RESPONSE echoing the 8-byte data (the responder side of path validation is unconditional per §8.2) — verify both frame codecs and the auto-response exist. |
| `21.1-19` | informative | Any modification to an authenticated portion of a packet causes the receiving endpoint to drop the packet as invalid, since payloads are both authenticated and encrypted. <br>_(receiver,endpoint [CLIENT])_ | any modifications to an authenticated portion of a packet will cause it to be dropped by the receiving endpoint as invalid | packet.go receive/decrypt path: tampered packets (AEAD failure) must be dropped, not partially processed — confirm no header fields from a packet are acted on before its AEAD check succeeds. |
| `21.1-20` | informative | Once the handshake completes, an off-path attacker must not be able to cause the connection to close — endpoints therefore act on connection-terminating signals only from authenticated packets. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | An off-path attacker cannot cause a connection to close once the handshake has completed. | close.go + packet.go: CONNECTION_CLOSE must only be honored when carried in a successfully decrypted packet, and stateless-reset detection must require an exact token match — verify no unauthenticated datagram can tear down an established connection. |

### 9000 section 21.2-21.14 - Security Considerations: handshake DoS, amplification, optimistic ACK, request forgery, Slowloris, stream fragmentation/commitment, peer DoS, ECN, stateless reset oracle, version downgrade, routing, traffic analysis

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC 9000 verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| `21.2-21.14-1` | informative | Apart from Initial packets and Stateless Resets, an endpoint accepts only packets whose Destination Connection ID matches a value that endpoint previously chose. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Except for Initial and Stateless Resets, an endpoint only accepts packets that include a Destination Connection ID field that matches a value the endpoint previously chose. | quic/packet.go receive path + quic/connid.go: verify non-Initial packets whose DCID does not match a client-chosen connection ID are silently dropped. |
| `21.2-21.14-2` | informative | The Source and Destination Connection ID fields in received packets are required to match the values set by the peer, as the primary off-path protection during the handshake. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | These are required to match those set by a peer. | quic/packet.go + quic/retry.go: verify the server's Source Connection ID on Handshake/Retry/subsequent packets is checked against the expected value, mismatches dropped. |
| `21.2-21.14-3` | informative | Destination Connection ID matching is the only protection offered for Version Negotiation packets, so a client must apply it before acting on one. <br>_(client,endpoint [CLIENT])_ | This is the only protection offered for Version Negotiation packets. | quic/packet.go Version Negotiation handling: verify a VN packet is ignored unless its DCID/SCID mirror the connection IDs the client sent in its Initial. |
| `21.2-21.14-4` | informative | The client selects the Destination Connection ID of its Initial packet to be unpredictable, because Initial keys are derived from it and this denies servers control over Initial ciphertext. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The Destination Connection ID field in an Initial packet is selected by a client to be unpredictable, which serves an additional purpose. | quic/connid.go or quic/initial.go: verify the original Destination Connection ID is generated from crypto/rand (unpredictable, per Section 7.2 length rules). |
| `21.2-21.14-5` | informative | Packets that cannot be authenticated (AEAD failure) are discarded rather than acted upon. <br>_(endpoint [CLIENT])_ | Packets that cannot be authenticated are discarded. | quic/packet.go decrypt path: verify AEAD/header-protection failure causes a silent per-packet drop with no connection error and no state change. |
